# EDITORIAL PREFACE

-> "Finally, brothers, whatever things are true, whatever things are honorable, whatever things are just, whatever things are pure, whatever things are lovely, whatever things are of good report; if there is any virtue, and if there is any praise, think on these things." <note>This quote is from the New Testament, Philippians 4:8.</note> <-

No group within the population of India can afford to ignore its ancient heritage. In India's literature, philosophy, art, and structured social life <note>original: "regulated life"; this refers to the traditional social, ethical, and religious systems that organized Indian society, such as Dharma.</note>, there is much that is worthless and much that is clearly unhealthy; yet the treasures of knowledge, wisdom, and beauty they contain are too precious to be lost. Every citizen of India needs to engage with these treasures if they are to be a truly cultured modern Indian. This is as true for Christians, Muslims, and Zoroastrians <term>Zoroastrian: A follower of one of the world's oldest monotheistic religions, practiced in India primarily by the Parsi community.</term> as it is for Hindus. 

However, while India's heritage has been extensively researched by scholars, and the results of their hard work are documented in books, these findings are not truly accessible to the average person. In most cases, these volumes are expensive, technical, and difficult to understand. Therefore, this series of affordable books has been planned by a group of Christian men so that every educated Indian—whether rich or poor—may be able to find their way into the treasures of India’s past. Many Europeans, both in India and elsewhere, will undoubtedly also find this series useful.

The General Editors are taking the greatest care in selecting writers and approving manuscripts for publication. Two strict standards are applied to every book: everything must be scholarly, and everything must be sympathetic. The purpose is to bring the best out of the ancient treasuries so that it may be known, enjoyed, and used.

<summary>This preface introduces "The Heritage of India Series," explaining its goal to provide affordable, scholarly, and accessible books on Indian culture and history for all citizens. It emphasizes that India's ancient wisdom is a vital resource for modern life, regardless of one's religious background.</summary>

<keywords>India, heritage, scholarship, cultural history, accessibility, education, religion, Hindu, Christian, Muslim, Zoroastrian</keywords>

## AUTHORS' NOTE

This book has gone through the printing process under unusually difficult circumstances. During most of the production time, one of the authors has been in Mesopotamia <note>modern-day Iraq</note> and the other has been in India. Partly because of this distance, and partly due to practical difficulties with printing, there are a few minor inconsistencies in the application of the system of <term>transliteration</term> <note>transliteration: the process of representing the characters of one alphabet (Tamil) in the characters of another (English/Latin script)</note>.

The authors would have wished, had it been possible, to alter the note on stanza 17 (page 27), which is treated there as a case of <term>intercession</term> <note>intercession: the act of praying or petitioning on behalf of others</note> for others. A more thorough comparison of this hymn with similar ones in Tamil religious poetry has convinced them that the "she" in the hymn is actually the devotee himself, who compares himself to a love-sick woman, as is seen in stanza 19.

They would also like to acknowledge their gratitude to Dr. Farquhar, General Editor of the series, for the significant hard work he contributed to this book. They also thank Mrs. Phillips, Mr. G. S. Duraiswamy, B.A., and the Reverend F. Goodwill for the valuable help they provided with proofreading.

The Wesleyan Mission Press in Mysore <note>a city in the state of Karnataka, India</note>, which does not usually print the Tamil language, has been kind enough to undertake this unfamiliar task for the sake of this book, carrying it through with tireless patience.

<summary>The authors explain that the book's production was complicated by their geographic separation in India and Mesopotamia and offer a correction regarding the symbolic gender of the devotee in the hymns.</summary>

<keywords>Authors' Note, Mesopotamia, India, transliteration, Tamil poetry, intercession, symbolism, Dr. Farquhar, Wesleyan Mission Press, Mysore, proofreading</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the introduction to the collection, explaining the cultural and religious importance of Tamil Śaivite hymns. # INTRODUCTION

## (A)—The Hymns and their Significance

The sound of chanting and song, accompanied by unfamiliar instruments, drifts over the high walls of the temple in the cool air of the evening or the dawn. It leaves Westerners passing by wondering about the nature of these songs. If travelers had access to a Hindu hymnbook, they might feel they could understand the spirit of Hinduism just as well as a non-Christian might understand Christianity through Christian hymns. 

In the Tamil country <note>the region in southern India where the Tamil language is spoken</note>, at least, such a hymnbook *does exist*. Our current goal is to provide enough examples from it so that readers can understand what these hymns are like. People in Britain are more eager to understand India than ever before, recognizing the significant debt they owe to the country. Likewise, people in India are increasingly eager to learn about their own country's remarkable history, the wonders of which are inextricably linked to its religion. 

This makes these hymns worth examining now. They are sung in temples and homes across the Tamil region, where Tamil is the native language of over eighteen million people. For devout Śaivites <note>Śaivites: followers of the god Shiva</note>, these hymns hold as much authority as the Sanskrit Vedas <note>original: "Sanskrit Vedas"; the most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note>. Simply memorizing them is considered a virtuous act, and devout Tamil parents have their children learn them by heart, much as Christian parents might have their children memorize the Psalms.

<summary>This page introduces the cultural and spiritual importance of Tamil Śaivite hymns, comparing their status in South India to the Vedas and the Christian Psalms.</summary>

<keywords>Tamil hymns, Śaivism, Hinduism, Sanskrit Vedas, religious education, South Indian culture</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction, focusing on the two primary collections of Tamil Śaivite hymns—the Dēvāram and the Tiruvāchakam—explaining their origins, authorship, and the challenges of translating their poetic complexity into English.</meta>

->### HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS<-

The hymns provided here are examples from the *Dēvāram* and the *Tiruvāchakam*. The *Dēvāram* is the first of the collections of works held as canonical <note>canonical: recognized as sacred, official scripture</note> by Tamil Śaivites <note>Śaivites: followers of the Hindu god Shiva</note>. Its hymns were composed between 600 and 800 A.D. by the three authors described in this book, and the whole work was compiled into a single collection of 797 stanzas by Nambi Āṇḍār Nambi around 1000 A.D. The *Tiruvāchakam*, or *Sacred Utterance* <note>original: "Tiruvāchakam"</note>, was written by a single author, Māṇikka Vāsahar <note>original: "Māṇikya Vāsahar", the Sanskrit form; "Māṇikka Vāsahar" is the Tamil form</note>. The date of this work is still so uncertain that scholars are divided on whether it was written before or after the *Dēvāram*, though most place it in the ninth or early tenth century. Whenever it was written, it is held in even higher regard than the *Dēvāram* in the hearts of the Tamil people.

From a vast number of hymns, we have tried to select those that are most representative, those that are favorites, and those that contain the most striking thoughts. However, it is incredibly difficult to provide a fair or adequate sense of them in an English translation. These are essentially songs, intended to be sung to Indian melodies, using poetic rhythms that no English meter can replicate. Much of their charm depends on assonance <note>assonance: the repetition of vowel sounds to create internal rhyming</note>, on wordplay, on the tight connection between words, and on intricate rhythms and rhymes—almost as much as it does on their actual content. We can only claim a reasonable degree of accuracy in our translations, and we apologize to lovers of Tamil poetry for the plainness and lack of richness in our representation of such a diverse and beautiful original. All our translations are new, and nearly all those from the *Dēvāram* represent verses that have never been translated into English before.

<summary>This page introduces the two primary scriptures of Tamil Śaivism, the Dēvāram and the Tiruvāchakam, while explaining the historical context of their authors and the difficulties of translating their musical poetry into English.</summary>
<keywords>Dēvāram, Tiruvāchakam, Tamil Śaivism, Nambi Āṇḍār Nambi, Māṇikka Vāsahar, Indian music, devotional hymns, translation methodology</keywords>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

...into English. One of the translators of this book grew up as a Shaivite <note>Shaivite: a follower of the Hindu god Shiva</note> child and learned to love these hymns; he is therefore the authority on their interpretation, while the Englishman was responsible for the literary form. We shall be quite satisfied if our translations serve to draw attention to these poems and are one day replaced by worthier versions.

We have tried to keep the introductory material to a minimum, providing only the information necessary to enable readers to understand the hymns and their various allusions. However, it is absolutely necessary to say something about the worship of Shiva and to provide a brief biography for each of the four authors whose work is featured in this book.

### (B)—The Worship of Shiva

1. *Its history prior to these poems.*

The word Shiva appears even in the *Rig Veda* <note>Rig Veda: the oldest of the four Vedas, the ancient sacred scriptures of Hinduism written in Sanskrit</note>, but there it is only used in conjunction with Rudra. The joining together of these names suggests that we may be seeing the merging of two earlier deities—one Aryan <note>Aryan: referring to the Indo-European peoples who settled in ancient India</note> and one Dravidian <note>Dravidian: referring to the indigenous peoples and language families of southern India, such as Tamil</note>. However, we do not need to dwell on that here, as it is clear that even at this early date, Shiva was an Aryan deity identical to Rudra, the storm god and father of the Maruts <note>Maruts: storm deities in Hindu mythology who accompany Indra or Rudra</note>, who are storm gods themselves. Rudra is a handsome god; he uses his thunderbolts primarily to punish evildoers and is, on the whole, a benevolent being. The name Shiva means "auspicious," and it must not be confused with the Tamil word for "red," although as it happens, Rudra-Shiva was described as a red-colored being.

<vocab>Shaivite, Shiva, Rig Veda, Rudra, Aryan, Dravidian, Maruts, Tamil</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the collaborative effort behind the translation of the hymns and introduces the historical origins of Shiva, identifying him with the Vedic storm god Rudra.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva, Rudra, Rig Veda, Shaivism, Aryan, Dravidian, Maruts, translation methodology</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical introduction to the worship of Shiva, moving from the Vedic period into the era of the Puranas and the religious shifts in South India.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

In the era of the Puranas <note>Puranas: a vast genre of Indian literature about a wide range of topics, particularly myths and legends about the gods, composed primarily in Sanskrit</note>, we find that Shiva—rather than being merely one of many nature deities—has risen to become part of the great triad: Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva, who stand far above all other gods. We do not yet have the means to discover exactly how this change occurred. The deity's function changed as much as his identity; Shiva is now regarded as the destroyer, just as Brahma is the creator and Vishnu is the preserver. The process of reducing the number of superior deities continued until Brahma practically faded into the background, leaving only Vishnu and Shiva as the supreme beings worshipped by the people of India. 

By the time Hinduism penetrated southward into the Tamil country (probably around 500 B.C.), it existed in two main forms: the worship of Vishnu and the worship of Shiva. At that time, these two paths were not strictly separated. The Tamil Hindus believed in the existence of both gods but considered their own chosen deity—whether Shiva or Vishnu—to be supreme. Hinduism seemed firmly established, but it was seriously threatened when the Jains and Buddhists spread throughout South India. During this crisis, the teachers known as the Alvars <note>Alvars: twelve ancient Tamil poet-saints who were devotees of Vishnu</note> rose to defend the Vaishnavite <note>Vaishnavite: a worshipper of the god Vishnu</note> faith, while Shaivism was defended by the poets whose work is featured in this book. Hinduism was saved, but from that point forward, it existed in two distinct forms—Vaishnavism and Shaivism—separated by a wider divide than in earlier days.

2. *The portrait of Shiva and its interpretation.*

Worshippers imagine Shiva in a human form, usually with one head, though occasionally he is depicted with five or six. He has three eyes: the right eye is the sun, the left eye is the moon, and the one in the middle of his forehead is fire. His reddish hair is

<vocab>Purāṇas, Śiva, Brahmā, Vishṇu, Hinduism, Tamil, Jains, Buddhists, Vaishṇavites, Āḻvārs, Śaivism</vocab>

<summary>This page traces the historical rise of Shiva as a supreme deity within the Hindu triad and describes how Shaivism and Vaishnavism emerged as distinct movements to counter the spread of Jainism and Buddhism in South India.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva, Puranas, Brahma, Vishnu, Tamil Hinduism, Jainism, Buddhism, Alvars, Shaivism, Vaishnavism, religious history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the physical description of the god Shiva, detailing his traditional appearance, symbols, and various forms as recognized in South Indian Shaivism.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

His hair is matted in the way of an ascetic <note>ascetic: a person who practices severe self-discipline and abstains from all forms of indulgence, often for religious reasons</note>, and upon it rests the crescent moon, the Ganges River, and one or more cobras; wreathed around his head is a garland of *kondai* <note>original: koṇdai; the golden shower tree or Cassia fistula</note> flowers. He typically has four arms—though he is occasionally shown with eight—but only one body and two legs. He is commonly shown seated on a grey-colored bull <note>The bull is Nandi, Shiva's mount and gatekeeper</note>. His skin is reddish in color, but his body is smeared over with white sacred ash. In his hands, he holds various symbolic objects such as a battle-axe, a deer, fire, a trident, and a bow. Around his neck, which is dark <note>Shiva's neck is blue/dark because he swallowed poison to save the world</note>, hangs a long necklace made of beads that are actually skulls. At his waist, he wears sometimes an elephant’s hide, sometimes a tiger’s skin, and sometimes only a very simple loincloth. 

Generally, his consort, Uma, is at his left side, but he is sometimes pictured as being half-man and half-woman <note>This form is known as Ardhanarishvara</note>; in this form, the right half (Shiva) is pink-colored, and the left half (Uma) is green or black. Shiva’s home is said to be on Mount Kailasa in the Himalayas, but one of his favorite haunts is the cremation ground <note>original: "burning-ground"</note>, where bodies are burned. 

One of the most popular manifestations of Shiva is *Nataraja* <note>Nataraja: "The Lord of the Dance"</note>, the dancer in the great hall at Chidambaram, which is shown in the frontispiece of this book. In this form, Shiva has one face, four arms, and two legs, performing a spirited dance. His right foot rests upon a demon named Muyalahan <note>Muyalahan: a demon representing spiritual ignorance and carelessness</note>. He is sometimes represented as dancing alongside Kali—not the Kali who is identified with Uma in North India, but a local spirit feared in the South.

Undoubtedly, each of these features in the physical representation of Shiva has its own history, though much of it is currently unknown. The legends provide imaginative explanations for most of them. For example, the tiger’s skin and the elephant’s hide are said to be those which Shiva stripped from wild animals sent to attack him by the magic of his enemies...

<summary>This page describes the complex iconography of the god Shiva, detailing his physical attributes, his various symbolic possessions, and his famous manifestation as Nataraja, the Lord of the Dance.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva, iconography, Uma, Nataraja, Chidambaram, Mount Kailasa, cremation ground, Hindu mythology, Tamil saints</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of Shiva’s symbolic attributes and begins a section detailing four major legends associated with him in Tamil Shaivite tradition, specifically starting with the story of the pillar of fire.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL SHAIVITE SAINTS</header>

enemies, the *rishis* <note>original: rishis; ancient Hindu sages or seers</note> of Darukavana. However, it is more interesting to explore the religious ideas these symbols suggest to a thoughtful Shaivite devotee today. The animal hides remind the devotee that Shiva possesses all power and that all opposition to him is futile. The right foot of Nataraja <note>Nataraja: "The Lord of the Dance," one of Shiva's most famous forms</note> placed upon Muyalahan <note>Muyalahan: a dwarf-like demon representing ignorance and the human ego</note> signifies that God crushes all evil. The skulls in his necklace are those of successive Brahmas, each of whom died after a lifespan lasting many ages. This symbolizes that while other gods eventually reach their end, Shiva remains eternal and unchanging. Shiva’s dance illustrates how easily and rhythmically he performs his five cosmic functions: creation, preservation, destruction, judgment, and purification. Finally, his dance in the cremation ground <note>original: "burning-ground"</note> conveys the message that God becomes most real to people during the solemn moments when they part from their dead.

### 3. *Four common legends and their meaning.*

Of the many legends concerning Shiva, four are so frequently alluded to in our poems that they should be told here to avoid repeated explanatory notes.

1. Brahma and Vishnu once saw a pillar of fire that seemed to grow from the depths of the earth and pierce beyond the highest heavens. Longing to discover its depth and height, they agreed that Brahma would become a swan to fly to the top of the pillar, while Vishnu would become a boar to dig toward its base. The swan flew high into the sky but never reached the summit. The boar dug through the earth with his tusks but never found the pillar's origin. Brahma and Vishnu were forced to acknowledge their limitations and prayed to the pillar. At that moment, Shiva revealed himself, for the pillar was a form he had taken. Not even the greatest and...

<summary>This page explains the symbolic meanings behind Shiva's iconography and introduces the "Pillar of Fire" legend, which establishes Shiva's supremacy over Brahma and Vishnu.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva iconography, Nataraja, Muyalahan, Brahma, Vishnu, Shaivite symbolism, Hindu mythology, Lingodbhava</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page provides context for the mythological background of the hymns, specifically the story of the churning of the ocean and the origin of Śiva's title as "the blue-throated one."</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVA SAINTS</header>
<warning>Visible foxing and staining throughout the page. A piece of tape or a dark stain at the top edge partially obscures the header word "ŚAIVA".</warning>

Those who forget God in their pride must be punished. When those whom God uses as his instruments begin to think themselves indispensable to him, he shows <note>original: shews</note> that his purposes can be fulfilled without them.

4. The gods once began to churn the ocean in the hope of obtaining <term>divine nectar</term> <note>original: amrita, the liquid of immortality</note>. The mountain <term>Mandāra</term> <note>A sacred mountain in Hindu mythology used as a churning rod</note> was their churning-stick, the <term>primeval tortoise</term> <note>The Kurma avatar of Vishnu, who supported the mountain on his back</note> was the pivot on which the stick rested and turned, and the serpent <term>Vāsuki</term> was the churning-rope. 

As they churned, at first great and splendid things came up. But suddenly something black rose up and darkened the whole universe. It was a mass of poison, deadly to gods and men alike. In terror of destruction, the gods and demons called on Śiva. He came, drank the poison, and saved them all. That which was enough to destroy the universe could only stain his throat with a bluish color. That is why Śiva is often called the <term>“poison-necked”</term> or <term>“blue-throated”</term> god <note>original: Nilakantha</note>. There is a link here—small but real—with the Christian teaching of God as being ready to suffer for the sake of humbler beings.

<vocab>Mandāra, Vāsuki, Śiva, churning of the ocean, divine nectar, poison-necked, blue-throated</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts the Hindu myth of the Churning of the Ocean, explaining how Śiva saved the universe by consuming a deadly poison, which resulted in his iconic blue throat. The authors also draw a parallel between this act of divine sacrifice and Christian theology.</summary>
<keywords>Śiva, Mandāra, Vāsuki, Samudra Manthan, Blue-throated God, Nilakantha, divine sacrifice, mythology</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page begins the biographical introduction to Sambandar, following the section title page. It establishes the historical and religious context of 7th-century South India.</meta>

### I

# SAMBANDAR

## (Tamil: TIRU JÑĀNA SAMBANDAMŪRTI SWĀMĪ)

During the first half of the seventh century A.D., the worship of Shiva <note>original: Śiva</note> was at its lowest point, overshadowed by Jainism and Buddhism, which prevailed throughout the Tamil region. However, a few devout Saivites <note>followers of Shiva</note> remained faithful. One of them—whose name means "his heart was laid at Shiva's foot" <note>original: Śivapāda-hrudayar</note> and who lived in the town in the Tanjore District now known as Shiyali <note>modern Sirkazhi</note>—prayed to the Shiva worshipped in the Shiyali temple. He asked that he might be given a son who would dispel the godless darkness and win people back to Lord Shiva. 

Sambandar’s birth was the answer to that prayer. At the tender age of three—as orthodox Saivites believe—this child was fed by Shiva’s wife <note>the goddess Parvati</note> with milk from her divine breast, mingled with divine wisdom. This is why his full name, **Tiru Jñāna Sambandar**, means "The man connected with divine wisdom."

He grew up to be a pilgrim poet who visited most of the Saivite shrines that abound in South India, singing the praises of Shiva in every place he worshipped. The cause he loved suffered a severe blow when the great king of Madura <note>modern Madurai</note>, along with many of his subjects, converted to the Jain religion. The queen-consort and her prime minister (see stanzas 20 and 21) remained faithful to Saivism and sent for Sambandar.

<vocab>Sambandar, Tiru Jñāna Sambandamūrti Swāmī, Śiva (Shiva), Śaivism (Saivism), Śaivites (Saivites), Jainism, Buddhism, Tamil, Shiyāli (Shiyali), Madura (Madurai)</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the 7th-century saint Sambandar, describing his miraculous childhood and his role in revitalizing Saivism during a time when Jainism and Buddhism were dominant in South India.</summary>
<keywords>Sambandar, Shiva, Saivism, Jainism, Buddhism, Shiyali, Madurai, Tamil saints, religious history, 7th Century</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Sambandar, focusing on his conflicts with Jain and Buddhist rivals and establishing his historical timeframe in the 7th century.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>SAMBANDAR</header>

The lonely saint faced a vast multitude of Jains in the presence of the king, defeated them in argument, and reconverted the king. With Sambandar's consent, eight thousand of the stubborn Jains were impaled alive <note>This event, while central to traditional Saivite hagiography (sacred biography), is viewed by many modern historians as a legendary exaggeration symbolizing the decisive religious shift from Jainism to Saivism in the Madurai region.</note>. Later, after a similar adventure in another of the three great kingdoms of the Tamil country <note>The three kingdoms were the Chera, Chola, and Pandya dynasties.</note> of his time, Sambandar converted a crowd of Buddhist opponents to Saivism.

This is nearly all that is known of a man who helped to "sing" Buddhism out of Southern India and who composed the collection of hymns which stands first among the sacred scriptures <note>original: "canonical works"</note> of Saivites. Legends portray him as a worker of wonders, but we must draw our knowledge of the man from his poems themselves. He was certainly skillful in handling the many meters in which Tamil poetry is written, and it is not impossible that his compositions were as effortless as the stories about him suggest. That is their weakness, for there is not very much "heart religion" <note>The 1921 authors, writing from a Western missionary perspective, used this term to suggest a perceived lack of personal, inward emotional piety compared to their own traditions.</note> in them. But they seem to have powerfully helped in the process of eliminating Jainism and Buddhism from India—a process of which we know very little, though it was complete enough to be one of the marvels of history. Their author holds the foremost place among the four great "Saivite Preceptors" (*Shivacharyar*) <note>The four *Samaya Kuravars* or religious teachers: Sambandar, Appar, Sundarar, and Manikkavacakar.</note>, and some call him the incarnation of one of the sons of Shiva <note>Sambandar is traditionally considered an incarnation of the god Murugan (Skanda).</note>.

His date seems to be one of the few clearly established dates in the history of the religion of the country. Stanza 19 shows <note>original: shews</note> that he was a contemporary of another great early Saivite, whose name means "Little Servant of God" <note>This refers to Siruthondar, also known as Paranjothi, who was a general under the Pallava King Narasimhavarman I.</note>, and who is known to have fought in a battle which took place in 642 A.D. <note>The Battle of Vatapi, where the Pallavas defeated the Chalukyas.</note>

<vocab>Sambandar, Jains, Saivism, Buddhism, Tamil, Saivites, Shivacharyar (Śivāchāryar), Shiva (Śiva)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes Sambandar's successful religious challenges against Jains and Buddhists and confirms his historical placement in the 7th century through his contemporary, the warrior-saint Siruthondar.</summary>

1. He who wears an earring in one ear, who rides the bull, and wears the pure white crescent moon;
<margin>c</margin> He who is smeared with ash from the forest’s cremation grounds, the thief who has stolen my heart—
<margin>c</margin> He is the Lord of great Brahmapuram <note>Brahmapuram is an ancient name for the city of Sirkali. This is traditionally the first verse Sambandar sang as a three-year-old child after receiving divine milk from the Goddess Parvati.</note>, where long ago He showed grace to the one on the flower-throne <note>original: "Eadu-udaiya-malarān"—referring to the god Brahma, who is seated on a lotus flower.</note> who bowed and praised Him.

We begin with the first verse that the author composed. According to the legends, he spoke it at the age of three on the banks of the temple tank at Shiyali (once called Brahmapuram), after Shiva’s consort <note>Parvati, the divine mother</note> had fed him with milk from her own breast. The stanza itself, of course, contains no direct reference to this story, but it is one of the best-known verses in the Shaivite hymn-book.

> 1. He wears rings in His ears, He rides the bull;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;His head is decorated with the light of the crescent moon;
> He is white with ash swept from the cremation ground;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And He is the thief who steals my heart away.
> Great Brahma, seated on the blooming lotus,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the past bowed down and praised His glory,
> And through singing, he received the grace of our Lord
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who lives in famous, ancient Brahmapuram <note>Brahmapuram is an ancient name for the city of Sirkali.</note>.

---

No pilgrimage in South India is more popular than the one to Tiruvannamalai in North Arcot, a temple located by a hill celebrated in many poems. Shaivism <note>The branch of Hinduism that worships Shiva as the supreme deity.</note> has tried to express the existence of the "eternal feminine" within the deity by giving Shiva a lady who is not only His consort but is actually a part of Him. This is represented in many images which show Shiva as masculine on one side and feminine on the other <note>original: Ardhanarishvara</note>.

> 2. He is our only Lord, still joined
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To her whose breast no child's lips have ever known <note>original: "Unnamulai," the name of the Goddess at Tiruvannamalai, meaning "She whose breasts are never suckled."</note>.
> Those who bow before our Great One—
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who is both Lord and Lady—
> At the holy hill of Annamalai,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Where waterfalls noisily chatter down
> And the mountain sparkles like a gem;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For them, the terrifying fruit of the good and bad deeds
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That they may do shall surely exist no more <note>This refers to the concept of karma, the cycle of cause and effect that binds a soul to reincarnation.</note>.

3. பறையும்பழி பாவம்படு துயரம்பல தீரும்

> 3. Guilt will vanish, and the many sorrows born of sin will come to an end, if you only say the name "Neythanam" <note>Neythanam, now known as Thillaisthanam, is a sacred village near Tanjore famous for its Shiva temple.</note>. It is the town of our Lord who wears the crescent moon, the holy river, and the serpent in His matted hair <note>original: "jata"; Shiva caught the descending Ganges river in His hair to break its fall and wears a snake as a symbol of His power over death and nature.</note>. The town stands upon the northern bank where the sounding waves of the Cauvery <note>The Cauvery (Kaveri) is the primary sacred river of South India.</note> roll, and it is a place where modest, beautifully adorned women dwell.

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hymns dedicated to the sacred sites along the Cauvery River, transitioning from the description of Neyttanam to the famous legend of Markandeya, which illustrates Shiva's power over death.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>AR</header>

One of the first puzzles for a student of Shaivism <note>Shaivism: The branch of Hinduism that worships Shiva as the Supreme Being.</note> is how each of the numerous shrines is spoken of as if it were Shiva’s exclusive home. The broad river marked on English maps as the Cauvery—but called the Kaveri in Tamil—brings so much prosperity to a large part of South India that the deep respect in which it is held is easy to understand. It is lined throughout its length with shrines believed to grant Shiva's blessings to all who visit them. One of these is "Neyttanam," which means "Place of Ghee" <note>Ghee: Clarified butter, highly valued in Indian cooking and sacred rituals.</note>.

> 3. If you only say that Neyttanam is the home
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of our great Lord, who wears in His long matted hair
> The crescent moon, the river, and the snake—
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Neyttanam, where chaste and beautiful maidens gather
> On the north bank of the Kaveri’s roaring stream—
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then your wickedness, your guilt, the sin you dread,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And your many sorrows shall all be driven away.

This example of a hymn connected with Palani <note>original: "Palny"</note> in the Madurai District alludes (in stanza 5) to the well-known legend which teaches, in the Shaivite tradition, that those who love God need not fear death. Markandeya was a boy devoted to Shiva, but a terrible cloud hung over his life: the fates had decreed that he would not live past his sixteenth year. As the appointed time drew near, his father lived in an agony of dread; however, Markandeya remained free from fear and spent all his time worshiping Shiva. The god of Death <note>Yama, the Hindu god of death and justice.</note> finally arrived. Ignoring the fact that the boy was in the middle of worship, he threw over him the noose used to pull human life out of the body. The boy clung to Shiva's <term>lingam</term> <note>Lingam: An abstract, often cylindrical representation of the god Shiva used for worship in temples.</note> with both hands. From within the lingam, Shiva burst forth, kicked the terrifying death-god, and pierced him with his <term>trident</term> <note>Trident: A three-pronged spear, or "Trishula," the primary weapon of Shiva.</note>. Thus, Markandeya was saved. This scene is depicted in sculptures on many temples.

<vocab>Shaivism, Shiva, Cauvery, Kaveri, Neyttanam, Palani, Madurai District, Markandeya, lingam, trident</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the significance of the many shrines dedicated to Shiva along the Kaveri River and recounts the popular legend of Markandeya, whom Shiva saved from the god of Death.</summary>
<keywords>Neyttanam, Kaveri River, Markandeya, Shiva, Lingam, Yama (God of Death), South Indian Shrines, Shaivism</keywords>

4. Chanting the Vedas <note>Vedas: The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</note>, wearing the white sacred thread, and riding upon the white bull, He comes in glory surrounded by His spirit-host <note>original: "Bhutas" (ghostly attendants of Shiva)</note>. Clad in the skin of a flayed tiger, He is the Lord of Pazhanam town. He will wash away the sins of all those who stand and worship His feet, crying out, "O Master!", "O Naked One!", "O our Hope!"

5. He has an eye upon His forehead <note>The "third eye" represents spiritual wisdom and the power to destroy evil.</note> and the crescent moon in His matted hair. My Father and Great Lord, together with the Daughter of the Himalayas <note>original: "Imavan Makal" (the goddess Parvati)</note>, dwells in Pazhanam, where bees hum melodies in the flowering groves. It was He who kicked Death <note>original: "Kala" or Yama, the god of death</note> with His foot, making the blood flow like a river, until Death was destroyed. <note>This refers to the legend of Markandeya, where Shiva saved a young devotee from the clutches of Death.</note>

6. In the "Little Temple" <note>original: "Chit-ambalam"</note> of Tillai <note>Tillai: The ancient name for Chidambaram, one of the most sacred Shiva temples in South India.</note> live those who have driven away the evils of the dark age <note>original: "Kali" (the Kali Yuga, an age of spiritual decline)</note> by tending the sacred fires according to their learning. Sin shall never cling to those who cling to the feet of the Primal Lord, who wears the young white moon.

4. Chanting the holy Vedas <note>original: "Vedas", the oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He wears the sacred thread;
All His spirit-hosts <note>original: "hosts", the Bhutas or supernatural attendants of Shiva</note> surround Him
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As He is carried by the white bull.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Dressed in a tiger-skin.
"Lord, our naked beggar,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Desired above all others,"
Cry out in your worship,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As you appeal at His feet.
He who lives in Pazhanam <note>original: "Palny"</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Will heal all your sins.

5. He has three eyes on His forehead,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And the beautiful moon crowns His hair;
When Death sought a victim,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Shiva's <note>original: "Śiva’s"</note> foot struck there;
Gory streams of blood flowed,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And it was Death itself that died.
Such is He, our Father,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With Uma <note>original: "Umā", the goddess Parvati, Shiva's consort</note> at His side;
He lives forever in Pazhanam <note>original: "Palny"</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Where bees hum around,
Drunk with honeyed sweetness,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Until the groves echo with the sound.

---

A multitude of hymns celebrate the glory of Chidambaram, known in ancient times as Tillai, which is the holiest of all the Shaivite <note>original: "Śaivite", followers of the god Shiva</note> shrines. Devout Shaivites feel for it much as the Jewish people feel for Jerusalem. The practice of maintaining the sacrificial fire dates back to prehistoric times and was already firmly established when the earliest hymns of the Rig Veda <note>The oldest of the four Vedas</note> were composed.

6. Tending the sacrificial fire as taught in ancient times,
The pure Brahmans <note>original: "Brahmans", the priestly class</note> at Tillai drive away terrible misfortune.
The First of All Beings lives there, crowned by the moon; and those who cling
Forever to His feet will never be troubled by the sins that would otherwise cling to them.

<vocab>Sambandar, Vedas, Shiva, Uma, Pazhanam, Chidambaram, Tillai, Shaivite, Rig Veda, Brahmins</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a hymn to Shiva at Pazhanam, highlighting the legend of Shiva defeating Death, and introduces the spiritual significance of the Chidambaram temple (Tillai).</summary>
<keywords>Sambandar, Shiva, Pazhanam, Chidambaram, Tillai, Markandeya, Brahmins, Rig Veda, Shaivism</keywords>

> 7. He of the Vedas <note>original: "Maṟaiyāṉai", the Master of the sacred scriptures</note>, He whose bright locks the white crescent moon 
> Crowns; the Lord who is joined with the Woman <note>original: "peṇṇoṭākiya", referring to the Ardhanarishvara form where Shiva and the Goddess Parvati are one body</note>, the Sovereign 
> Who dwells in beautiful Kanchi Ekambam <note>original: "Kacchi-ekambaththu", the Ekambareswarar Temple in Kanchipuram</note>—
> My heart thinks of Him and no other.

> 8. He whose nature is good, who has no evil; He who has 
> A white bull, who has Uma <note>original: "Umai", the Goddess</note> for half His form; He who has 
> The wealth of grace that never fails those who reach Him;
> He who owns the hill of Tiruchirappalli <note>original: "Chirāppalli"</note>—
> At the mention of Him, my heart is soothed with joy <note>original: "kuḷirum", literally "grows cold." In Tamil poetry, a "cool" heart represents the peace and refreshment of divine love, whereas a "hot" heart represents suffering.</note>.

> 9. You are our faults, and You are our virtues, O Lord of Aalavai <note>original: "Ālavāy", an ancient name for the city of Madurai</note>;
> You are our kin, You are our Master, You are the light 
> That shines forever; You are the meaning of the scriptures 
> We study, the purpose and the joy; 
> Since You are all these things, how could I praise anyone but You?

<meta>This page provides poetic translations of the three hymns introduced on the previous page. It focuses on the sacred sites of Kanchipuram, Tiruchirappalli, and Madurai, exploring the theological concept of Shiva as an all-encompassing deity who transcends the duality of good and evil.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>SAMBANDAR</header>

Kanchipuram, the ancient Tamil name of which is given in this stanza, though more famous as a Vaishnavite <note>original: "Vaishṇavite"; a devotee of the god Vishnu</note> than as a Shaivite <note>original: "Śaivite"; a devotee of the god Shiva</note> shrine, offers in its temples a remarkable summary of the religious history of South India. See the article ‘Kanchipuram’ in Dr. Hastings’ ‘Encyclopedia of Religion and Ethics.’

> 7. He is the essence of holy scripture <note>original: "pith of holy writ", referring to the Vedas</note>;
>     And in the tangle of His hair
> The spotless crescent moon’s ray is lit;
>     He is both Lord and Lady fair <note>This refers to Ardhanarishvara, the form of Shiva where He is half-man and half-woman, representing the union of masculine and feminine energies.</note>.
> He, our great sovereign, does reside
>     In Kanchi Ekambam’s <note>original: "Kachchi Ehambam", the Ekambareswarar Temple in Kanchipuram</note> beautiful town.
> My mind can think of nothing else,
>     Nothing besides Him, and Him alone.

The next two stanzas, taken from two separate hymns associated with the great cities of Tiruchirappalli <note>original: "Trichinopoly"</note> and Madurai <note>original: "Madura"</note>, both sacred places of Shaivism, are set side by side in order to bring out a point which even the most sympathetic student may not ignore. Shiva is commonly spoken of as all good, as in stanza 8, and yet not infrequently He includes, as in stanza 9, both good and its opposite. The pantheistic <note>The theological belief that God is identical with the universe and all things within it, both positive and negative.</note> tendency even in these hymns causes God to be sometimes depicted as so all-embracing as to include evil as well as good.

> 8. All goodness has He and no shadow of evil.
>     Grey-white is His bull, and fair Uma <note>original: "Umā", the Goddess Parvati</note> shares His form.
> His wealth is beyond searching. Tiruchirappalli’s <note>original: "Chirāpaḷḷi"</note> hill
>     Is His; praising Him keeps my heart ever warm.

> 9. You are right and You are wrong,
>     Lord of holy Aalavai <note>original: "Ālavāy", an ancient name for the city of Madurai</note>;
> Kinsman, I to You belong;
>     Never fades Your light away.
> You are the meaning of divine books,
>     You are my wealth, my bliss are You,
> You are my all, and in Your shrine
>     With what praises can I bow?

The text explores Shiva's nature as an all-encompassing deity who represents both the essence of scripture and a pantheistic reality that transcends the distinction between good and evil.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hymns of the saint Sambandar, focusing on the themes of divine revelation and the devotee's protection from astrological influences. It provides commentary on a specific stanza and explains cultural metaphors used in the poetry.</meta>

<warning>There is a large dark stain at the top center of the page, obscuring part of the header.</warning>
<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>SAMBAN DAR</header>

No one can know Shiva unless He chooses to reveal Himself. This thought constantly recurs with great emphasis in these hymns. Its favorite expression is found in the first of the four legends told in our introduction. Hymn singers are fond of contrasting the unsuccessful search of Brahma and Vishnu with the revelation of Himself which Shiva has graciously granted to his devotees. Compare stanzas 25 and 48.

> 10. You are the Light whom Brahma, the source of all being, and
>       Vishnu could not see;
> I have no righteousness of my own, I only speak in praise of
>       You.
> Come, Lord of Valivalam <note>A sacred site in Tamil Nadu</note>, I pray that no dark consequences of my deeds <note>original: "fruit of deeds", referring to the burden of karma</note>
>       may torment your servant, who extols You with his song
>       day after day.

---

Astrology plays a large part in popular Hinduism, and the influence of harmful or lucky stars must be reckoned with in daily life. Most harmful of all is the influence of an eclipse, which is traditionally said to be caused by two dragons, <term>Rahu</term> and <term>Ketu</term>, who swallow the moon or the sun. This stanza lists the nine planets—the Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, Rahu, and Ketu—and says that for the singer who has Shiva in his heart, all of them, even the dragons of the eclipse, are lucky. It is a powerful and characteristically Hindu way of saying that all things work together for good for those who love God.

The reference to bamboo constantly recurs in descriptions of a woman's beauty. Everyone who has seen a feathery cluster of bamboo trees waving in the breeze will understand why it is used as a symbol of delicate grace.

The <term>veena</term> <note>original: "vīṇa"</note> is the most delicate and beautiful stringed instrument played in South India.

<vocab>Shiva, Brahma, Vishnu, Valivalam, astrology, Rahu, Ketu, veena, South India, Hinduism</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of divine revelation for knowing Shiva and explains how devotion to God protects a person from the perceived negative influences of astrology and karma.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva, Brahma, Vishnu, Valivalam, Rahu, Ketu, astrology, karma, bamboo metaphor, veena</keywords>

# HYMNS OF TAMIL SAINTS

...those who love God. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "It is a powerful and characteristically Hindu way of saying that all things work together for good for those who love God."</note>

> 11. He whose consort has shoulders as smooth as bamboo, He who swallowed the poison and has a dark throat,
> He who skillfully plays the beautiful lute <note>original: "veena"</note>,
> He who wears the flawless moon and the Ganges river in His hair—
> Because He has entered into my heart,
> The Sun, the Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus,
> Saturn, and both of the shadow-dragons <term>Rahu and Ketu</term> together,
> Are truly good, yes, very good; they are perfectly favorable for those who serve Him.
<note>This verse is from the "Kolaru Pathigam," a hymn sung to overcome the ill effects of planetary alignments.</note>

---

> 12. The sacred ash is the mystic prayer <note>original: "mantram"</note>; the ash is what the celestial beings wear;
> The ash is beauty; the ash is that which is praised;
> The ash is the divine method <note>original: "tantram"</note>; the ash is the essence of the faith;
> It is the sacred ash of the Lord of Alavay <note>An ancient name for the city of Madurai</note>,
> Who shares His form with Uma <note>The goddess Parvati</note> of the coral-red lips.
<note>This verse is from the "Thiru Neetru Pathigam," which Sambandar famously sang to heal the Pandyan King of a fever and convert him back to Shaivism.</note>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of hymns by the child-saint Sambandar (here called Samandar), focusing on the spiritual protection offered by God and the ritual significance of sacred ash (vibhuti).</meta>

# SAMANDAR

> 11. She <note>The Goddess Parvati, who occupies the left half of Shiva's body</note> shares the form of Him whose shoulders' curve rivals the grace of bamboo.
> His throat drank the poison, and He tuned the *vīṇā* <note>A traditional Indian stringed instrument</note> to play.
> The lustrous moon and the Ganges river crown His hair, and He has made a place for Himself within my heart.
> Therefore, whether the moon, the sun, or any star—be it good or ill—should shine, or even the two serpents <term>Rahu and Ketu, the shadow planets that cause eclipses</term>, it does not matter.
> For the servant of Śiva, all are kind; all work for his great gain.

---

White ash from burnt cow-dung must be worn by all true Śaivites <note>Followers of the god Shiva</note>. Every day the worshipper, facing north-east and crying "Śiva, Śiva," must dip the fingers of his right hand in the ash and draw the three middle fingers from left to right along his forehead, thus leaving three horizontal white lines <term>This mark is called the Tripundra</term>. The ceremonial side of Śaivism is so prominent that this one stanza must be included as a specimen of many that extol the virtues and powers of the ash.

The Tantras are works that teach ceremonies, as well as magical performances and mystic rites. Some of these are of an immoral nature <note>The 1921 authors are expressing a common colonial-era bias against Tantric traditions; modern scholars view these texts as complex philosophical and ritual systems</note>.

> 12. The sacred ash has mystic power;
> It is worn by those who dwell in the sky.
> The ash bestows true loveliness.
> Praise of the ash ascends on high.
> The ash reveals what the Tantras mean,
> And tells the essence of true religion—
> The ash of the Lord of Ālavāy <note>An ancient name for the city of Madurai</note>,
> In whom red-lipped Umā <note>The Goddess Parvati</note> dwells.

<summary>This page presents two hymns by Sambandar: one asserting that a devotee is protected from ill astrological omens, and another praising the mystical and ritual power of sacred ash (vibhuti).</summary>
<keywords>Sambandar, Shiva, Parvati, Sacred Ash, Vibhuti, Madurai, Alavay, Tantras, Astrology, Shaivism</keywords>

> ...The ash of the Lord of Alavai <note>A poetic name for the city of Madurai</note>,
> Spotted with red as He sits on His bull.

---

> 13. To those who chant with melting hearts and eyes overflowing with tears of love, it shows the way to the path of goodness. The name of the Lord—**Namaḥ Śivāya** <note>original: நமச்சிவாய—literally, "Obeisance to Shiva"</note>—is the true essence of all four Vedas.

---

> 14. You who seek clarity of mind: if you scatter flowers of devotion at the feet of the Father of Arur <note>Tiruvarur, a sacred temple city in Tamil Nadu</note>, you will find spiritual liberation <term>mukti</term>.

---

> 15. You who wish to break the cycle of rebirth: never forget to praise the Righteous One of Arur; then you shall attain the spirit of renunciation.

---

> 16. You who would wipe away your sorrow: scatter beautiful, golden flowers before the Beloved of Arur; then you shall know eternal bliss <term>ananda</term>.

<note>original vocabulary:
**Namaḥ Śivāya**: The sacred five-syllable mantra of Shiva.
**Mukti**: Final liberation from the cycle of death and rebirth.
**Rebirth**: The continuous cycle of "births" believed in Hindu philosophy.
**Bliss**: Divine happiness or joy.
**Themselves**: Referring to the devotees mentioned in the verses.</note>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues from the discussion of the sacred ash, transitioning to the importance of the sacred five-syllable mantra "Namaśivāya" and presenting three stanzas dedicated to the deity at Ārūr (Tiruvarur).</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

# SAM<unclear>BAN</unclear>DAR

<warning>A large stain at the top of the page partially obscures the running header.</warning>

Equally important with the wearing of the sacred ash is the constant repetition of the five syllables, or *panchākshara* <term>Sanskrit for "five letters," referring to the mantra Na-ma-śi-vā-ya</term>, "Namaśivāya." This, which means literally "a bow to Śiva," is the chief mantra or mystic utterance of Śaivism <note>The branch of Hinduism that worships Shiva as the Supreme Being</note>. In Śaivite catechisms <term>Instructional summaries of religious doctrine</term>, a whole chapter is devoted to its uses.

> 13. To those who repeat it while tears of love overflow,
>     It gives them life and guides them on the path.
>     It is the true essence of the four Vedas <note>The most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note>,
>     The Lord’s great name; therefore, say "Hail Śiva."

---

The next three stanzas are from a hymn written in a very attractive short-lined meter, and they promise light, freedom from rebirth, and bliss through devotion to Śiva at Ārūr (now Tiruvaḷḷūr <note>Today known as Tiruvarur</note> in the Tanjore District).

> 14. For the Father in Ārūr,
>     Sprinkle the flowers of love;
>     In your heart, true light will dawn,
>     And every bondage will be removed.

> 15. Never forget to laud and praise
>     The Holy One in Ārūr;
>     The bonds of birth will be severed,
>     And worldly ways left behind.

> 16. In Ārūr, the jewel of our Loved One,
>     Scatter beautiful golden blossoms.
>     You shall wipe away your sorrow,
>     And yours will be bliss beyond compare.

<vocab>panchākshara, Namaśivāya, Śiva, Śaivism, Vedas, Ārūr, Tiruvaḷḷūr, Sambandar</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the spiritual significance of the mantra 'Namaśivāya' and provides verses by the saint Sambandar that encourage the worship of Shiva at Tiruvarur to achieve enlightenment and end the cycle of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Sambandar, Namaśivāya, Panchākshara, Tiruvarur, Vedas, Shaivism, Rebirth, Mantras</keywords>

> 17. She cries, "O Lord of the matted hair!" She cries, "O my Refuge!"
>     She cries, "O Rider of the Bull!" and falls trembling in fear.
>     O Lord of Marugal <note>Tiru-marugal, a sacred site where Sambandar is said to have revived a man from a snakebite</note>, where the blue lilies bloom in the water-channels,
>     Is it right that this girl should waste away with such inner grief?
>     <note>original: "சடையாயெனுமால்..." (Saṭaiyāy enumāl...). This verse expresses the distress of a devotee—or in the traditional story, a young woman whose companion had died—appealing to Shiva's mercy.</note>

---

> 18. While Buddhists and Jains <term>Samanas; followers of Jainism who were often religious rivals to the Saivite saints</term>, who lack true understanding, speak slander and do not follow the righteous path, the Lord of Brahmapuram <note>Brahmapuram is an ancient name for Shiyali, the birthplace of Sambandar</note> seeks alms from the world. He performed a wonder by skinning a fierce, wild elephant and wearing its hide as a garment. Is this not He, our Lord, who appears like one possessed by divine madness?
>     <note>original: "புத்தரோடுபொறியில்..." (Puttharōdu poṟiyil...). This verse highlights the sectarian tensions of the time and references the myth where Shiva defeated an elephant-demon (Gajasura) and wore its skin.</note>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the study of the poet-saint Sambandar, focusing on his hymns of intercession and his role in the religious conflicts of 7th-century South India, specifically his opposition to Buddhism and Jainism.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

# SAMBANDAR

<warning>There is a piece of adhesive tape or a stain obscuring the top center of the page over the header.</warning>

Associated with the hymn from which our next verse is taken is a story of the author, Sambandar, helping a sorrowing woman by raising to life the man she loved, who had been killed by a snakebite. The hymn itself makes no reference <note>original: "allusion"</note> to such a miracle, but it does provide an example of interceding on behalf of another person—an element that is somewhat rare in these devotional books.

> 17. Prostrate with fear at Your feet, she cries, "Lord
> with matted hair, my Refuge, Rider of the bull!"
> Lord of Maruhal <note>original: "Maruhal"; refers to the shrine of Tiru-marugal</note> where fresh water-lilies bloom, is
> it right to leave her in this heart-wrenching anguish?

---

The current writer's poems contain such frequent denunciations of Buddhism or Jainism that it is clear they were written at a time when the struggle between Hinduism and these other religions was at its peak. Buddhism and Jainism are hardly known in South India today <note>Note: This refers to the time of the book's publication in 1921; while Jainism remains in small communities, Buddhism had largely disappeared from the region by then</note>, though at one time they were the dominant faiths. It is likely that these songs significantly helped to drive them out of the country.

> 18. Those Buddhists and mad Jains may speak slander.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Such speech is fitting for those who wander from the path.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But He who came to earth and begged for alms <note>Shiva is often depicted as the "Supreme Beggar" (Bhikshatana), wandering with a skull for an alms bowl</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He is the thief who stole my heart away.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The raging elephant charged down at Him;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O marvel! He simply took and wore its hide;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Men think Him a madman, but He is the Lord
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who lives in great Bramāpuram <note>original: "Bramāpuram"; an ancient name for Shiyali, Sambandar's birthplace</note>.

<vocab>Sambandar, Hinduism, Buddhism, Jainism, Maruhal, Bramāpuram, miracle, intercession</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses Sambandar’s hymns of intercession, including a verse pleading for a woman’s grief to be eased, and explores the poet's aggressive stance against Buddhism and Jainism during a period of religious transition in South India.</summary>

<keywords>Sambandar, Tiru-marugal, Brahmapuram, Shiva, religious conflict, Gajasura, intercession, Bhakti movement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the collection of hymns by the poet-saint Sambandar. The previous page discussed his miraculous intercession for a woman in Tiru-marugal and his active opposition to Buddhism and Jainism in 7th-century South India.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

19. O birds among the flowering branches of the laurel tree <note>original: "punnai," a tree common in coastal India often associated with sacred groves</note>! He has caused my beauty to fade and has ended our companionship, leaving me with a sickness of the heart that will not depart. He is the Great Lord <note>original: "Peruman"</note> who dances, holding fire in the wild forest <note>This refers to Shiva's dance in the cremation ground, symbolizing the destruction of the ego and the universe</note>, while Siruthondan of Sengattangudi <note>Siruthondan was a famous contemporary of Sambandar and one of the sixty-three Saivite saints; Sengattangudi is his home shrine</note> performs his service of devotion.

<note>original: "பைங்கோட்டுமலர்ப்புன்னைப்பறவைகாள்பயப்பூரச் சங்காட்டந்தவிர்த்தென்னைத்தவிராநோய் தந்தானே செங்காட்டங்குடிமேயசிறுதொண்டன்பணி செய்ய வெங்காட்டனலேந்திவிளையாடும்பெருமானே."</note>

<summary>This page features a hymn by Sambandar that blends the themes of "love-sickness" (symbolizing the soul's longing for God) with a tribute to his contemporary, the saint Siruthondan.</summary>

<keywords>Sambandar, Siruthondan, Sengattangudi, Shiva's Dance, Punnai Tree, Saivite Saints, Tamil Poetry, Bhakti movement</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the poet-saint Sambandar, focusing on the historical context of his hymns and his contemporary, the saint Siruthondan. It provides a verse translation of the hymn introduced on the previous page and explores the historical figures associated with Sambandar's life.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

<header>SAMBANDAR</header>

The "Little Servant of God" <note>This is the English translation of the name "Siruthondan," one of the 63 canonized Saivite saints.</note> mentioned in the next verse is one of the 63 canonized saints of Saivism. According to the collection of legends known as the *Periya Puranam* <note>The "Great Purana," a 12th-century Tamil hagiography of the 63 Nayanmars</note>, which is a Tamil Saivite classic, he fought at the battle of Vatapi (modern-day Badami), which took place in 642 A.D. There are other indications which strengthen the view that these hymns date from the seventh century A.D.

In the first three lines of the verse, Shiva is conceived as a lover, and the devotee as the woman whom He loves. In India, the pain of being away from a lover is supposed to cause spots <note>Known in Tamil as "pasappu," a pale or yellowed discoloration of the skin caused by the distress of longing.</note> to appear on the skin of the woman who loves.

19. Birds in the flowering green-branched laurel <note>original: "punnai"</note> tree;
Love writes its marks clearly on me, for He
Who cured my grief yet left me with unending pain.
Sengattangudi is His holy temple <note>original: "fane"</note>,
And there His "Little Servant" <note>Siruthondan</note> dwells, who now
And ever bows before Lord Shiva.
There in the cremation ground <note>original: "burning-ground"</note>, with fire in hand,
Our grand Master dances <note>original: "sporteth"</note> unceasingly.

---

Another possible indication of the date occurs in the next two verses, given here in English prose because the Tamil names will not fit into English poetic meters. The Mangaiyarkkarasi mentioned here was the wife of a king of Madura, Kun Pandiyan, who is known to history. According to the collection of stories mentioned above, this king became a Jain. Then the queen and the prime minister named in our poem sent for Sambandar, our author, through whose efforts the king was re-converted, and all the Jain teachers were executed by impaling. Unfortunately, the date of Kun Pandiyan cannot at present be accurately determined. An able discussion of it can be seen in "The Tamilian Antiquary, No. 3."

<vocab>Saivism, Periya Puranam, Vatapi, Badami, Shiva, punnai, Sengattangudi, Mangaiyarkkarasi, Madura, Kun Pandiyan, Jain, Sambandar</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the historical dating of Sambandar’s work to the 7th century based on his connection to Saint Siruthondan and King Kun Pandiyan of Madura. It also explains the "bride-and-groom" mysticism in Saivite poetry, where the soul pining for God is likened to a woman pining for her lover.</summary>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Sambandar’s hymns, focusing on the specific cultural and historical context of his mission in Madurai. It elaborates on the poetic descriptions of the Pandyan royalty and the religious symbolism of the goddess Minakshi.</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>

<header>SAMBANDAR</header>

The explanation of the term "fish-eyed maid," which may sound unusual to English ears, is that in Madurai, Shiva’s wife is called Minakshi, that is <note>original: "i.e."</note>, "the fish-eyed one." This title, frequently given to beautiful women, suggests that the movement of their eyes is as graceful as the motion of a fish in water.

20. This is Aalavaai <note>The sacred center of Madurai</note>, where the Lord of the spirit-beings <note>original: "hosts," referring to the Bhutas who serve Shiva</note>—who takes the form of a flame and gave us the four Vedas <note>The ancient sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note> and their meaning—dwells with the beautiful fish-eyed goddess. Here, reigning like the goddess of good fortune <note>Lakshmi</note>, Mangaiyarkkarasi, the daughter of the Chola king and the virtuous, bracelet-wearing Queen of the Pandyas, serves and praises God every day.

---

The poem from which verses 20 and 21 are taken consists of stanzas like these, which alternate between praising the queen and the king’s minister; the final verse praises them both together.

21. This is Aalavaai, the home of Shiva. To those who renounce the world, He reveals Himself as one who has also renounced all things. As the leader of the heavenly beings, He rides upon a single white bull <note>Nandi, Shiva’s sacred mount</note>. He is praised by Kulachchirai, the minister to that king who wears the white holy ash <note>Sacred ash, or vibhuti, worn by devotees of Shiva</note> and delights in bowing before the feet of Shiva’s humble devotees <note>original: "slaves," a term used in Bhakti poetry to describe absolute devotion</note>.

According to tradition, when Sambandar was about to engage in a religious debate with the Jains <note>Followers of Jainism, who were the religious rivals of the Saivites at the time</note>, the queen feared what might happen to him. However, he reassured her in this verse, declaring that he could face any danger because the God of Madurai was on his side.

<vocab>Sambandar, Minakshi, Madurai, Aalavaai, Mangaiyarkkarasi, Chola, Pandiyan, Kulachchirai, Shiva, Jains, Vedas</vocab>

22. "O great Queen of the King, you who have eyes like a doe, listen! Do not be distressed or pity me, thinking, 'He is only a child with milk still on his lips.' Though I may seem small, I am no weakling to those vile people <note>original: "eenarkal," referring here to the Jains</note> who cause such trouble in places like Anaimamalai <note>Elephant Hill, a prominent Jain center near Madurai</note>, for the Lord of Aalavaai <note>Madurai</note> stands by me."

23. "O pure Lord, Master who dwells in Aalavaai, grant me your grace and say, 'Do not fear!' Let the fire lit by the lying Jains <note>original: "Amanar." Tradition holds that the Jains set fire to Sambandar’s quarters; he prayed for the heat to be transferred to the King as a fever</note> move slowly and go instead to the Pandyan King."

24. "May the Brahmins <note>original: "Andhanar," often used by the saints to mean those who are virtuous and walk in the path of God</note> flourish, and the heavenly beings too. May the herds of cows thrive. May the cool rain fall, and may the King also prosper. Let all that is evil sink away. Let the name of Hara <note>A title for Shiva meaning "The Destroyer of Sin"</note> encompass the whole world, and let all suffering end!"

<summary>These verses capture Sambandar's fearless challenge to his religious rivals in Madurai and conclude with one of the most famous universal blessings in Tamil literature.</summary>

22. O fair one with the deer’s gentle glance,
The Pandya's great Queen, do not think of me
As just a weak, nursing infant,
Just because such wicked enemies are here.
If only Hara <note>original: "Hara," a name for Shiva meaning "The Destroyer" (of sin and suffering)</note> stands by me,
I am stronger than their entire group.

---

The story here is that the Jains had set fire to Sambandar’s house. He prayed in this stanza that the fire, transformed into a fever, might go to the Pandyan King (who was then a Jain). It happened as he prayed, and the King was later converted to the faith of Shiva.

23. O You whose form is fiery red,
Our Father in holy Aalavaai <note>The ancient name for the city of Madurai</note>,
In your grace, deliver me from dread.
False Jains have lit a fire for me;
O, let it go to the Pandyan ruler,
So that he may feel the torture of a slow flame.

---

Our examples of Sambandar’s poetry may end with a verse which is a kind of blessing <note>original: "benediction"</note>, often placed as a lucky or auspicious word on the front page of a Tamil book.

24. Blessed be the pure Brahmins <note>The priestly class</note>, the heavenly beings,
and the cows <note>original: "kine," an archaic word for cattle</note>.
May cool rain fall on the earth! May the King’s glory
shine!
May all forms of evil perish! Let Hara’s name resound!
May sorrow pass away, to the farthest reaches of the earth.

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific poems of the child-saint Sambandar to a broader critical analysis of his work within its historical and social context in Southern India.</meta>

The poems of Sambandar are truly the poems of a seer. However, one thing is lacking—not so much in the realm of the poet’s poetry, but in the realm of the critic’s introduction. The commentator himself is not well-acquainted with these grand verses and, consequently, with the proper foundations of the great Shaiva <note>original: "Saivāt," likely a typo for Shaivism, the branch of Hinduism that worships Shiva as the supreme being</note> tradition.

Regardless of the vessel, one must understand the content from an Indian perspective. Shiva and Bhakti <note>original: "Vhakti," meaning religious devotion or love for God</note>, as well as the specific times and places, are all blended together, and the resulting work radiates a flood of light. In short, the social conditions of the era are revealed in a clear form. That form shows that the Tamil lands were dominated by a landed aristocracy, of which Shiva was the symbol. There was a conflict between Buddhism, Jainism, and Hinduism. At the time, Buddhism held the upper hand. Jainism moved into the sphere of the Shiva cult. One culture was decaying—the tribal <note>original: "gentileris," likely referring to the *gens* or tribal social organization</note> culture represented by Buddhism.

From the ashes of that culture, these poems show the people the way toward Shiva and the Vedic tradition. His form and mobility depend not only upon Him, but upon the Bull <note>Nandi, the sacred bull who serves as the mount and gatekeeper of Shiva</note>. Also, this bull was also

<meta>fragmentary text at bottom: in this light</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes Sambandar’s poetry as a reflection of a social transition in the Tamil country, where a new Shiva-centered Vedic culture arose from the decline of older Buddhist and Jain influences.</summary>
<keywords>Sambandar, Shaivism, Bhakti, Tamil history, Buddhism, Jainism, Vedic tradition, Nandi the Bull</keywords>

# II.

## TIRUNAVUKKARASU SWAMI

->(More commonly known as APPARSWAMI)<-

Sambandar, whose works we have been studying, had a friend older than himself named Appar, or Tirunavukkarasu. Appar belonged to the Vellala <term>Vellala: An influential agricultural caste in South India.</term> caste, which remains a substantial part of the Tamil population to this day. Orphaned at a young age, Appar was raised by a loving elder sister as a devout follower of Shiva. His sister was deeply grieved when Appar abandoned his ancestral faith and became a religious teacher among the Jains. However, her earnest prayers finally succeeded; Appar not only returned to Saivism himself but was also responsible for converting the king of his country back to the faith. 

His full name was Tirunavukkarasu, which means "King of the Tongue," <note>original: "Tirunāvukkarasu"</note> but his young friend Sambandar called him Appar, meaning "Father," <note>original: "Appar"</note> and the name remained with him. He also traveled throughout the Tamil region—sometimes alone and sometimes with Sambandar—singing as he journeyed from one shrine to another. Traditional portraits show him holding a small tool for scraping grass, which he used to clean the stones of the temple courtyards. The Jains persecuted him, and many stories recount his miraculous escapes from their hands.

<summary>This page introduces Tirunavukkarasu Swami (Appar), a prominent 7th-century Tamil saint who converted from Jainism back to Saivism and became a famous hymn-writer.</summary>
<keywords>Tirunavukkarasu, Appar, Sambandar, Vellala caste, Saivism, Jainism, Tamil saints, conversion, Nayanars</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Tirunavukkarasu Swami (Appar), contrasting his devotional style and character with that of the other two primary authors of the Tevaram.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>

# TIRUNAVUKKARASU SWAMI

His hymns reveal a deeply religious nature, characterized by a profound sense of sin and spiritual need, along with an intense joy in God. There is real critical insight <note>original: "acumen"</note> in the old epigram <term>epigram: A concise, clever, and often satirical statement.</term> which depicts Shiva <note>original: "Śiva"</note> evaluating the three great authors of the *Tevaram* <note>original: "Dēvāram"</note>, the Shaivite hymn-book:

> “Sambandar praised himself; Sundarar praised Me for wealth <note>original: "pelf"</note>; My Appar praised Me, Myself.”

<summary>This page analyzes the spiritual quality of Appar's hymns, using a traditional saying to distinguish his selfless devotion from the styles of Sambandar and Sundarar.</summary>
<keywords>Tirunavukkarasu Swami, Appar, Tevaram, Shiva, Sambandar, Sundarar, Shaivite hymns, Tamil literature, religious devotion</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the translations of hymns by Tirunavukkarasu Swami (Appar), moving from the introductory analysis of his spiritual character to specific verses from the Tevaram that illustrate his devotion.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

25. The Lord of her who dwells in the lotus flower <note>original: "The Lord of her who dwells in the lotus-flower" — This refers to the god Vishnu, whose consort is Lakshmi.</note>, and he of the four faces <note>original: "He of the four faces" — This refers to the god Brahma.</note>, both alike sought to find Your feet—one rising up and the other falling down—yet they saw them not. O unique One, our Lord, who dwell in Athikai Veerattanam <note>original: "Athikai Virattanam"</note>, You who have no form, grant me the grace to behold Your holy feet.

---

26. When he who reached Kailash <note>original: "Kailas" — referring to the demon-king Ravana, who attempted to uproot Mount Kailash, the home of Shiva.</note> found his chariot could no longer move, You placed but one of Your holy toes upon him; and he, like a great mountain of snow, was crushed. O You Father of Kadanthai Attenganaimadam <note>A temple in the town of Pennakadanthai.</note>, where the moon touches the great groves, mark me with Your bull <note>original: "Idabam" — The bull Nandi is the emblem and vehicle of Shiva. Being "marked" or branded with the bull signifies being claimed as a servant of God.</note>, and take me for Your own.

<vocab>Tamil Shaivite Saints, Athikai Veerattanam, Kadanthai, Tirunavukkarasar</vocab>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the presentation of hymns by the saint Tirunavukkarasu Swami (Appar). While the previous page provided prose translations, this page offers metrical, poetic versions of those hymns along with commentary on the symbolism of divine ownership.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

<header>TIRUNAVUKKARASU SWAMI</header>

God, who is essentially beyond human understanding, will reveal himself to people through his grace. (See the first of the legends told in the Introduction.) Athihai Virattanam <note>original: "Athihai Virāṭṭānam" — An ancient and famous Shiva temple in the town of Tiruvadigai.</note>, in the South Arcot District, is the temple celebrated in this verse.

> 25. Vishnu, husband of Lakshmi, and four-faced
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Brahma <note>original: "Brahm"</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Searched the heights and depths, but your feet
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;they could never see.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Yet, O unique Lord, who in Athihai dwells,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Though you are formless, in your grace, allow the sight of them
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to be given to me.

---

The most significant thing about our next verse is not just the legend of the crushing of Ravana <note>original: "Rāvaṇa" — The powerful demon-king who attempted to uproot Mount Kailash.</note>, who tried to attack Mount Kailash <note>original: "Kailāsa" — The sacred mountain home of Shiva.</note> where Shiva has his heaven, but rather the idea of the devotee being "stamped" as the property of his God. This is a theme that appears frequently in other hymns. According to tradition, Appar Swami <note>original: "Appar-swāmi"</note> actually received a Hindu equivalent of Saint Francis's stigmata: the mark of Shiva's bull <note>The bull Nandi is Shiva's vehicle and emblem; being marked with it symbolizes total surrender and service to God.</note> as if it were branded on his body. We cannot help but be reminded of Saint Paul’s expression in Galatians 6:17 <note>original: "Galatians vi. 17" — In this verse, Paul writes, "I bear on my body the marks of Jesus," signifying his identity as a servant of Christ.</note>.

> 26. His scepter ruled over all other worlds,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But when he tried to rule Kailash,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Your crushing foot punished his arrogance.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O mark me with the sign of your sacred bull,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As white as the snowy peaks of the Himalayas.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Accept me, O our divine Truth,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There where the moon still rises above
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The groves of Tunganaimadam temple <note>original: "Tūṅgānaimāḍam's shrine" — A specific temple dedicated to Shiva in the town of Pennagadam.</note>.
