<meta>This page continues the collection of hymns by the 7th-century saint Appar (Tirunavukkarasu). While the previous page discussed the physical "stamping" of a devotee as God's property and his vision of Shiva's feet, this page presents two famous verses: one describing the vivid appearance of Shiva at the Tiruppuvanam temple, and another contemplating the ancient, eternal nature of Shiva's presence at Tiruvarur.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 27. The sharp-edged trident appears, and appears again;
> The young moon upon his growing, matted hair appears, and appears again;
> The fragrant garland of golden-shower flowers <note>original: "Konrai" — The yellow Cassia fistula flowers, which are sacred to Shiva.</note> appears;
> The white earring in his ear appears, blending with his radiance;
> The cloak made from the skin of the thundering elephant appears;
> The beautiful, shining holy crown appears;
> The holy body smeared with white ash <note>original: "Podi" — Sacred ash (Vibhuti), representing purity and the transitory nature of the world.</note> appears in all its glory;
> All these appear for our Holy Lord of Tiruppuvanam <note>original: "Pūvaṇam" — An ancient Shiva temple located on the banks of the Vaigai River.</note>.

---

The following hymn addresses the antiquity of Tiruvarur, one of the most sacred sites in Tamil Saivism. The poet asks when Shiva first made this city his home, listing various cosmic events and legends to suggest that God's presence there predates time itself.

> 28. Was it on the day he stood as the Unique One while the world praised him?
> Was it on the day he, the One, became the Three Forms <note>The "Trimurti" — Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva, representing the three cosmic functions of creation, preservation, and destruction.</note>?
> Was it on the day he burned the god of desire <note>original: "Kāman" — Kamadeva, the god of love, who was reduced to ashes by the fire of Shiva's third eye when he tried to disturb the Lord's meditation.</note>?
> Was it on the day he opened his eye and consumed the god of desire with fire?
> Was it on the day he created the earth and the heavens?
> Was it even before he joined with the Goddess <note>original: "Mātu" — Parvati, the female half of Shiva; the union of the divine male and female.</note> as a part of his own body, while holding a young deer in his hand?
> Tell me, when was the day he first took Tiruvarur <note>original: "Tiruvārūr" — A premier temple city in Tamil Nadu, central to the lives of the Saivite saints.</note> as his temple?

<summary>This page features two hymns by Appar: a vivid description of the iconographic features of Shiva at Tiruppuvanam and a philosophical inquiry into the eternal, timeless residence of the Lord at Tiruvarur.</summary>

<keywords>Appar, Tevaram, Saivism, Tiruppuvanam, Tiruvarur, Shiva iconography, Trimurti, Kamadeva, matted hair, sacred ash</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the hymns of the 7th-century Tamil saint Tirunavukkarasu (also known as Appar). While the previous page provided a literal prose translation of Verse 27, this page offers a poetic, metered version of the same vision at the Tiruppuvanam temple. It then introduces Verse 28, which contemplates the prehistoric antiquity of the Shiva temple at Tiruvarur.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>

<header># TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI</header>

Here is the divine vision as the enraptured Shaivite <note>A devotee of the god Shiva.</note> sees it.

> 27. See, there His bright trident appears to me;
> See, there is the moon in His matted hair;
> His garland of flowers from the kondai tree, <note>original: "kondai tree" — The Konrai or Golden Shower tree (Cassia fistula), which produces yellow blossoms sacred to Shiva.</note>
> And the white earring in each of His ears,
> The cloak that He tore from the wild elephant, <note>Refers to the legend of Gajasurasamhara, where Shiva defeated a demon in elephant form and wore its skin.</note>
> His glittering crown and His body's radiance.
> Smeared with ash, He is ever the undefiled,
> In Puvanam <note>original: "Pūvaṇam" — The Tiruppuvanam temple near Madurai.</note> circled by groves all green.

---

The singer, standing at the shrine of Tiruvarur <note>original: "Tiruvalur (Ārūr)"</note> in the Thanjavur <note>original: "Tanjore"</note> district, muses over the ancient connection of his Lord with the holy place. He suggests that it began before the creation, before Shiva performed his greatest miracles, and perhaps even before the one Supreme Lord, Ishvara, expanded into the trinity <note>original: "triad"</note> of Brahma, Vishnu, and Rudra.

> 28. When was that ancient day our Lord
> Chose Arur to be His temple?
> Was it when He stood amidst praising worlds
> Alone, or when the One grew into three?
> Was it when in wrath He burned up Death, <note>Refers to Shiva defeating Yama, the god of death, to save his devotee Markandeya.</note>
> Or turned His flaming eye on Lust? <note>original: "Lust" — Referring to Kamadeva, the god of desire, whom Shiva reduced to ashes with his third eye.</note>
> Or when, creative and immanent,
> He called the earth and sky into being?
> Was it when, with His young deer in His hand,
> He came with Uma as a part of Him? <note>Refers to Shiva’s form as Ardhanarishvara, where he is half-male and half-female, joined with his consort Uma.</note>
> Or before He joined with that lady fair,
> Did He take our Arur to His heart?

<meta>This page continues the hymns of the 7th-century Tamil saint Tirunavukkarasu (Appar). Having previously reflected on the ancient nature of Shiva’s temples, the saint now shifts to a deeply personal tone of confession and surrender, specifically addressing Shiva as the deity of the Tiruvathigai Veerattanam temple.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 29. I have no strength to live by the laws of righteousness; I am not pure in my daily life. I cannot understand the scriptures through study, nor do I have the discipline to keep You constantly in my heart. O Source of All, O Radiant Light! In my longing to see Your holy, flower-like feet, I have wandered aimlessly and grown weary, O Lord of Adhigai Veerattanam <note>original: "Atikai Vīraṭṭāṉar" — The presiding deity at the temple in Tiruvathigai, where Appar was famously cured of a painful stomach ailment.</note>.

> 30. I cannot find clarity of mind; instead, I am sinking into the depths of worldly attachments, trapped in a cycle of past evil deeds <note>original: "tīviṉai" — Referring to the burden of karma resulting from past sins.</note>. I see no path of escape from this daily struggle. O Lord with the gem-like blue throat <note>original: "maṇikaṇṭā" — Shiva’s throat turned blue when he swallowed a lethal poison to save the gods and the world from destruction.</note>, show me the way of Your grace so that I may behold Your two feet, O Lord of Adhigai Veerattanam.

> 31. Like a swing held by a sturdy rope, my heart moves back and forth—letting go of one desire only to grasp another, coming and going without rest. But now, O Lord with the crescent moon tucked in Your matted hair, I have become like a swing whose rope has snapped. I have fallen entirely at Your feet, O Lord of Adhigai Veerattanam <note>The "snapped rope" is a powerful metaphor for the end of worldly wavering and a total, helpless surrender to God’s mercy.</note>.

<summary>In these verses, Appar expresses his personal inadequacy and spiritual exhaustion, using the famous metaphor of a broken swing to illustrate his total surrender to Shiva at the Tiruvathigai temple.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Tiruvathigai Veerattanam, surrender, karma, blue throat, bhakti poetry, Shiva</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the hymns of the 7th-century Tamil saint Tirunavukkarasu (also known as Appar). While previous verses focused on his miraculous cure and the external majesty of the temple, these verses delve into his internal spiritual struggle, shifting from a sense of failure to a state of total surrender to Shiva.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

<header>TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI</header>

It is often said, and not without truth, that Hinduism does not always cultivate a strong sense of sin. However, there are major exceptions. Look at the following verses—samples of many—taken from a hymn that trembles with deep emotion. The author feels overwhelmed by sin. He describes himself as being like a swing, tossing back and forth toward evil and then toward God. But now, what joy! The rope has snapped, and he lies resting firmly at his Lord’s feet. Yet, his old moods still return; his soul feels trapped and sluggish, as if drugged by sleep, and he feels that life has no happiness to offer unless God intervenes to save him.

->THE SOUL’S BITTER CRY<-

29. I do not have the power to live a righteous life;
        Day after day, I am stained with sin.
    I read the scriptures, but I do not understand them;
        I do not keep You within my heart.
    O Light, O Flame, O Source of all existence,
        I have wandered far so that I might see
    The Lord of Athihai Veerattanam <note>original: "Atikai Vīraṭṭānam"—A major Shiva temple in South Arcot where the poet-saint was famously converted and healed.</note>
        And Your pure, flower-like feet.

30. Every day I am sinking into worldly sin;
        I know nothing as I truly should.
    I am as consumed by vice as if it were my own family;
        I can see no path for me to follow.
    O You whose throat is like a dark gemstone <note>original: "maṇikaṇṭā"—A reference to the myth where Shiva’s throat turned blue after he drank poison to save the world.</note>,
        Be gracious and grant me such grace
    That I may see Your beautiful feet,
        O Lord of Athihai Veerattanam.

31. My fickle heart abandons one love
        And immediately clings to another;
    It swings quickly toward one thing
        And just as quickly swings back again.
    O You with the crescent moon in Your hair,
        Lord of Athihai Veerattanam,
    From now on, I lie still at Your feet,
        For You have broken the rope of my soul <note>The "broken cord" signifies the end of the soul's aimless wavering between worldly desires, leading to a total fall into God's mercy.</note>.

<summary>In these verses, the saint Tirunavukkarasu expresses deep personal remorse for his sins and his inability to lead a righteous life through his own will. He uses the powerful metaphor of a snapped swing-rope to describe his final, desperate surrender to Shiva at the temple of Athihai Veerattanam.</summary>

<keywords>Tirunavukkarasu, Appar, sin, repentance, Athihai Veerattanam, Shiva, blue throat, surrender, Tamil Bhakti poetry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the hymns of Tirunavukkarasu Swāmi (Appar), further exploring his themes of spiritual inadequacy and the desperate need for divine intervention. While the previous page established his sense of sin, these verses use vivid metaphors—nautical journeys and the imagery of a trapped animal—to illustrate the soul's peril in the world.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

32. I have not cast off the burning disease of lust; I have not broken the bond <note>original: "pasam"—the spiritual rope or attachment that binds the soul to the material world.</note> of worldly affection. I have not opened the eye of wisdom to gaze upon You and truly understand. I have gathered a cargo of sin <note>original: "vinai"—deeds or karma that accumulate and distance the soul from God.</note> that brings only emptiness. I am ruined and exhausted, O Lord of Adhigai Veerattanam <note>original: "Atikai Vīraṭṭānar"—the deity of the temple where Appar was famously cured of an incurable stomach ailment.</note>.

33. ->I CHERISHED THE BODY IN VAIN<-

    I have nourished this body to no purpose, while harboring evil within my heart. I am like a ladle without a handle <note>A metaphor for someone who is in the presence of God's grace but lacks the spiritual "handle" or capacity to grasp and enjoy it.</note>; I cannot partake of Your goodness. Like a frog trapped in a snake's mouth, I am distracted by many frantic thoughts. Protect me and grant me salvation, O King who dwells in Ottriyur <note>original: "Oṟṟiyūr"—a sacred coastal town near modern Chennai, famous for its ancient Shiva temple.</note>.

34. ->THE SHIPWRECKED SOUL<-

    Taking the boat of the mind, and steering it with the pole of the intellect, I loaded it with the cargo of anger and sailed out upon the crowded sea of life. When the boat strikes the rock of lust and capsizes, grant me the awareness of Your support, O King who dwells in Ottriyur.

> 32. I cannot break the bond of lust;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The fierce torture of desire will not die.
> I cannot wake my soul
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To examine my physical nature with seeing eyes.
> I carry a heavy load of my past deeds <note>original: "deeds"—referring to karma, the accumulated consequences of past actions that bind the soul to the cycle of rebirth.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A burden such as I can never put down.
> Lord of Athihai Veerattanam <note>original: "Athihai Vīraṭṭānam"—one of the eight sacred places where Shiva is said to have performed heroic deeds; specifically, where he destroyed the triple cities of the demons.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I have grown weary of this joyless life.

Fresh pictures in another hymn describe his miserable condition. God’s vessels are full of the sweetness of grace, but his spoon has no handle <note>A metaphor for having the means of salvation nearby but lacking the spiritual capacity or "grip" to utilize them.</note>. He feels himself in the deadly grasp of fate, like a frog in the cruel mouth of a snake which is slowly swallowing it down. Or he is on a raft on the sea of life, wrecked on the rock of lust.

> 33. While violence is in my heart,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Caring for the cage of my body is in vain.
> My spoon has no handle when I
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Am eager to drink the honey of Your grace.
> Like a frog in the serpent’s mouth,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Caught in life’s terrors, I rave wildly.
> You, King of holy Ottriyur <note>original: "Ottiyūr"—an ancient temple town near modern Chennai, famous for the Thyagaraja Temple.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Will You not care for me and save me?

> 34. When I launch out upon life’s angry waves,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My heart is the raft I take for myself,
> My mind is the pole I lean upon,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And I carry a cargo of misery to sea.
> I strike upon the rock of lust!
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O then, even though I am completely witless,
> Grant me such wisdom that I think of You,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;King of holy Ottriyur.

<summary>In these hymns, the saint Appar uses desperate metaphors—a handleless spoon, a frog trapped by a snake, and a shipwrecked raft—to express his spiritual helplessness and his total dependence on God's mercy.</summary>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the hymns of Tirunavukkarasu Swami (Appar). Having previously lamented his spiritual failings using metaphors of shipwrecks and trapped animals, Appar here descends into a profound "dark night of the soul," questioning his very existence before finding a transformative vision of God's grace at the temple of Poonthuruthi.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

### 35. My lineage is low, my character is poor

> My lineage is low, my character is poor, and my standing is worthless.
> My faults are many. Even my appearance is not good;
> I am not good at all, I am no wise man <note>original: "jnani"—one who possesses spiritual wisdom or realization.</note>,
> Nor have I kept company with the virtuous.
> I am like a beast, yet I cannot even say I am purely a beast <note>original: "விலங்கலேன்விலங்கலாதொழிந்தேனல்லேன்"—The poet feels he is in a wretched middle ground: he lacks the innocence of an animal but also lacks the virtue of a realized human being.</note>.
> I am skilled only at speaking of things that are loathsome.
> I am wretched and poor; I can only beg and have nothing to give.
> Why was I even born, a poor creature like me?

### 36. I saw the Holy One at Poonthuruthi

> He made the restless waters stay upon His matted hair <note>A reference to the legend of the Descent of the Ganges, where Shiva caught the falling river Ganga in his hair to break its fall and save the earth from destruction.</note>;
> He made my heart—which never thought of Him—to think of Him;
> He taught me all the things I had not learned;
> He showed me all the things I had not seen;
> He spoke things that had never been spoken, and following me here,
> He took me, a humble servant, as His own.
> The Pure One who cured my evil disease <note>This may refer both to the poet's spiritual malaise and the literal stomach ailment (shulai) that led to his conversion from Jainism back to Shaivism.</note>, the Holy One—
> I saw Him at Poonthuruthi <note>original: "Pūnturutti"—A sacred town and temple site in the Tanjore district of Tamil Nadu.</note>.

<summary>Appar concludes his lamentation of personal unworthiness (Verse 35) and transitions into a joyful testimony of divine intervention, describing how Shiva transformed his ignorance into wisdom at the temple of Poonthuruthi (Verse 36).</summary>

<keywords>Appar, Tevaram, Poonthuruthi, matted hair, Ganga, spiritual conversion, Tamil Shaivism, humility, divine grace</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the hymns of Tirunavukkarasu Swami (also known as Appar). While the previous page introduced his sense of spiritual desperation, this page captures the transition from a "confession of sin" to the "joy of religion" and divine revelation at the temple of Poonthuruthi.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>

<header>TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI</header>

It would be hard to find a more comprehensive confession of sin than this next stanza from another hymn.

> 35. My lineage is evil, all my qualities are evil;
> I am great only in sin; even my good deeds are evil.
> My innermost self is evil; I am foolish, avoiding what is pure.
> I am not a beast, yet I can never abandon the ways of a beast.
> I can preach with strong words, telling men what they should hate,
> Yet I can never give gifts; I only know how to beg for them.
> Oh, what a wretched man I am! Why was I ever born?

---

Next, we provide a series of stanzas in various meters from different hymns, in which the saint expresses in song some of the joy that his religion has brought him. God has revealed mysteries to him that the tongue cannot describe, and He dwells in the innermost parts of the saint's life. To him, God is the legendary *katpaha* tree <note>original: "katpaha tree"—also known as the Kalpavriksha, a divine wish-fulfilling tree in Hindu mythology.</note>, supplying his every need. God is his all in all, and His presence is sweeter than music or evening moonlight.

> 36. He made the flowing water <note>A reference to the river Ganga (Ganges), which Shiva caught in his matted hair to prevent it from flooding the earth.</note> stay still in His hair;
> And He has fixed my thoughtless heart on thoughts of Him alone.
> He taught me what no one can learn, and what no one can see revealed;
> He told me what the tongue cannot express; He pursued me and made me His own.
> The spotless, pure, and holy One healed my terrible disease <note>original: "fell disease"—likely referring to the severe abdominal pain (shulai) that led to Appar's conversion from Jainism back to Saivism.</note>,
> And in Poonthuruthi <note>original: "Pūnturutti"—a sacred site and temple dedicated to Shiva in Tamil Nadu.</note>, He revealed Himself even to me.

<vocab>Tirunavukkarasu Swami, confession, hymn, katpaha tree (wish-fulfilling tree), Poonthuruthi, Saivism</vocab>

<summary>This page features Appar's transition from a profound confession of personal unworthiness to a joyful celebration of God's grace and revelation at Poonthuruthi.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Tirunavukkarasu, confession of sin, divine grace, Ganges, Poonthuruthi, Tamil Saivism, Kalpavriksha</keywords>

> 37. Oh Treasure! My wealth! Oh Honey! Bright light of the heavens! 
> Oh bright Form of noble, radiant light! My kin! My very soul! 
> The core that stands within the heart! 
> My wish-fulfilling tree! <note>original: "katpaha"—The Kalpavriksha, a divine tree in Hindu mythology that grants all desires.</note> My eye! The pupil of my eye! 
> The image that dances within that pupil! 
> Protect me, so that the cruel disease of karma <note>The spiritual burden of past deeds that leads to suffering and rebirth.</note> may never reach me, 
> Oh Lord of the Immortals who dwells in Tiruvavaduthurai!

> 38. You are my Father, You are my Mother, You are my Elder Brother! 
> You are my affectionate uncle and aunt! 
> You are the virtuous wife and precious wealth! 
> You are my entire race and my family, all in one form! 
> You create both the things I enjoy and the path I must follow! 
> You are the companion who helps my heart find renunciation! 
> You are this gold, this gem, this pearl! 
> You are the Sovereign, the Lord who rides the bull! <note>Shiva is traditionally depicted riding Nandi, the sacred bull, symbolizing strength and devotion.</note>

> 39. Like the flawless music of the lute, like the evening moon, 
> Like the blowing southern breeze, like the burgeoning spring, 
> Like a pond where buzzing bees swarm— 
> Such is the shade of the twin feet of the Lord, my Father.

<summary>In these stanzas, Appar describes God as the ultimate source of all earthly and spiritual relationships, concluding with a famous comparison of the peace found at God's feet to the cooling comforts of nature and music.</summary>

40. We are subjects to no one; we do not fear Death <note>original: "Naman"—referring to Yama, the Hindu god of death and justice.</note>.
      We shall not suffer the torments of hell; we live without deception.
    We shall rejoice; we know no disease; we do not bow down to any man.
      It is eternal bliss; for us, there is no more sorrow.
    For we have become the eternal servants of the King,
      Of Shankara <note>original: "Sankaran"—a name for Shiva meaning "the one who grants prosperity or happiness."</note>, who is Himself subject to no one,
    Who wears a white conch-shell earring in His ear;
      We have reached the sanctuary of His two flower-like, crimson feet.

---

41. Even if they gave us the treasure of the Conch and the treasure of the Lotus <note>original: "Sanganidhi" and "Padumanidhi"—two of the nine legendary treasures belonging to Kubera, the god of wealth.</note>,
      And offered us the rule of the earth along with the heavens,
    We would not value the wealth of those who must eventually perish,
      Unless they are devoted to the Great God <note>original: "Mahadeva."</note>.
    But even if they are lepers with limbs rotting and wasting away,
      Or outcasts <note>original: "pulaiyar"—a term for those at the very bottom of the social hierarchy, often tasked with "impure" work.</note> who skin cows and eat the flesh,
    If they are but lovers of Him who hid the Ganges in His matted hair,
      Look! They are the very gods whom we bow down to worship.

<vocab>Appar, Tirunavukkarasar, Tevaram, Saivism, Tamil hymns</vocab>

<summary>In these famous verses, Appar declares his absolute spiritual freedom from earthly kings and the fear of death, while asserting that a humble devotee of Shiva, regardless of their social status or physical health, is worthy of divine worship.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Shiva, Independence, Spiritual Equality, Sanganidhi, Padmanidhi, Devotion, Sovereignty</keywords>

> 40. No man holds power over us,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We fear neither death nor hell;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We feel no trembling or mental grief,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We know no pain or subservience <note>original: "cringings"—referring to the act of bowing or acting out of fear toward earthly rulers.</note>.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Unchanging joy is ours, day after day,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For we belong to Him,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Forever His, who reigns
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In bliss, our <term>Śaṅkara</term> <note>original: "Sankara"—a name for Shiva meaning "the one who grants prosperity or happiness."</note>.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We have come here to His feet,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Feet as fair as freshly-picked flowers;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;See how His divine ears
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Wear an earring and a white conch shell <note>original: "couch-shell"—an OCR error or archaic spelling for the conch shell (shankha) often associated with Hindu divinity.</note>.

> 41. Though they give me the jewels from <term>Indra</term>'s <note>the King of the gods in Hindu mythology.</note>
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;abode,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Though they grant me rule over the earth, and even
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;over heaven,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If they are not the friends of our lord <term>Mahādēv</term> <note>literally "Great God," a title for Shiva.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What do I care for wealth given by such spiritually ruined hands?
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But if they love <term>Śiva</term>, who hides in His hair
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The river <term>Gaṅga</term> <note>the Ganges River, which in myth descended from heaven and was caught in Shiva's hair to soften its impact on earth.</note>, then whoever they may be,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whether they are suffering from leprosy, or are outcasts, or even slaughterers of cattle <note>original: "kine"—an archaic word for cows.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I offer them my respect; they are like gods to me.

Often the Hindu devotee asks and re-asks the fundamental question "Who am I?", reaching a somber conclusion, but contrasting his own illusory life with the ultimate reality of God; worshipping Him brings liberation from the prison-house of individual personality.

<vocab>Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi, Śaṅkara, Indra, Mahādēv, Śiva, Gaṅga, conch-shell, devotee, personality</vocab>

<summary>This page contains two hymns by the Tamil saint Appar (Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi) expressing spiritual independence from earthly rulers and the radical equality of all devotees regardless of social status or health. It concludes with a brief commentary on the Hindu concept of self and liberation through the worship of God.</summary>

# TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI

> 42. Who are your father and mother? Who are those born with you?
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who is your wife? Who are your children? And who, indeed, are you?
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How did you come to be? By what way will you depart?
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;All this is a deceptive illusion; do not find your joy in it.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Listen while I tell your hearts one thing:
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For those who rise and say <term>Namasivaya</term> <note>original: "Namaccivāya"—the 'Five Syllables,' the most sacred mantra of the Śaivite faith, meaning 'Salutations to Shiva.'</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The holy name of my Father, whose head is graced by the crescent moon
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and the playful serpent—
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A place in the great heavens shall be theirs.

> 43. O my head, bow down!
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Bow to the Leader who wears a garland of skulls upon His head,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And who, with a skull as His bowl, seeks alms.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O my head, bow down! <note>The image of Shiva as 'Bhikshatana' or the supreme beggar reflects the ascetic tradition where the god wanders to test the devotion of others.</note>

> 44. O my eyes, look upon Him!
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Look upon the one who swallowed the poison of the sea <note>referring to the myth of the churning of the ocean, where Shiva drank the 'Halahala' poison to save the world, turning his throat blue.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Lord who stands dancing, swinging His eight arms.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O my eyes, look upon Him!

<summary>In these hymns, Appar reflects on the illusory nature of worldly relationships and exhorts the body—specifically the head and the eyes—to devote itself entirely to the worship of Shiva.</summary>

***

Our next hymn with short-lined verses (numbers 43 to 48) is a kind of <term>Śaivite</term> <note>A follower of the god Shiva.</note> consecration hymn. It mentions various parts of the body in succession—head, eyes, ears—which are to be dedicated to the worship of <term>Śiva</term>. Verse 46 must sound sorrowful to a Śaivite, for it is frequently sung into the ears of the dying as a mournful appeal to think of God. Verse 47 rises far above the common ideas of future absorption <note>original: "absorption"—the philosophical concept of the soul merging into the divine until its individual identity is lost.</note> into the divine, moving instead toward the thought of a blissful state of communion and the eternal praise of God.

->43. My head, bow down to Him,<-
->The true Leader, garlanded with skulls;<-
->A skull was His strange begging-bowl;<-
->Bow low to Him, my head.<-

->44. My eyes, gaze upon Him<-
->Who drank the poison <note>original: "bane"—refers to the 'Halahala' poison produced during the churning of the ocean, which Shiva drank to save all living beings, turning his throat blue.</note> of the dark sea.<-
->He swings eight arms in His dance;<-
->Keep your gaze fixed on Him.<-

<summary>In these verses, the saint Appar reflects on the illusory nature of family and self, urging devotees to focus on Shiva. He begins a "consecration hymn" that ritualistically dedicates the physical body—the head and the eyes—to the service and contemplation of the Lord.</summary>

# TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI

45. My ears, listen to His praise,
->To Shiva, our radiant King.<-
His form is as radiant as red coral:
->Ears, listen to the songs of praise that men sing.<-

->When life departs from us?<-
Who but the dancing Lord of Kuttalam <note>original: "Kuṭṭālam"—a sacred town in Tamil Nadu (modern Courtallam) famous for its waterfalls and its temple where Shiva is worshipped as the "Dancer."</note>
->Can then be our true kinsman?<-

47. How proud I will be there,
->As one of His heavenly host,<-
At the beautiful feet of Him who holds the deer, <note>original: "holds the deer"—Shiva is traditionally depicted holding a young deer, which symbolizes His mastery over the restless mind and the natural world.</note>
->How proud will be my boast!<-

48. I sought Him and I found Him.
->Brahma <note>original: "Brahm"—the creator god.</note> searched in vain high above.<-
Vishnu delved in vain underground. <note>This refers to the myth where Brahma and Vishnu tried to find the beginning and end of Shiva when He appeared as an infinite pillar of fire; neither could find His limit.</note>
->I found Him within my own soul.<-

---

A mystic can never be satisfied with mere religious rituals. These Shaivite devotees often praise the God of a particular shrine using language that might suggest Shiva is only to be found in that specific place. Anyone familiar with India remembers the endless streams of pilgrims traveling to the Ganges or the Cauvery (the Tamil *Kāviri*), to Rameswaram, Cape Comorin, or hundreds of other holy places. Yet, with a beautiful inconsistency, these ancient poets sometimes guide people away from external rituals toward an inner, spiritual worship; notice this in the following hymn. Regarding the terms used in verse 50: the *Vedas* <note>original: "Vedas"—the oldest and most authoritative scriptures of Hinduism.</note> are the religious works of the highest authority; *Shastras* <note>original: "Śāstras"—treatises on philosophy, law, or science based on the Vedas.</note> are philosophical and practical works based on them; and *Vedangas* <note>original: "Vēdāngas"—six auxiliary disciplines or 'limbs' of Vedic study, including phonetics, ritual, and grammar.</note> are the six sciences subordinate to the Vedas.

<vocab>Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi, Shiva, Kuttalam, Brahma, Vishnu, Shaivite, Ganges, Cauvery, Kāviri, Rameswaram, Cape Comorin, Vedas, Shastras, Vedangas</vocab>

<summary>This page features verses by Appar emphasizing that God is found within the soul rather than through external searches, followed by an explanation of the Shaivite transition from ritual pilgrimage to internal mysticism.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Shiva, Kuttalam, internal worship, mysticism, pilgrimage, Vedas, Brahma, Vishnu, devotional poetry</keywords>

These verses (49–52) emphasize that external acts of piety—such as pilgrimages, scriptural study, and asceticism—are meaningless without internal devotion to the Lord.</meta>

<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

# TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI

49. What use is bathing in the Ganges <note>original: "Gangai"—the sacred river of northern India.</note> or the Cauvery? <note>original: "Kāviri"—the sacred river of southern India.</note>
->What use is bathing in the cool waters of Cape Comorin?<-
What use is bathing in the roaring waters of the great ocean,
->If one does not remember the Lord <note>original: "Īsan"—a title for Shiva meaning "The Lord" or "The Ruler."</note> who is everywhere?<-

50. What use is reciting the Vedas <note>original: "Veda"—the most ancient and sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</note> or hearing the Shastras? <note>original: "Shāstra"—traditional texts of law, philosophy, or science.</note>
->What use is daily practicing many books of moral law?<-
What use is knowing and understanding the six parts of the Vedas, <note>original: "Angam or-ārum"—referring to the Vedangas, six auxiliary disciplines used to understand and apply the Vedas.</note>
->If one does not cherish the Lord within?<-

51. What use is wandering through forest lands?
->What use is performing penance without any flaw?<-
What use is giving up meat and looking toward the heavens,
->If He does not belong to those who call Him the Wise One?<-

52. What use is strict fasting or religious vows?
->What use is performing penance upon a hilltop?<-
What use is wandering about and bathing in the waters,
->If He is not for those who always call Him the Lord?<-

<summary>In these verses, the saint Appar argues that external religious practices like pilgrimages to sacred rivers, scriptural study, and ascetic self-denial are spiritually hollow unless accompanied by a sincere, internal devotion to Shiva.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Tevaram, Shiva, Ritual vs. Devotion, Ganges, Cauvery, Vedas, Shastras, Penance, Pilgrimage, Tamil Saivism</keywords>

While the previous page critiqued the futility of empty rituals, this page provides a poetic list of specific religious acts—such as bathing in sacred rivers, studying complex scriptures, and practicing extreme asceticism—contrasting them with the only act that leads to salvation: constant devotion to the Lord.</meta>

49. Why bathe in the waters of the Ganges <note>original: "Gaṅga"</note> or the Kaveri? <note>original: "Kāviri"—the most sacred river in Southern India.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why go to Cape Comorin <note>The southernmost tip of India.</note> in the land of Kongu? <note>original: "Koṅgu"—a historical region in Tamil Nadu.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why seek the waters of the roaring sea?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Salvation <note>original: "Release"—the spiritual liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</note> belongs to them, and them alone, who call
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In every place upon the Lord of all.

50. Why chant the Vedas, or listen to the wisdom of the Shastras? <note>original: "Vēdas" and "Śāstras"—the primary sacred scriptures and philosophical texts of Hinduism.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why teach the books of righteousness every day?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why recite the six Vedangas <note>original: "Vēdāṅgas"—six auxiliary disciplines used to understand and apply the Vedas correctly.</note> over and over?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Salvation belongs to them, and them alone, whose hearts
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Will never stop thinking of their Lord.

51. Why roam the jungle, or wander through the cities?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why burden your life with unrelenting and harsh penance?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why abstain from eating meat and gaze into the heavens? <note>original: "the blue"</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Salvation belongs to them, and them alone, who cry
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Unceasingly to the high Lord of wisdom.

52. Why fast and starve? Why suffer through austere pains?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why climb the mountains to perform harsh penance?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Why go to bathe in waters far and near?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Salvation belongs to them, and them alone, who call
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At all times upon the Lord of all.

---

It might seem like a sudden change in tone when the same writer, in our next hymn, appears to say that everything depends on the pronunciation of the five sacred syllables <note>The *Panchakshara Mantra*: "Na-Ma-Shi-Va-Ya," meaning "Adoration to Shiva."</note>, which can be translated as "Hail, Shiva!" <note>original: "Śiva"</note>. In the later development of <term>Shaivism</term> <note>original: "Śaivism"—the branch of Hinduism that worships Shiva as the Supreme Being.</note>, the pronunciation of these syllables was elevated to a primary religious duty. However, during the formative period in which these hymns were written, the name likely represented the person of the deity himself. Therefore, what we have here is a religious "calling upon the name of..."

<summary>In these four verses, the saint Appar argues that external religious acts like pilgrimages, scriptural study, and asceticism are worthless without sincere, internal devotion to Shiva. The author notes that Appar’s focus on "calling upon the name" of God refers to a personal relationship rather than the mere mechanical repetition of mantras.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Shaivism, devotion vs. ritual, Ganges, Kaveri, Vedas, Shastras, penance, salvation, Five Sacred Syllables, Shiva</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the hymns of the saint Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi (Appar). While the previous page focused on the insufficiency of outward rituals and scholastic learning, these verses celebrate the supreme protection and beauty found in the sacred five-syllable mantra "Namah Sivaya."</meta>

53. The Lord of the Vedas <note>original: "Vēdiyan"—referring to Shiva as the source and master of sacred knowledge.</note> is the companion of my speech; he is the radiant inhabitant of the heavens. Even if someone were to tie me to a stone pillar and cast me into the depths of the sea, my constant and saving companion would still be the name: *Namah Sivaya* <note>original: "Hail to Shiva"—the most sacred five-syllable mantra in Saivism. Tradition says that when Appar was persecuted by a king and thrown into the sea tied to a stone, he floated to safety while chanting this verse.</note>.

54. The most precious ornament for all flowers is the blooming lotus.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The most precious ritual for a cow is to provide offerings for anointing the Lord Hara <note>A name for Shiva meaning "The Destroyer of Evil."</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The most precious quality for a King is a heart free from injustice.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But the most precious ornament for the tongue is the name: *Namah Sivaya*.

55. Sacred ash <note>original: "Vibuthi"—white ash smeared on the body by devotees to represent the burning away of ego and the permanence of the soul compared to the transient body.</note> is the precious jewel for all who take religious vows.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the priests, the precious jewels are the hidden Vedas and their six branches of study.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For the crescent moon, its crowning jewel is the radiant, high head of our Lord <note>Shiva is traditionally depicted wearing the crescent moon in his matted hair.</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For us, the only precious jewel is the name: *Namah Sivaya*.

<summary>This page contains three influential verses by Saint Appar emphasizing that the mantra 'Namah Sivaya' is the ultimate protection, beauty, and religious fulfillment, surpassing even the most dire physical threats or formal scholarly achievements.</summary>

the Lord" in the devotion of worship. In the first stanza, there is a remarkable use of the term "Word." Modern Saivites identify this "Word" with Umā, Shiva's consort. We can compare this to the Sanskrit *Vāk* <note>original: "Vāk"—meaning "speech" or "voice," often personified as a goddess in ancient Indian scripture.</note> (Word) in the Rig Veda.

The last lines of verse 53 are connected in the minds of Saivites with a story that Appar Swami was actually sunk in the sea by Jain persecutors with two great stones tied to him; however, upon crying "Hail, Shiva!" he floated to the surface.

The five products of the cow referred to in verse 54 are all used together in ceremonial purification—milk, curds, ghee <note>Clarified butter.</note>, urine, and dung <note>Known as *Panchagavya*, these five substances are considered sacred and purifying in Hindu ritual.</note>.

> 53. O Lord of Scripture, whom the Word helps,
> Heavenly light of heaven—if I only praise
> Your golden feet with hands properly raised,
> Then even if men tie two heavy stones to me
> And sink me in the ocean's depths, even then
> The cry "Hail, Shiva," would be my salvation.

> 54. The lotus is the glory of all flowers;
> The glory of all cattle <note>original: "kine"—an archaic word for cows.</note> is Hara's <note>A name for Shiva meaning "The Destroyer."</note> use
> Of what they produce. The glory of kings
> Is the unswerving justice of their deeds.
> But if we ask what the glory of the tongue is,
> It is to cry out aloud, "Hail, Shiva, hail."

> 55. For those who renounce everything, it is true glory
> To wear the sacred ash <note>White ash smeared on the body to symbolize the burning away of the ego.</note>. For pure Brahmans <note>The priestly class in Hindu society.</note>,
> The Vedas and Vedangas <note>The Vedas are the primary scriptures of Hinduism; the Vedangas are the six "limbs" or auxiliary disciplines used to understand and chant them.</note> are their pride.
> The white moon's glory is to shine serenely
> On the long locks of Shiva, while for *us*,
> True glory is to cry "Hail, Shiva, hail."

<meta>This page continues the hymns of the saint Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi (Appar). These verses mark pivotal moments in his life: his conversion back to Shaivism through the healing of a painful ailment, and his mystical vision at Tiruvaiyaaru where he saw the divine couple, Shiva and Parvati, manifested in the world's creatures.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

### The Prayer for Healing at Atikai

> 56. O Lord who dwells in Veeratṭānam <note>One of the eight "Veeratṭānam" temples where Shiva performed heroic deeds.</note> at Atikai on the banks of the Gedilam river! You do not banish this pain, which attacks me like the God of Death. I am not aware of having done many evils, yet day and night without ceasing, I worship at your fitting feet. Hidden within my belly, my bowels are being twisted and knotted; I, your humble servant, can no longer endure it.
<note>This hymn is traditionally considered the first one sung by Appar. Legend says he had left Shaivism to become a Jain monk, but was struck with a severe stomach ailment (*shulai* or colic) that his Jain peers could not cure. Upon returning to the Shiva temple at Atikai, his sister Tilakavatiyar encouraged him to pray, and he was miraculously healed after singing this verse.</note>

### The Vision at Tiruvaiyaaru

> 57. Singing of the One who wears the beautiful crescent moon, together with the Daughter of the Mountain <note>original: "Malaiyān makaḷ"—referring to the goddess Parvati, daughter of the King of the Mountains.</note>, I followed those who enter the temple to offer praise, carrying flowers and water. As I reached the shrine of Tiruvaiyaaru <note>A famous temple town near Thanjavur where the five rivers meet.</note> without a trace of weariness, I saw a bull elephant walking with its loving mate. In them, I saw the Lord’s holy feet; I saw wonders I had never seen before.
<note>In this famous "Tiruvaiyaaru" pathigam (decad), the saint describes a mystical experience where he saw Shiva and Parvati everywhere in the natural world, specifically in the form of pairs of animals. Here, the bull and female elephant represent the divine masculine and feminine principles.</note>

<summary>This page contains two of Appar's most significant hymns: his desperate prayer for relief from a painful stomach ailment at Atikai, and his joyful vision of the divine manifest in nature at Tiruvaiyaaru.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi, Atikai, Veeratṭānam, Tiruvaiyaaru, Shiva, Parvati, healing, nature mysticism, Tamil Shaivism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the life and hymns of the saint Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi, specifically detailing the traditional context behind his prayers for healing and his famous mystical vision at Tiruvaiyaru.</meta>

Tradition connects our next stanza with a story of Appar Swami <note>original: "Apparswāmi"</note> being struck with an internal illness when he abandoned Shaivism and became a Jain. The legend says the pain proved to be a convincing argument that converted him back, at which point he was promptly cured. However, evidence within the text suggests this hymn was composed long after his return to Shaivism. Nandi is the name of Shiva’s bull.

> 56. You do not take my deadly pain away;
>      My torments, rider of Nandi <note>Shiva is traditionally depicted riding the bull, Nandi.</note>, never cease.
>   At your feet I would worship night and day,
>      But since my insides twist and never find peace,
>         I can do no more! O Father, to you I cry,
>         Who dwells by the Gedilam <note>original: "Keḍila"</note> river, in Atikai <note>original: "Athihai"</note>.

---

Nature sometimes spoke to our author of God. To him, the union of male and female, even among animals, one day served as a revelation of divine truths.

> 57. I will follow those who, going to the shrine, sing their praises,
>         With flowers and water for the God who wears the gentle moon
>   So lovely in his hair, a garland wreathed around his neck;
>   And with him they sing of Parvati, the mountain god's fair child.
>      Once as I went to Tiruvaiyaru <note>original: "Aiyāṛu"</note>, with a light and reverent step,
>      I saw two young elephants come—a male led by his beloved mate—
>   And in that sight I saw the Lord's foot; I saw unspoken secrets.

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 58. O Trustworthy One, our King, our Lord, the Primal Being,
>      You who share your form with your Consort <note>original: "pangan"; refers to Shiva’s form as Ardhanarishvara, half-male and half-female</note>, the Supreme Yogi—thus I have praised you daily.
>   Like pure red gold, like a mountain of coral, I have wandered as your devotee,
>      Seeking to see your radiant, flower-like feet,
>      O Lord of Atikai Veerattanam <note>original: "Athihai Viraṭṭanār"; the temple where Appar's sister lived and where he returned to the Shaivite faith</note>.

---

> 59. O Partner of her whose speech is like music!
>      O Lord of Maraikkadu <note>modern: Vedaranyam; "Maraikkadu" means "Forest of the Vedas"</note>, whom the people of this earth circle in worship!
>   So that we may see you with our own eyes,
>      In your mercy, open the door wide <note>This hymn refers to a legend where the temple doors had been shut by the Vedas themselves and could only be opened by a sincere song of devotion</note>.

<summary>These hymns illustrate Appar’s deep devotion and the legendary power of his poetry, including his praise at Atikai and his miraculous opening of the temple doors at Maraikkadu.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi, Atikai Veerattanam, Maraikkadu, Vedaranyam, Shiva, Parvati, temple miracles, Tamil Saivism</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the hymns of the saint Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi (Appar), specifically focusing on his devotion to Shiva as a mystical yogi and his famous miracle at the temple of Vedaranyam.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI</header>

God is the great yogi <note>original: "yogi"; a master of spiritual disciplines and supernatural powers</note>, the master of mystical powers.

> 58. “O greatly loved, our King, our Lord from all eternity,
> Our portion <note>meaning: our shared inheritance or destiny</note>, our true mystic,” thus I sing from day to day.
> O Golden One, O mountain of coral, out of love for You
> I have wandered far and wide, O King of Athihai Veerattanam <note>original: "Athihai Vīraṭṭānam"; one of the eight sacred sites where Shiva is said to have performed heroic deeds</note>;
> I have wandered far just to see the shining blossom of Your foot.

---

One entire hymn, from which our next verse is taken, is a prayer for the opening of a door. Tradition holds that the great locked temple door at Vedaranyam <note>original: "Vedāraṇyam"; a coastal pilgrimage site</note> swung open in response to this song.

> 59. Uma <note>original: "Umā"; the goddess Parvati, Shiva's consort</note> is Your partner <note>original: "portion"; referring to the half-male, half-female form of Shiva</note>, she whose words are like song.
> In beautiful Maraikkadu <note>original: "Maṛaikkāḍu"; the Tamil name for Vedaranyam, meaning "Forest of the Vedas"</note>, people circle around You
> In worship. O graciously open this door,
> So that we, Your true servants, may see Your glory.

---

The following is a very popular stanza. There used to be a beggar in Madras <note>modern: Chennai</note> who recited it—and it alone—all day long.

60. He is the Rare One, dwelling in the minds of the priests <note>original: "andaṇar"; refers to Brahmins or those of spiritual character</note>. He is the essence within the sacred Vedas. He is the atom, the Truth whom no one can fully know. He is honey, He is milk, and He is the shining light. He is the King of the gods. He is the Dark One <note>referring to Vishnu</note>, the Four-faced One <note>referring to Brahma</note>, the fire, the wind, the deep sea, and the mountain ranges. He is the Great One who has made His home in Perumpatrapuliyur <note>original: "Perumpatrapuliyūr"; an ancient name for Chidambaram, the site of the famous temple of the Dancing Shiva</note>. Every day that we do not spend speaking of Him is a day on which we have not truly lived <note>original: "pira-vā-nāḷ"; literally "unborn days," suggesting that life without devotion is a waste of existence</note>.

---

61. O You who wear the skin of the flayed deer as Your garment! O Lord of the goddess Uma! O Bull among the immortals! O You with the dark throat who swallowed the poison of the churning sea! O Lord of Mount Kailash! It is Your duty, O Great One, to forgive the faults of those who love You. Is Your great grace not always with You? O Bull among the gods, You who dwell in Tiruvavaduthurai <note>original: "Tiruvāvaduthurai"; a sacred town in Tamil Nadu famous for its ancient Shiva temple and its connection to the saint Tirumular</note>, where the great peacocks dance in the groves!

<summary>This page contains two hymns by Appar: the first emphasizes that a life not dedicated to praising Shiva is a wasted existence, while the second is a plea for divine forgiveness, highlighting Shiva's role as a compassionate protector.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the collection of hymns by the saint Appar (Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi). It focuses on the omnipresence of God within both nature and the soul, and concludes with a prayer for forgiveness based on the inherent grace of the Divine.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>

> 60. He is always difficult to find, yet He lives in the thoughts of the virtuous;
> He is the deepest secret within the Scriptures <note>original: "Scripture"; likely referring to the Vedas</note>, impossible to fathom or fully know;
> He is honey, He is milk, and He is the shining light. He is the king of the gods <note>original: "Devas"; celestial beings</note>,
> Present within Vishnu, within Brahma, in the flame, and in the wind,
> Yes, in the mighty, roaring sea and in the mountains.
> He is the Great One who chose Perumpattapuliyur <note>original: "Perumpattapuliyūr"; an ancient name for the temple city of Chidambaram</note> as His own.
> If there are days when my tongue is silent and does not speak of Him,
> Let those days not even be counted in the record of my life.

---

Regardless of what karma <note>the law of cause and effect where one’s actions determine their future destiny</note> may teach about the unavoidable consequences of wrongdoing, devotees believe they can rely on receiving divine forgiveness. For them, the gracious nature of God is a sufficient promise and guarantee.

> 61. Your throat drank the poison of the dark sea as if it were sweet nectar <note>original: "ambrosia"; refers to the myth of Shiva drinking the halahala poison to save the universe during the churning of the ocean</note>,
> O wearer of the deer-skin, Lord of the goddess Uma, King of the gods on high;
> Mount Kailash is Your home, and when those who love You cry out,
> "Forgive our sin," O Great One, it is Your proper duty to forgive;
> For great grace resides with You, Lord of all celestial beings,
> You who live in Avaduthurai <note>original: "Āvaḍutuṟai"; a sacred site in Tamil Nadu</note>, where peacocks dance and call.

<sig>5</sig>

The verses emphasize that a life without devotion is a life wasted and that God's grace outweighs the strict laws of karma.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the hymns of the saint Appar (Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi). The previous page explored God’s presence in the elements and the theme of divine forgiveness. This page shifts focus to the necessity of outward signs of devotion and the vanity of a life lived without spiritual purpose.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

<margin>62</margin>The town that has no temple, the town where people do not wear the holy ash <note>original: "திருவெண்ணீறு" (Tiruvenneeru); sacred white ash or vibhuti worn by devotees of Shiva</note>, the town that does not sing songs of intense devotion, the town that lacks many well-ordered shrines, the town where the white conch shell is not blown with joy, the town without canopies and white flags, the town where people do not pluck fresh buds and flowers for worship before they eat—none of these are truly towns; they are merely wild jungles.

<margin>63</margin>Those who do not chant the sacred Five Letters <note>original: "திருநாமமஞ்செழுத்து" (Tirunāmam añceḻuttu); refers to the mantra 'Na-Ma-Śi-Vā-Ya'</note>, those who never speak of the nature of the Fire-colored One <note>original: "தீவண்ணர்" (Tīvaṇṇar); an epithet for Shiva, who is often depicted as or associated with fire</note>, those who never walk around the holy temple, those who do not pluck flowers as an offering before they eat, those who do not wear holy ash to heal their deep-seated illnesses—if we ask why such pitiless people were ever born, they seem born only to die again, suffering under the weight of great diseases, making a trade of dying and being born again.

<margin>64</margin>Entangled in the struggle to provide for my children and the wife I married, I have not lived according to the Lord’s way, nor have I understood the path of penance. Even though I have seen life appear and then vanish just like a bubble on the water, what was the purpose of my birth, O people, if not to cherish the Supreme One?

<summary>Appar emphasizes that a community or individual life without active devotion—such as visiting temples, wearing holy ash, and chanting the Five Letters—is spiritually empty and trapped in the cycle of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Appar, Tevaram, holy ash, Five Letters, Panchakshara, cycle of rebirth, temple worship, Saivism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the hymns of the saint Appar (Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi), focusing on the spiritual necessity of religious devotion and the consequences of a life lived without it. The previous page described the emptiness of a town without a temple; this page expands on that theme, emphasizing the specific rituals and the personal struggle of balancing worldly duties with divine devotion.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>TIRUNĀVUKKARASU SWĀMI</header>

The terrible fate awaiting those who are not religious is that of being slowly killed by painful sickness, then being born again into a joyless life that cycles back to death in never-ending, dreary loops.

The "five letters" in hymn number 63 refer to the five-syllable mantra <term>Na-mah-Shi-va-ya</term> <note>original: "na-mah-Śi-vā-ya"; a sacred Sanskrit mantra meaning "Adoration to Shiva"</note>, which is praised in verses 53 through 55.

62.
> The cursed town that has no house of God,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Where the sacred white ash does not glow on any man's forehead,
> The town where devout songs of praise are never sung,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Where there are no roadside shrines to show people's faith,
> Where no one joyfully blows the white conch shell,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Where no decorative canopies or flags are seen,
> Where no one makes offerings of flowers before they eat—
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Do not call it a town; it is nothing but a gloomy jungle.

63.
> If people do not speak His name using the five letters,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor ever repeat the praise of Shiva, who is formed of fire,
> And never walk in reverence around His shrine,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And pluck no flowers for an offering before they eat,
> If they wear no sacred ash for healing,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I will tell you why such people were born:
> It was so that horrible diseases might torture them to death,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And then death would bring endless, miserable rebirths.

This final fragment from Appar Swami is written in a somber, melancholic tone, similar to many of his other refrains. Contrary to popular tradition, these lines suggest that Appar may have once been a married man.

64.
> Deeply focused on the painful task
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of caring for my child and wedded wife,
> There is no room in me to feel
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Your power, Lord of my life.
> Oh, why was I even born?
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To care for this false world?
> Or to watch it appear like a bubble,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then be tossed into nothingness?

<vocab>Tirunāvukkarasu Swāmi, na-mah-Śi-vā-ya, Śiva, Apparswāmi, sacred ash, rebirths</vocab>

<summary>Appar Swami warns of the spiritual desolation and the cycle of suffering that await those who neglect devotion to Shiva, while also reflecting on how his own family life once distracted him from the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Appar Swami, Shiva, Sacred Ash (Vibhuti), Panchakshara Mantra, Five Letters, Rebirth, Devotion, Religious Rituals</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the devotional hymns of the saint Appar to a historical and sociological analysis of the rise of Saivism in South India. It discusses the shifting loyalties of the Brahmin class from Jainism to the worship of Shiva and the mythological significance of the Linga.</meta>

What was internal to the earlier spiritual practitioner (<term>Sadhaka</term> <note>original: "Sadhaka"; a person following a spiritual discipline or practice</note>) has become manifest in this Seer. In him, social life has taken a human form. Things are shifting; he is in transition. Shiva worship is on the rise, while Jainism is in decline. Society was, of course, being reshaped at its very roots. A group of Brahmin landowners (<term>Zamindars</term> <note>original: "Zamindar"; historically, an aristocrat or landlord who held a large estate</note>) who were formerly Jain began to shift their loyalty toward the regional kings (<term>Samanta</term> <note>original: "Samanta"; a title for a vassal king or subordinate ruler</note>). This shift led to the rise of Saivism.

We must consider what Saivism was and what role it played in society. This involved the belief that Shiva emerged from the <term>Linga</term> <note>original: "Linga"; an abstract or aniconic representation of Shiva, often depicted as a pillar-like symbol of divine energy</note>. He was not a man, but appeared in the form of a Linga; it was like a pillar whose beginning or end could not be discovered by the gods Vishnu and Brahma. They could not succeed in finding its limits. How did this impact society? The worship of the Linga form of Shiva helped the Brahmin class become dominant in society.

The ideals that emerged were designed to create a scapegoat. In this context, Shiva is not merely about devotion (<term>Bhakti</term> <note>original: "Bhakti"; religious devotion or love for a personal god</note>) or spiritual liberation (<term>Mukti</term> <note>original: "Mukti"; release from the cycle of rebirth</note>), but a form of binding commitment in both the present world and the hereafter <gloss>in this</gloss> world.

<summary>This text explores the sociological shift in South India from Jainism to Saivism, highlighting how the Brahmin class and regional kings adopted Shiva worship, centered on the symbol of the Linga.</summary>
<keywords>Saivism, Jainism, Brahmins, Zamindar, Samanta, Linga, Vishnu, Brahma, Bhakti, Mukti</keywords>

# SUNDARAMURTI SWAMI <note>Sundaramurti (meaning "of beautiful form") was an 8th-century poet-saint. He is one of the four primary teachers (Samayacharyas) of the Tamil Saiva tradition.</note>
### AND HIS
## HYMNS

<keywords>Sundaramurti Swami, Saivite saints, Tamil hymns, Shaivism, Hindu devotional poetry</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page introduces Sundaramūrti Swāmī, the third of the major Tamil Śaivite hymn-writers, following the biographies of Sambandar and Appar.</meta>

## SUNDARAMŪRTI SWĀMI
### (Abbreviated as SUNDARAR)

The third of these hymn-writers, whose full name was Sundaramūrti Swāmī, was a <term>Brahmin</term> <note>original: "Brahman"; the highest-ranking social group in the traditional Hindu caste system, typically serving as priests and scholars</note>, just like Sambandar. He was born in the South Arcot District and is generally believed to have been active during the first quarter of the ninth century AD. He evidently did not strictly follow caste restrictions, as neither of his two wives was a Brahmin. One wife was a temple dancer <note>original: "dancing-girl"; likely referring to the *Devadasi* tradition of women dedicated to temple service and the arts</note> in the Śaivite temple at Tiruvārūr (modern Tiruvalur in the Tanjore District). His other wife was a woman of the <term>Vēļāļa</term> caste <note>an influential community of landowners and agriculturists in South India</note> from Tiruvottiyūr, which is now a suburb of Chennai <note>original: "Madras"</note>.

His life does not seem to have been any happier than polygamous lives usually are; to add to his difficulties, he sometimes found himself without enough food to provide for his wives. He was quite frank in praising God for the material things he received. On the whole, his hymns are considered to be on a lower spiritual level than those of the first two writers, though some of them show signs of genuine spiritual experience. Among the sixty-three <term>Nayanars</term> <note>the sixty-three saints held in special honor by Śaivites</note>, Sundarar seems to have been the last, as he wrote hymns praising the other sixty-two.

<summary>This page provides a biographical overview of Sundarar, the third great Śaivite poet, noting his Brahmin background, his unconventional marriages, and his role in honoring the other sixty-two Śaivite saints.</summary>
<keywords>Sundaramūrti Swāmī, Sundarar, Śaivite saints, Nayanars, South Arcot, Tiruvārūr, caste, Brahmin, Vēļāļa, Tamil hymns</keywords>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>SUNDARAMŪRTI SWĀMI</header>

Sundarar, as our first sample of his work shows, was not only later than the two authors we have been studying (Sambandar and Appar); he was also the last of the sixty-three canonized saints of Śaivism <note>The "sixty-three" (Arupathu-moovar) are the primary saints of the Tamil Śaivite tradition whose lives are chronicled in the Periya Puranam.</note>. A significant weakness of the religion reveals itself here: Śiva has his favorites, who can do no wrong. The following stanza is provided in prose, as these names cannot fit into any English metrical line. The first two will be recognized as the names of the poets whose work we have been considering. Nāḷaippōvān is Nandan, the pariah <note>historical term for a member of the Dalit community; Nandanar is the only Dalit saint among the sixty-three</note> saint. Silandi (meaning "spider") is Kōchchengạt Chola, a king who figures prominently in early Tamil history <note>According to legend, a spider who worshipped a Shiva lingam was reborn as this Chola king.</note>.

65. Nānasambandar and Tirunāvukkarasar, skilled in the Tamil tongue, Nāḷaippōvān, learned Sūdan, Sākkiyan, Silandi, Kannappan, Kanampullan—these may do wrong, and yet You count it as right. Therefore, I have come to the sounding anklets of Your feet, O Lord of Tiruppungūr, with its pools where many a golden lotus gem blossoms.

---

To English ears, the rhythm of the next two verses—which are common favorites—has a distinct sound. It is a close reproduction of the Tamil meter, so close that the tune of the original Tamil hymn could be sung to these English words.

66. Golden are You in Your form, girt around with the fierce tiger’s skin,
Fair shines Your tangled hair, crowned with blossoms from the *kondai’s* <note>original: "koṉṟai"; the Golden Shower tree (Cassia fistula), which produces bright yellow flowers sacred to Shiva</note> bright tree,
Sovereign, great jewel are You, the red ruby of Maḷapādi,
Mother, on You, none but You, can my heart evermore be fixed.

<vocab>Sundarar, Śaivism, Śiva, Nāḷaippōvān, Nandan, Silandi, Kōchchengạt Chola, Tamil, Nānasambandar, Tirunāvukkarasar, Sūdan, Sākkiyan, Kannappan, Kanampullan, Tiruppungūr, kondai, Maḷapādi</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Sundarar as the final of the sixty-three Śaivite saints and presents two hymns illustrating his belief that Śiva overlooks the faults of his devoted followers.</summary>
<keywords>Sundarar, sixty-three saints, Śaivism, Nandanar, Tiruppungūr, Maḷapādi, Tamil hymns, divine grace</keywords>

67. Wearing a simple loincloth and the sacred ash <note>original: *thiru-neeru*; white ash made from burnt cow dung, signifying purity and the soul's detachment from the material world</note> smeared upon Your body, I have come to reach Your feet. O Master, accept me! O Jewel of Mazhapadi <note>A sacred temple town on the banks of the Kollidam River</note>, whose consort has eyes as bright as swords—whom else can I think of but You?

68. I have been a sinner, casting aside devotion and the life of a servant. Now I have learned the true nature of this body, burdened as it is with disease. I will go and worship Him! O Pearl, O great Gem, O Diamond! How many more days can a fool like me live apart from the Lord of Arur <note>Modern-day Tiruvarur, one of the most significant sites in the Śaivite tradition</note>?

69. O Madman <note>original: *Pithā*; a reference to the legend where Sundarar called an old man—actually Śiva in disguise—a "madman" for claiming Sundarar was his slave. Śiva later commanded him to begin his first hymn with this very word.</note> with the crescent moon in Your hair! O Great Lord, Giver of Grace! I remember You in my heart, never forgetting. O Father of the Arutturai temple in Vennainallur, south of the Pennai river—having once become Your servant, can I ever deny it now?

<meta>This page continues the translations of hymns by Sundaramūrti Swāmi (also known as Sundarar). It explores the saint's intense personal devotion and his travels between sacred shrines, specifically focusing on his deep connection to the Lord of Ārūr and the famous "Madman" hymn that marked the beginning of his spiritual calling.</meta>

# SUNDARAMŪRTI SWĀMI

> 67. Dressed in a simple loincloth, my body white with holy ash,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Look, I have come to Your feet; O my Lord <note>original: "head" — used here as a title for a leader or master</note>, I beg You, accept me.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Partner of sword-eyed Umā <note>Umā is the consort of Śiva; the phrase "portion of" refers to the Ardhanarishvara form where Śiva and Umā are joined as one body</note>, You red ruby of Maḻapāḍi,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Friend, my heart can be fixed on You, and none but You, forevermore.

Is the Śiva revealed at one shrine so distinct from the Śiva revealed at another that it threatens the unity of God? If tradition is correct, the danger is very real; Sundarar was already worshipping at one shrine, Tiruvottiyūr, when he remembered the Lord of Ārūr. Deciding to return to Him like a returning prodigal son, he sang this stanza:

> 68. Ah, I have been sinful! I have left the path of pure love and service!
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now I understand well the meaning of my sickness and my pain.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I will go and worship Him. Fool! How much longer can I stay so far away
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From Him—my pearl, my rare diamond, the King of great Ārūr?

---

The joy in God which shines in our next hymn clearly rests on some experience of divine grace that we would have liked to hear described more specifically.

> 69. O madman with the moon-crowned hair <note>According to legend, Śiva appeared as an old man to stop Sundarar's wedding, claiming the saint was his bondslave. Sundarar called him a "madman" before realizing the old man's divine identity; Śiva then commanded him to use that "insult" as the opening of his first hymn.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You Lord of men, You fountain of grace,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How could I bear to forget You?
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My soul always has a place for You.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In "Grace's Shrine" <note>original: *Arutturai*</note> at Veṇṇey-nallūr,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;South of the stream of Peṇṇai, there
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My Father, I became entirely Yours;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How could I now deny my own word?

<vocab>Sundaramūrti Swāmi, Śiva, Umā, Maḻapāḍi, Tiruvottiyūr, Ārūr, Veṇṇey-nallūr, Peṇṇai, hymn, divine grace</vocab>

<summary>This page presents three hymns by Sundarar that emphasize his personal bond with Śiva, including his repentance for neglecting the Lord of Ārūr and his famous "Madman" hymn composed at Veṇṇey-nallūr.</summary>
<keywords>Sundarar, Śaivism, Tevaram, Arur, Vennainallur, divine grace, repentance, Ardhanarishvara</keywords>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 70. For many days I, who am like a dog <note>original: *Nayēn* — a common expression of humility used by Tamil saints to describe their state before receiving grace</note>, wandered without a thought of You in my heart. Like a ghost I wandered until I was exhausted; then I received Your grace, which is so hard to obtain. O Master <note>original: *Ayā*</note> of the shrine of Arutturai in Vennainallur, on the south bank of the bamboo-shaded Pennai river—now that I have become Your bond-slave, can I ever say I am not Yours?

> 71. I shall not die anymore, nor will I be born again, since I have found the Lord <note>original: *Mūrtti*</note> who never ages. O Master <note>original: *Adigal*</note>, look upon me, a wicked man who speaks many lies. O Lord of the shrine of Arutturai in Vennainallur, on the south bank of the thicket-filled Pennai river—now that I have become Your bond-slave, can I ever say I am not Yours?

> 72. Having no other attachment, I have cherished only Your feet in my mind. Since attaining them, I have truly lived; now I have reached the state where I will never be born again. O You of great penance in Pandikkodumudi in Karaiyur, where the learned worship and praise You—even if I should forget You, my tongue will still cry, "Adoration to Śiva!" <note>original: *Namaśivāya* — the sacred 'Five Letters' or Panchakshara mantra</note>.

<vocab>Tamil Śaivism, Tevaram, Sundarar, Vennainallur, Namah Shivaya, Śaivite Saints, Panmurai</vocab>

<summary>In these stanzas, Sundarar reflects on his past spiritual ignorance and celebrates his liberation from the cycle of rebirth through Śiva's grace. He emphasizes his eternal bond to God at the shrine of Vennainallur and his commitment to the sacred mantra "Namah Shivaya."</summary>

<keywords>Sundarar, Vennainallur, Namah Shivaya, reincarnation, grace, Pennai River, Pandikkodumudi, devotion</keywords>

> 70. I roamed like a stray dog <note>original: "cur"</note> for many days
>         Without a single thought of You,
>     I wandered and grew weary, then You gave me
>         A grace that no one else could win.
>     In Vennainallur, in the "Shrine of Grace" <note>original: *Arutturai*</note>
>         Where bamboos line the banks of the Pennai River,
>     My Shepherd, I became completely Yours;
>         How could I ever deny You now? <note>original: "forswear" — to falsely deny or renounce an oath of service</note>

> 71. From now on, there is no more birth or death for me,
>         No more creeping old age, O my Lord who rides the bull. <note>Śiva is traditionally depicted riding the white bull, Nandi.</note>
>     I am sinful and full of lies,
>         But please, mark me as Your own.
>     In Vennainallur, in the "Shrine of Grace"
>         South of the wooded Pennai River,
>     My Master, I became completely Yours;
>         How could I ever deny You now?

---

The varying mood of the saint—at times joyous and triumphant, at others sadly longing for an end to life—is reflected in the next two verses from a single hymn.

> 72. Attached to nothing else in life, my mind thinks only
>         of Your holy feet.
>     I am born anew; from this time forward, I will never
>         walk the path of rebirth again.
>     In Kodumudi, O ascetic Lord, where wise men 
>         greet You with songs of praise,
>     Even if I should forget You, my own tongue would still
>         adore You, crying "Hail, Śiva!" <note>original: *Namaśivāya* — the "Five Letters," the most sacred mantra in Śaivism.</note>

<vocab>Sundaramūrti, Vennainallur, Pennai, Kodumudi, Śiva, Bhakti poetry, Śaivism</vocab>

<summary>Sundarar celebrates his liberation from the cycle of birth and death, declaring his eternal bond to Śiva at the shrines of Vennainallur and Kodumudi. He emphasizes that his devotion is now so deep that his very tongue would praise God even if his conscious mind wavered.</summary>

> 73. The day my senses fail, the day my life departs,
>         The day I am lifted onto the high funeral bier <note>original: *pāḍai* — a litter or stretcher used to carry a corpse to the cremation ground</note>—
>     Instead of dwelling on these things, I think only of You.
>     In Pandikkodumudi, where the cool, spreading waters 
>         Of the Kaveri River descend in all their glory,
>     O Supreme Light, whom the wise adore:
>         Even if I should forget You, my tongue would still cry, "Hail, Śiva!" <note>original: *Namaccivāya* — the sacred five-syllable mantra of Śiva.</note>

---

> 74. When the young Brahmin <note>This refers to the legend of Markandeya, a devotee of Śiva who was destined to die at sixteen but was saved by the Lord.</note> sought Your refuge,
>         To protect him, You snatched away
>     The very life of Death himself when he arrived.
>         Seeing such power, I, Your servant, cry:
>     "You are my Father! If the messengers of Death <note>original: *Naman* — another name for Yama, the god of death.</note> come to torment me,
>         Turn them away by saying: 'He is my servant!'"
>     With this single thought, I have reached Your holy feet,
>         O Lord who dwells in Tiruppunkur, surrounded by lush groves!

<summary>In these verses, Sundaramūrti Swāmi expresses his total reliance on Śiva to transcend the fear of death, citing the Lord's historic rescue of Markandeya as proof of His protective power.</summary>
<keywords>Sundaramūrti Swāmi, Śiva, Namaccivāya, Pandikkodumudi, Tiruppunkur, Markandeya, Yama, Kaveri River, salvation, death</keywords>
