<meta>This page continues the hymns of Sundaramūrti Swāmi, concluding a verse about the sacred site of Kodumudi and beginning a new sequence regarding the Lord's protection from death and His help in Sundaramūrti's domestic life.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>SUNDARAMŪRTI SWĀMI</header>

> 73. When will my end draw near, my senses fade, my life close,
> And I ascend the funeral stretcher? <note>original: "bier" — a stand on which a corpse or a coffin is placed.</note>
> This, and nothing but this, is my only thought.
> But, Lord of Speech, You are the Light on high;
> Where the bright streams of the Kaveri River bring coolness to Kodumudi,
> Even if I should forget You, my own tongue would loudly cry to You, "Hail, Śiva!"

---

God should deliver His own from death. The appeal here is to the familiar story of Markandeya <note>A young devotee of Śiva destined to die at sixteen; when the god of death came for him, he clung to a Lingam, and Śiva appeared to save him.</note>. Yama is the god of death who gathers the souls of men.

> 74. The young saint sought refuge from Death;
> To save him, You killed grim Death himself.
> Your power accomplishes such great deeds,
> And I, who have witnessed them, pray:
> "O Father, if dreaded Yama should press upon me,
> Forbid him. Say, 'He is my servant';
> Show Yourself in lush Pungur <note>modern: Tiruppunkur</note>.
> I have reached Your feet, and You are able to save."

---

Sundarar <note>Another name for Sundaramūrti Swāmi</note> is certain that Śiva will understand his confusion in providing for the needs of his beautiful wives. For does not Śiva Himself bear the burden of two ladies: Parvati, His consort, and Ganga (the goddess of the Ganges) in His hair? In the legend associated with these verses, when one of Sundarar's wives was suffering from hunger, he miraculously received some raw rice from Śiva. This was not enough; to complete the miracle, Śiva had to transport the rice for him to Arur, the home of his beautiful wife. This, too, was done in answer to the hymn of which the next two stanzas are a sample.

<summary>This page features hymns by Sundaramūrti Swāmi asking for Śiva’s protection at the time of death and introduces a legendary account of the Lord providing rice for the saint’s family.</summary>
<keywords>Sundaramūrti Swāmi, Śiva, Kaveri River, Kodumudi, Markandeya, Yama, Tiruppunkur, Parvati, Ganga, Arur, miracles</keywords>

> 75. Thinking of You constantly, I, Your servant, worship You every day.
> So that my wife, with eyes like bright swords, may not wither and suffer,
> O Lord of Kolili <note>modern: Tirukkollili</note>, I have received some rice at Kundiyur.
> But I have no one to carry it; O my Lord, command that it be delivered for me.

> 76. You keep a woman <note>The goddess Parvati, who occupies the left half of Śiva's body.</note> as half of Yourself, and You hold the River Ganga in Your spreading matted hair.
> Surely You understand the distress that beautiful women feel?
> In Kundiyur, surrounded by faultless groves, I have received some rice.
> O Ancient One, O Wondrous One, command that it be carried for me.

---

The following verse is a famous exhortation to poets. Sundarar <note>The author, Sundaramūrti Swāmi</note> advises them not to waste their talents flattering wealthy men who are often ungrateful, but to sing instead of God, who rewards His servants both in this life and the next.

> 77. Even if you praise them and speak only to please them, fickle men give nothing to those who serve them.
> O you poets, do not sing of such liars! Sing instead of our Father at Pugalur <note>modern: Tiruppugalur</note>.
> In this life, He will give you food and clothing; your sorrows will vanish.
> And there is no doubt that in the world to come, you will reign in Śivaloka <term>Śivaloka: the "World of Śiva," or the heavenly realm of the soul's liberation.</term>.

<summary>Sundaramūrti Swāmi prays for the miraculous transport of rice to his family and encourages poets to seek rewards from God rather than from ungenerous men.</summary>
<keywords>Sundarar, Tirukkollili, Tiruppugalur, Shiva, Parvati, Ganga, miracles, Tamil poetry, devotion, Sivaloka</keywords>

> 75. I think only of You always;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Daily I bow in worship;
> She whose eyes are sharp as swords <note>Referring to the saint's wife, Paravai.</note>,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Do not leave her suffering now.
> Lord of Kolili <note>original: Kōḷili; modern: Tirukkollili</note>, You have given

> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I pray, command it to be sent to me.

> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The River Ganga is in Your long hair.
> You surely understand
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the burdens of beautiful women.
> In Kundiyur, which is circled by gems,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;there You gave rice today.
> Source of all things, wonderful one,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I pray, command it to be sent to me.

---

The saint advises his fellow poets to sing the praises of Śiva rather than the praises of men, because men seldom reward the poets. Śiva rewards them both in this life and the next. The Pāri <term>Pāri: a legendary Tamil chieftain famous for his extreme generosity, such as giving his chariot to a climbing vine that had no support.</term> mentioned in hymn 78 <note>Though the following hymn is numbered 77, the text likely refers to the upcoming sequence regarding Pāri.</note> was a chieftain in the Tamil country in the early centuries of our era, famed for his generosity.

> 77. Though you flatter men of lies,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;they will give nothing to the holy;
> do not sing of them, O wise poets,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;but if you would receive true wealth,
> sing of the Father at Pugalur <note>original: Puhalūr; modern: Tiruppugalur</note>;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;here your needs will be supplied,
> pain will vanish; and there,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;you will live as kings in heaven forever.

> 78. Even if you call a weakling "Bhima" <note>Bhima: one of the five Pandava brothers from the *Mahabharata*, legendary for his immense physical strength.</note>, or call a man "victorious Arjuna" <note>original: Vijaya; another name for Arjuna, the supreme archer of the *Mahabharata*.</note> and say he is a master of the bow; even if you call a miser "Pari" <note>A legendary Tamil chieftain famous for his extreme generosity.</note>—they will still give you nothing. O poets, sing instead of our Holy One in Pugalur <note>original: Pukalūr; modern: Tiruppugalur.</note>, whose body is covered in sacred ash. If you do, there is no doubt that you will rule the layered heavenly worlds above.

> 79. This life is an illusion; that it will end in the dust of the earth is certain. The sea of rebirth is a wasteland, afflicted by the pains of hunger and disease. Therefore, perform acts of virtue without delay. Speak the name of Tirukkedaram <note>A sacred pilgrimage site in the Himalayas, modern-day Kedarnath.</note>, the place of Him who stood as a pillar of fire while the broad-eyed Vishnu <note>original: Taṭangkaṇṇan; a reference to the "broad-eyed" protector god.</note> and Brahma on his lotus <note>original: Malarōn; literally "the one on the flower."</note> searched in vain to find His feet below and His head above. <note>This refers to the *Lingodbhava* myth, where Śiva appeared as an infinite pillar of light to humble the ego of Brahma and Vishnu.</note>

<vocab>
**Weakling** (மிடுக்கிலாதான்): One lacking strength; **Bhima** (வீமன்): The strong Pandava; **Arjuna** (விசயன்): The victorious archer; **Pari** (பாரி): The generous patron; **Pugalur** (புகலூர்): A sacred Śiva shrine; **Tirukkedaram** (திருக்கேதாரம்): Kedarnath; **Life is an illusion** (வாழ்வாவது மாயமிது): A common theme on the transience of life; **Sundarar** (சுந்தரர்): The author; **Tevaram Hymns** (தேவாரப் பதிகங்கள்): The collection of devotional songs.
</vocab>

<summary>Sundaramūrti Swāmi urges poets to stop flattering unworthy men for rewards and instead praise Śiva at Pugalur and Kedarnath to gain eternal life. He emphasizes the transience of human existence and the supremacy of Śiva over other deities.</summary>

> 78. Even if you call the weak by the name of Bhima <note>original: Bhīma; a hero of the *Mahabharata* famous for his superhuman strength.</note>,
> Or describe a man as Arjuna <note>original: Arjun; the master archer of the *Mahabharata*.</note> with his bow,
> Or give a stingy <note>original: mean</note> man the fame of the generous Pari <note>Pāri was a legendary Tamil chieftain known for his extreme philanthropy.</note>,
> He still will not bestow a single gift upon you.
> O poets <note>original: bards</note>, sing instead of our holy God,
> Who is white with sacred ash and dwells in Pugalur <note>original: Puhalūr; a sacred site in Tamil Nadu.</note>.
> In the home of the Deathless One,
> You shall reign forevermore.

---

Life and experience have no ultimate value or reality. God alone is real, the only refuge from this unreal world.

> 79. Our life is entirely unreal;
> Its end is nothing but dust.
> Out of the sea of rebirth <note>original: sea of birth; refers to *samsara*, the cycle of reincarnation.</note>
> Come only ruin, pain, and lust.
> Do not delay in doing good,
> But praise the King of Kedaram <note>original: Ketāram; a reference to the sacred Himalayan site of Kedarnath.</note>,
> Whom Vishnu and the great Brahma <note>original: Brahm</note>
> Searched for in vain with sorrow. <note>This refers to the *Lingodbhava* myth, where Shiva appeared as an infinite pillar of fire that Brahma and Vishnu could not find the ends of.</note>

<summary>Sundaramūrti Swāmi warns poets against praising greedy men for rewards they will never receive, urging them instead to praise Shiva at Pugalur and Kedaram to escape the painful cycle of rebirth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical reflections on Sundaramūrti Swāmi’s teachings. While the previous page focused on the futility of earthly praise and the illusory nature of life, this section explains how Shiva replaces those worldly illusions with a stable reality grounded in faith.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>SUNDARAMŪRTI SWĀMI</header>

In the name of Shiva <note>original: Siva; the Supreme God of the Saivite tradition, regarded as the creator, protector, and transformer of the universe.</note>, He has created an established world of faith and has granted it true reality after exposing the [previous] reality as false <note>original: exploding; in this context, it means to disprove or discredit the illusory nature of the material world (Maya) to reveal spiritual truth.</note>. The cause of this is evident, as has been stated previously.

<vocab>Shiva, world of faith, reality, cause</vocab>

<summary>This passage describes how Shiva establishes a permanent world of faith for his devotees, replacing the fleeting illusions of material existence with a divine and certain reality. It serves as a theological justification for the transition from worldly pursuits to spiritual devotion.</summary>

<keywords>Shiva, Saivism, faith, reality, spiritual truth, Sundaramūrti Swāmi, religious philosophy</keywords>

# Māṇikka Vāsahar <note>Māṇikka Vāsahar (9th century CE): A celebrated Tamil poet-saint whose name translates to "He whose words are like rubies." He is the author of the *Tiruvasakam*, a foundational text in Tamil Shaivism.</note>

### AND HIS

## HYMNS

<term>Shaivism</term> <note>A major tradition of Hinduism that worships Shiva as the Supreme Being.</note>

<summary>This title page introduces the section dedicated to Māṇikka Vāsahar, a 9th-century Tamil poet-saint renowned for his profound devotional hymns.</summary>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page provides a biographical overview of the saint Māṇikka Vāsahar, continuing from the introductory title page of section IV.</meta>

### IV.

## MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR

->(Sanskrit form: *Māṇikya Vāchaka*)<-

During the era when the powerful Pandyan kings flourished in Madurai, there was once a prime minister who, early in life, became convinced of the fleeting nature of this world’s life and its riches. While on a visit to Perundurai (now known as Avudaiyarkoil in the Tanjore District), he suddenly and completely fell under the influence of a Brahmin religious teacher, whom he viewed as the manifestation of God himself. Right then and there, he began to sing the "Sacred Utterance" (*Tiruvasakam*) and was named by his preceptor the "Utterer of Jewels" (Manikkavasagar). Returning to Madurai, he gave up his high office and all its rewards to become a wandering religious poet, traveling from shrine to shrine without any earthly attachments. 

The stories surrounding his religious experiences can be read by English readers in Dr. G.U. Pope’s major edition of his work. We find accounts of him practicing asceticism <note>original: austerities</note> at Chidambaram, or miraculously granting the gift of speech to the mute daughter of the Chola king, or defeating a group of Buddhists from Ceylon <note>Ceylon: Modern-day Sri Lanka</note> in a debate <note>original: disputation</note>. However, we have almost no certain historical information about him. Even the century in which he lived is a subject of intense debate among historians <note>original: antiquarians</note>. Tradition places him in the fifth century, earlier than the writers of the *Tevaram*; <note>*Tevaram*: The first seven volumes of the *Tirumurai*, the twelve-volume canon of Tamil Shaiva bhakti poetry.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the life of Māṇikka Vāsahar, a former prime minister turned saint, and notes the legendary stories and historical uncertainties surrounding his life and dates.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Madurai, Tiruvasakam, Perundurai, Dr. G.U. Pope, Chidambaram, Tamil Shaivism, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical dating of Māṇikka Vāsahar and introduces his other literary works and his immense devotional influence.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

...but scholarly opinion seems to be converging on the view that he lived in the latter half of the ninth or the first half of the tenth century AD <note>original: of our era</note>. Another of his works is the *Tirukkōvaiyār*, an erotic poem <note>The *Tirukkōvaiyār* uses the "Akam" tradition of Tamil poetry, where the romantic longing between lovers serves as a metaphor for the soul's desire for God.</note> consisting of four hundred stanzas. Among Tamil Shaivite writers, none makes a stronger devotional appeal than Māṇikka Vāsahar. There is a common Tamil saying that nothing can melt the heart of a person who is not moved <note>original: melted</note> by the *Tiruvāsaham*.

---

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tirukkōvaiyār, Tamil Shaivite, Tiruvāsaham, 9th century, 10th century</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the biographical introduction of Māṇikka Vāsahar, placing his life in the 9th or 10th century and highlighting the profound emotional impact of his masterpiece, the Tiruvāsaham.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tirukkōvaiyār, Tiruvāsaham, Tamil Shaivism, Bhakti movement, 9th century, 10th century, Tamil literature</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the presentation of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns from the Tiruvāsaham, focusing on his intense personal devotion and his exclusive commitment to Lord Shiva.</meta>

<language>Tamil</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 80. My hair stands on end and my body trembles; placing my hands upon my head in worship at Your fragrant feet, my eyes overflow with tears. My heart warms with devotion as I cast away all falsehood, crying out, "Praise be to You! Victory, victory to You!" O Master, I will not let go of Your hand. Look upon me and accept me.
> <note>original: "Sayasaya" is a Tamil rendering of the Sanskrit "Jaya Jaya," meaning "Victory, Victory." This verse describes the physical symptoms of religious ecstasy (bhakti).</note>

> 81. I do not desire the high status of Indra, Vishnu, or Brahma <note>original: Purandaran, Mal, and Ayan</note>. Even if my family line should perish, I will not associate with anyone but Your devotees. Even if I must enter hell, I will not despise it if I am allowed to remain there by Your grace. O Lord, our Perfect One, I will think of no other god but You.
> <note>The poet expresses that the grace of Shiva is more valuable than the positions of the greatest gods in the Hindu pantheon or even the avoidance of hell.</note>

> 82. Like an actor playing a part, I move among Your devotees, hurrying to enter Your house <note>The "house" refers to the state of spiritual liberation or the presence of God.</note>. O Mountain of Jewels, glorious as a stage! Dwell within me and grant me unceasing love for You, O our Master.

<vocab>Tamil, Shaivism, Tiruvāsaham, Māṇikka Vāsahar, Bhakti literature, hymns</vocab>

<summary>These verses from the Tiruvāsaham express Māṇikka Vāsahar's profound emotional ecstasy, his rejection of worldly or even heavenly status in favor of divine grace, and his plea for sincere, unceasing love for God.</summary>

<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tiruvāsaham, Bhakti, Shiva, religious ecstasy, Indra, Vishnu, Brahma, devotion, Tamil poetry</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the presentation of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns from the Tiruvāsaham, specifically stanzas 80–82, which explore the poet's intense emotional devotion and his rejection of worldly or celestial rewards in favor of Shiva's grace.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

Stanzas 80–92 are samples from an opening poem of one hundred stanzas; these are organized into groups of ten, each of which has its own poetic meter and is fairly complete in itself. They clearly reflect the saint’s varying moods. Notice the importance he attaches to emotion; his deepest self-reproach is for failing to feel spiritual ecstasy <note>original: "frenzy"; in this context, it refers to the intense, almost "mad" fervor of divine love or *bhakti*.</note>. Regarding his conception of God, notice how the word "grace" recurs in nearly every stanza. And yet, that God of grace is described (in stanza 84) as being both existence and non-existence.

### THE HUNDRED VERSES

> 80. My body thrills and trembles, and my hands are lifted high in worship. Here at Your fragrant feet, I cry out with sobs and tears. Abandoning all falsehood, I shout, "Victory, victory, praise!" Lord of my life, these clasped hands will offer You worship forever.

---

> 81. I do not crave the divine bliss of Indra, Vishnu, or Brahma <note>original: "Brahm"</note>. Instead, I seek the love of Your saints, even if my own house and lineage should perish because of it. I would even go to the worst hell, provided that Your grace is with me. Best of all, how could my heart ever think of any god but You?

---

> 82. Though I may seem like one of Your saints, I am only playing a part. Yet I run with wonderful speed to reach the heaven where You dwell. O Mountain of gold and precious gems, grant me Your grace: a heart that melts in unceasing love for You, Lord of my life.

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Indra, Vishṇu, Brahm, grace, frenzy, saint, being, non-existence</vocab>

<summary>In these stanzas, Māṇikka Vāsahar expresses the physical and emotional intensity of his devotion, declaring that even the highest heavens or the lowest hells matter less than the presence of God's grace.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tiruvāsaham, Shiva, bhakti, religious ecstasy, divine grace, Indra, Vishnu, Brahma</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns from the Tiruvāsaham, moving from his rejection of worldly power to his descriptions of God's omnipresence and his own perceived lack of devotion.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 83. I do not fear rebirth in any way; what does death matter to me? Even if I were offered heaven, I would not want it. I have no desire to be a great ruler of this earth. O Shiva, our Lord, who wears the honey-dripping *konrai* flowers <note>original: *konrai*; the Golden Shower tree (*Cassia fistula*), whose yellow blossoms are traditionally associated with Shiva.</note>! I suffer, constantly crying out: "When will the day come when I receive Your grace?"

---

> 84. You became the sky, the earth, the wind, and the light. You became the flesh and the soul. You are both existence and non-existence. As the King, You make everyone dance the dance of "I" and "mine" <note>This refers to the "dance" of worldly illusion (*maya*), where individuals are driven by ego and possessiveness.</note>. How can I find the words to praise You?

---

> 85. O Lord with the long matted hair where the flood <note>A reference to the river Ganges, which Shiva caught in his hair to break its fall to earth.</note> rests! O Rider of the Bull, Lord of the heavenly ones! There are those who, upon hearing Your name, feel their hearts yearn; they tremble and melt like water rushing into a deep hollow. Yet, even though You have claimed me as Your own, my heart does not melt from the soles of my feet to the crown of my head. My whole body does not become eyes to pour out floods of tears. My heart must be stone and my eyes must be wood, all because of my past evil deeds <note>original: *vinai*; referring to the accumulated weight of *karma*.</note>.

<vocab>Thiruvasagam, Manikkavasagar, Tamil Saivism, Shiva, hymns, Saivite saints, karma, Ganges, konrai</vocab>

<summary>In these verses, Māṇikka Vāsahar expresses total indifference to worldly or celestial status, describes Shiva as the omnipresent source of all existence, and laments his own spiritual "hardness" in contrast to more fervent devotees.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Shiva, grace, omnipresence, ego, karma, devotion, Tiruvāsaham</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns, focusing on the poet's intense longing for divine grace and his realization of God’s omnipresence despite his own perceived spiritual coldness.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>MĀNIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

> 83. I have no fear of being reborn, but I shudder at the thought that I must die. Even heaven means nothing to me, and what do I care for ruling the entire earth? O Shiva, adorned with wreaths of honey-filled blossoms <note>original: "honeyed blossoms"; likely referring to the *konrai* (Golden Shower tree) flowers traditionally associated with Shiva.</note>! I cry out in agonizing pain: "When will the morning come when You will grant Your grace to me?"

---

> 84. You are the sky, the earth, the wind, and the light; You are our very flesh and soul. You are existence, and non-existence as well. You are the King, who sees how people dance like puppets with their foolish thoughts of "I" and "mine" <note>This refers to the concept of the ego and worldly attachment, which keeps the soul bound to the cycle of rebirth.</note>, while You pull the strings. What words can express Your divine praise?

---

> 85. At the sound of cries like this—"O Rider of the Bull <note>The bull Nandi is the traditional mount of the god Shiva.</note> whose spreading hair receives the falling stream <note>This refers to the myth of the river Ganges (Ganga) descending from heaven; Shiva caught the forceful river in his matted hair to prevent it from shattering the earth.</note>! Lord of Heaven!"—there were true devotees whose hearts, thrilled with love, broke open like dammed-up rivers rushing down. Yet You did not choose any of them; instead, You chose me to be Your own. And yet, my body does not become one single heart from head to toe to melt in love for You; my eyes do not shed stinging tears in swelling floods. Ah, how wretched I am, only moaning! My two eyes are like unfeeling wood, and my heart is a massive, dead stone!

<vocab>Mānikka Vāsahar, Śiva, Bull-rider, Tamil, devotion, bhakti, salvation</vocab>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāsahar expresses his indifference to worldly power and even heaven, longing only for Shiva's grace while lamenting his own lack of emotional devotion compared to other saints. He describes God as the ultimate reality who permeates all elements and controls the "puppets" of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Shiva, bhakti, ego, Ganges, Nandi, divine grace, Tamil poetry, Saivism</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns from the Tiruvacakam. It picks up from his lamentation over his own perceived spiritual coldness and his wonder at God's unexpected grace.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 86. While I was lying buried in the results of my past deeds <note>original: *vinai*; refers to the concept of Karma, the accumulated consequences of past actions which bind the soul to the cycle of rebirth.</note>, You entered into my life and stayed. As if saying, "I will destroy your karma," You made Yourself known to me. You took me as Your own and became my Lord. And yet, like a mere wooden puppet <note>original: *pāvai*; a doll or puppet. The poet laments that he feels as lifeless and unresponsive as a toy despite God’s presence.</note>, I do not sing for You or dance. Alas, I do not even cry out in longing, nor do I wither away; my soul does not faint with love. O Primeval One, is this right? I do not know how my life will end, You who are both the Beginning and the End.

---

> 87. He is unknown even to those who have heard of Him. He is indestructible and has no family ties. Yet, without being told, He heard my every need. While the people of this world looked on, He took me—who am no better than a dog—and placed me upon a throne. He showed me things I had never seen and made me hear things I had never heard. To prevent me from being born again, He claimed me as His own and protected me. This is the miracle <note>original: *viccai*; literally "magic" or "wonderful deed."</note> that my Lord has performed!

---

> 88. He is the God of gods, whom even the King of the gods <note>original: *tēvarkō*; referring to Indra, the ruler of the lower heavens in Hindu cosmology.</note> does not fully know! He is the Primal One who creates, preserves, and destroys the lush worlds. He is the Ancient Forefather, the Father who shares His body with the Great Lady <note>This refers to Shiva's form as *Ardhanarishvara*, the "Half-Woman Lord," where the right side is Shiva and the left side is his consort Parvati, symbolizing the union of the masculine and feminine principles of the universe.</note>...

<summary>Māṇikka Vāsahar expresses deep humility, contrasting his own perceived lack of devotion with the "miracle" of God's grace, which elevated him from a lowly state to spiritual safety.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tiruvacakam, Karma, Grace, Ardhanarishvara, Indra, Shiva, Bhakti, rebirth</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns from the Tiruvācakam. It focuses on the poet's self-reproach for his lack of emotional fervor despite God’s overwhelming grace and his description of Shiva's supreme nature.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

> 86. I lay amidst the consequences of my past actions <note>original: "fruits of deeds"; refers to karma, the accumulated results of past conduct that bind a soul to rebirth.</note>. You revealed Yourself to me with comforting words, saying, "Come, I will destroy the evil effects of your deeds." Thus, You made me entirely Your own servant <note>original: "slave"; in the context of bhakti (devotional) poetry, this indicates a total and willing surrender to God's will.</note>. And yet, I stand here as if I were a statue made of steel. I do not cry out in ecstasy, nor do I sing, nor weep, nor wail—how miserable I am! My spirit does not even faint with deep desire, so dull am I. O Ancient Being, You are the Beginning and the End: tell me, how can I be so cold-hearted? I do not dare to see how this will end.

---

> 87. Though people seek Him, none know Him fully; there is no evil in Him. He has no family; His knowledge is perfect and effortless. I am like a stray dog, yet in the sight of everyone, He has given me a place on earth and shown me things far beyond human understanding <note>original: "ken"; the range of what one can see or know.</note>. He told me things that no ears have heard; He saved me from future births. My Lord, who has lovingly claimed me as His own, has performed such a miracle!

---

> 88. He is our God of gods, whom even the king of the minor deities <note>original: "devas' king"; referring to Indra, the ruler of the heavens in Hindu cosmology.</note> knows only in part. He rules over the three <note>referring to the traditional functions of creation, preservation, and destruction, often associated with Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva.</note> who give life, maintain life, and destroy life in the beautiful gardens of this world. He is our Primal One, the Ultimate Reality, the Ancient Father whose companion is Umā <note>Umā is a name for the goddess Parvati, the consort of Shiva, representing the feminine energy of the divine.</note>. He is our Sovereign.

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar, devas (divine beings), Umā (consort of Shiva), karma (the law of cause and effect), salvation, Tiruvācakam (the sacred text these hymns are from)</vocab>

<summary>This page contains hymns 86 through 88 of Māṇikka Vāsahar, where the poet marvels at God's unmerited grace in saving him from karma while lamenting his own spiritual numbness. He concludes by praising Shiva as the supreme reality who governs the cycles of the universe.</summary>

...who give their praise. The King of all came and made me His own. We are subjects to no one; we fear nothing. We have joined with the servants of His servants, and we shall plunge and dance with them in joy, again and again.

---

> 89. I am like a lowly dog <note>original: "little dog"; a common metaphor in Bhakti poetry for the soul's humility and unworthiness before the Divine.</note> who knows not what to do. I am worthy only of the fate of liars who never see His golden, flower-like feet. Even though I have seen and heard how the sincere ones—those without deceit—attain those fragrant flower-feet, I, a liar, continue to eat, clothe myself, and remain here. O Mighty Warrior! <note>original: "War-bull"; an epithet for Shiva, signifying his power and his mount, the bull Nandi.</note>

---

> 90. I have come to loathe myself, O my Lord—praise be to You! I have no complaints against You. Praise to the feet of the One who has claimed me! To forgive the failings of others is the mark of the great—praise be! Take away this earthly life from me, O Lord of the land of the immortals! <note>original: "celestial land"; refers to the divine realm or liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the final stanza of Verse 88 and presents Verses 89 and 90 of Māṇikka Vāsahar’s hymns. It follows the poet's declaration of spiritual independence and transitions into a raw expression of personal unworthiness and a plea for divine liberation.</meta>

> He came down in grace and made even me His very own.
> From now on, I bow before no man; I fear Him alone.
> Now I have joined the sacred crowd of His servants' servants,
> And more and more I will bathe in bliss, with dancing and song.

---

89. I am the lowliest dog <note>original: "meanest cur"; a standard metaphor in Bhakti (devotional) poetry used to express the soul's extreme humility and perceived unworthiness in the presence of the Divine.</note>; I do not know how to do what is right.
It would be only what I deserve if You were to repay my wickedness
With the dreaded fate of those who never saw Your flower-like feet; <note>The "flower-like feet" are a recurring symbol in Tamil poetry representing the source of divine grace and the ultimate refuge for the devotee.</note>
For though my eyes have seen and my ears have heard of the sincere and worthy saints
Who reached Your fragrant presence, I still remain here, for I am false—
Fit for nothing except eating and dressing, O Lion of Victory! <note>An epithet for Shiva, portraying Him as a powerful and majestic conqueror of evil and ego.</note>

---

90. None but myself has caused my own downfall. May Your name be praised forever!
I lay no blame on You; let praises be raised to my Master!
Yet to forgive is always a mark of greatness. Praise to You!
Lord of the heavenly land <note>original: "land celestial"; refers to the divine realm of Shiva, often synonymous with liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</note>, Praise! Oh, end this earthly life for me.

<summary>Māṇikka Vāsahar concludes his song of spiritual joy before pivoting to a humble confession of his own perceived failings, ultimately asking Shiva for forgiveness and liberation from the physical world.</summary>

91. Partner of Her whose eyes are like a deer’s! <note>Refers to the goddess Parvati; Shiva is often depicted as half-male and half-female (Ardhanarishvara), symbolizing the unity of all opposites.</note>
O Lord of the Vedas, Whom even the end of the Vedas <note>original: "marai yiru"; referring to the Upanishads or the ultimate limits of scriptural knowledge which still cannot fully grasp God.</note> cannot fully know!
Honey and nectar! Inconceivable to the mind!
O King who forgives the faults of this lowly one!
I spoke a few harsh words.
Your devotees have reached the City of Shiva <note>original: "Siva-ma-nagar"; the divine realm of liberation and the eternal presence of God.</note>,
While I and my falsehood stay lingering outside.

---

92. I am false, my heart is false, and my love is false.
But even so, if I, a man of heavy karma <note>original: "vinaiyēn"; one burdened by the consequences of past actions or "deeds."</note>, weep for You, can I not reach You?
O Honey! O Nectar! Pure juice of the sugarcane!
Sweet Great One! Grant me the grace to reach You.

---

93. You make the harvest grow without seeds. <note>A metaphor for God's power to create or grant spiritual results without the need for prior human merit.</note>
You create and then withdraw all the heavens and the earth.
Even me, a deceitful and lowly man,
You have made a "madman" <note>original: "piccan"; in the context of Tamil Bhakti (devotion), this refers to a devotee so overwhelmed by divine love that they appear insane to the world.</note> at Your temple gate.
You have made me fit to be among Your great devotees.
Even if a tree they have grown is poisonous, men do not cut it down;
I am just like that, O Master who owns me. <note>A famous plea: the poet argues that since God has "cultivated" him or brought him into His service, God is now obligated to protect him, regardless of his sinful or "poisonous" nature.</note>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāsahar expresses deep humility, admitting that his devotion is imperfect ("false") but pleading for Shiva's grace. He uses the metaphor of a gardener who spares even a poisonous tree to ask God not to abandon him.</summary>

> 91. The deer-eyed goddess is a part of You! <note>Refers to Shiva’s form as Ardhanarishvara, where he is half-male and half-female, signifying that the Divine encompasses all dualities.</note> You are hidden from the holy scriptures!
> You are honey, yes, divine nectar <note>original: "ambrosia"; the drink of the gods that grants immortality.</note>, beyond the grasp of the human mind.
> O King who bears with my faults, even though I spoke harsh words.
> Your saints have entered heaven. But I, along with my falsehood, remain outside.

> 92. Since I am false, my heart is false, and even my love is false,
> though I weep, still bound by my past actions <note>original: "deeds"; referring to the weight of karma that prevents spiritual progress.</note>, can I reach You above?
> O Honey, Nectar, O essential Sweetness, as great as You are sweet,
> grant me the grace to find the path that leads to Your feet.

> 93. Heaven, earth, and everything within them, You create without a seed <note>A metaphor for God's power to create something from nothing or through pure divine will.</note>.
> You preserve and You destroy. It is You who has decreed
> that I, though treacherous and lowly, should be a man who faints in a spiritual frenzy <note>In the Bhakti tradition, this "madness" or frenzy represents a state of being completely overwhelmed by divine love.</note>
> before Your temple gates, standing among the company of Your true saints.
> Even if it is a poisonous mango tree, those who have planted it themselves
> will not tear it up. O Lord of mine, keep me as such a tree. <note>The poet argues that since God is the ultimate creator of all souls, He should not discard even a "poisonous" or sinful soul like the author's.</note>

***

Our next five stanzas, taken from a hymn of fifty, are full of the deep sorrow <note>original: "pathos".</note> expressed in the title, which is a refrain recurring in every verse. Only flashes of the light of the presence of God pierce the prevailing gloom. The saint cannot free himself from his physical desires <note>original: "sensuality".</note>,

<summary>Māṇikka Vāsahar expresses his deep sense of spiritual inadequacy, concluding with the famous "poisonous mango tree" metaphor to beg for God's protection despite his flaws.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the hymns of Māṇikka Vāsahar from the section titled "Neethal Vinnappam" (The Forsaking Prayer). While the previous page used the metaphor of the poisonous mango tree to illustrate God's ownership of even the flawed devotee, this page continues his desperate plea for Shiva to uphold him against the temptations of the senses and his own ignorance.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

> 94. You accepted even me, the lowest of the low, blending with my soul in Your great mercy.
> O Lord who rides the bull! Do not leave me, I pray!
> You who wear the skin of a fierce tiger, the King of sacred Uttarakosamangai <note>A famous and ancient Shiva temple in Tamil Nadu where the poet is said to have experienced a profound spiritual awakening.</note>.
> O Lord with the matted hair <note>original: "Sada"; the tangled locks of an ascetic, a primary iconographic feature of Shiva.</note>, I am fainting. My Master, hold me up and support me.

> 95. Like a tree standing on the crumbling bank of a river, I am being uprooted by the flood of the five senses and the allure of dark-eyed women.
> Do not leave me! You who dwell in the glorious city of Tiruvarur <note>One of the most important pilgrimage sites in the Saivite tradition.</note>, the King of sacred Uttarakosamangai.
> O Partner of the Goddess who wears jeweled ornaments <note>Referring to Shiva's form as Ardhanarishvara, where he is half-male and half-female, sharing his body with his consort, Parvati.</note>, You are the one who nurtures me!

> 96. In my ignorance, I rejected Your grace, O my Jewel!
> Yet, do not leave me! Forgive the mountain of my sins <note>original: "the multitude of my deeds/karma."</note> and take me under Your rule, O King of sacred Uttarakosamangai.
> For is it not the way of the great to overlook the petty lies of those who are as lowly as little dogs? <note>A common expression of extreme humility in Bhakti poetry, where the devotee compares their spiritual unworthiness to the status of a stray dog.</note>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāsahar continues his "Forsaking Prayer," using vivid metaphors like a tree on a collapsing riverbank to describe his spiritual instability. He pleads for Shiva's mercy, asking the Lord to overlook his ignorance and sins as a master might overlook the faults of a lowly dog.</summary>

<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tiruvasagam, Uttarakosamangai, Shiva, mercy, five senses, Bhakti, Tiruvarur, Ardhanarishvara</keywords>

### WILT THOU LEAVE ME?

94. Merging with me in Your grace, O Rider of the Bull <note>Shiva's vahana, or divine vehicle, is the bull Nandi.</note>,
      You made me Your own.
    But will You leave me? You whose body is clothed
      in the skin of a fierce tiger,
    Ancient Uttarakosamangai <note>An ancient Shiva temple in Tamil Nadu, significant in the poet's life.</note> has You for its King. O Lord
      of mine
    With matted hair <note>original: "Sada"; the long, tangled locks of an ascetic, typical of Shiva's iconography.</note>, hold me up; for I have grown weary and sad.

95. Like a tree planted on the edge of a flowing stream that
      erodes its banks, I am
    Shaking and ready to fall; for my senses are
      bewitched by the dark eyes of women.
    O King of Uttarakosamangai, husband of the jewel-clad
      Parvati <note>The goddess and consort of Shiva.</note>,
    You who dwell in holy Arur <note>Tiruvarur, a major center of Shaivite worship.</note>, O Protector, arise to help me.

96. In my ignorance, I rejected Your grace. Do You,
      my Jewel, now despise me?
    And will You leave me? O destroy the weight of my deeds <note>Referring to the accumulation of karma.</note> and make me Yours.
    King of Uttarakosamangai, it is surely true that the
      great and wise
    Are always inclined toward mercy, even when lowly dogs <note>A common metaphor in Bhakti poetry to express the devotee's extreme humility and perceived unworthiness.</note>
      act falsely.

<summary>In these verses from the "Forsaking Prayer," Māṇikka Vāsahar pleads with Shiva not to abandon him to his own weaknesses and the weight of his past karma. He uses the metaphor of a tree on a crumbling riverbank to describe his spiritual instability.</summary>

97. I was exhausted and wandering, with no one to say to me, "Do not fear, my child."
      O You who flash like lightning, do not leave me! Truly, if one seeks a comparison,
      You are like only Yourself, King of eternal Uttarakosamangai <note>An ancient Shiva temple in Tamil Nadu where the poet is said to have had deep spiritual experiences.</note>.
      You are like a Mother to me; You are my Father and my rare, precious Treasure.

98. Whether I revile You or praise You, I am broken and distressed
      by my own faults. Do not leave me! O You who shine like a mountain
      of red coral, You are my Master. Out of pity for small living things,
      You drank the burning poison <note>A reference to Shiva drinking the *Halahala* poison to save the universe during the churning of the ocean of milk.</note> so that even a lowly person like me might eat the nectar of immortality.

<meta>This page continues the "Forsaking Prayer" (Neethal Vinnappam) from the Tiruvacakam by Māṇikka Vāsahar. It then transitions into a discussion of various types of devotional songs the poet wrote for the common people to sing during their daily activities.</meta>

<language>English</language>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

> 97. With no one to comfort me in my fear, I have wandered far and wearily,
> O You who are like lightning—will You leave me?
> If I truly compare You, King of Uttarakosamangai <note>A historic Shiva temple in South India where the poet is said to have achieved enlightenment.</note>, I find nothing else that resembles You;
> You are a true father and a dear mother to me, my rare treasure.

> 98. Whether I praise or curse You, I am still stained by sin and sorrow.
> Even so, will You leave me? O Splendor shining like the mountain of red coral,
> Master, You drank the black poison <note>original: "poison black" — referring to Shiva consuming the *Halahala* poison to save the world.</note> out of pity for humble beings,
> so that I, Your lowliest servant, might find no poison, but a fountain of nectar.

The poet composed songs for young women to sing during their rhythmic games or while working at the grinding stone. In India, the boatman sings while rowing, the farmer (<term>ryot</term> <note>A term for a peasant farmer or tenant in India.</note>) sings while drawing water from the well, and the soldier (<term>sepoy</term> <note>An Indian soldier serving under European or local authority.</note>) sings while marching. A key feature of these songs is the refrain, which is usually a collection of pleasant-sounding syllables, though it may also have a specific meaning. 

Here are examples of a few songs intended for women. The refrain of the first, “Elorembavay,” likely means “Hear and consider what I say, my lady.” Curiously, the “Grinding Song” is used at funerals, as is the “Antiphony” <note>A song where two groups sing in response to one another.</note>. The song “The Destruction of the Three Castles” <note>The legend of *Tripura Samhara*, where Shiva destroys three demonic cities.</note> is intended to accompany a game played with a ball or a type of shuttlecock called an <term>undi</term>. (For the legend of the Three Castles, see page 7). The “Shoulder-Play” is for an ancient game where women held one another’s shoulders.

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Uttarakosamangai, farmer, soldier, Elorembavay, Antiphony, Three Castles, undi, Shoulder-Play</vocab>

<summary>The poet concludes his plea for divine presence, comparing God's mercy to a parent's love and the saving grace of drinking poison for others. The text then introduces communal songs Māṇikka Vāsahar wrote for everyday tasks and folk games.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tiruvacakam, Shiva, divine parentage, Halahala poison, devotional folk songs, Tamil culture, work songs</keywords>

99. O most ancient of all that is ancient,

### SONG OF THE MAIDENS

99. You are older than the oldest of all,
    And newer than everything that is new.
We will fall in service at the feet of your saints;
    We are your true handmaidens.
Let none but your devoted servants call us their own;
    Lord, we would marry no others.
We would be slaves at their command alone:
    In this way, let our happiness be made perfect.
->Ēlōrembāvāy.<- <note>A ritual refrain used by young women in these hymns, roughly meaning "Hear and consider, my lady."</note>

100. "Surely there is refuge with you for your child,"
    We cry out in trembling.
Hear us, O our Lord, as we bring you one request;
    Grant us just one favor for our joy.
Let only those who love you embrace us,
    Let the work of our hands be for them.
Let our eyes rest only on such people day and night;
    If that happens, who cares if the sun rises in the west or the east? <note>Meaning that with God's grace, the devotees are indifferent to worldly omens or the laws of nature.</note>
->Ēlōrembāvāy.<-

---

### THE GRINDING SONG

101. We grind the golden powder so that he may bathe;
For he is the Scripture <note>original: "Vedas"—the ancient sacred texts.</note>, he is the sacrifice;
He is the truth of existence, and he is the falsehood of existence too;
He is light, yes, and he is deep darkness;
He is deep sorrow, and he is true bliss;
He is the half, and he is also the whole;
He is bondage, but he is also true liberation;
He is the beginning, and he is the end <note>original: "alpha... omega," the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, symbolizing the totality of God.</note>.

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāśahar, Song of the Maidens, Ēlōrembāvāy, The Grinding Song, Tiruvāçakam, Tamil poetry, Saivism</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the "Song of the Maidens," where devotees pray to serve only God's followers, and begins "The Grinding Song," which uses paradoxical language to describe Shiva as the source of all opposites.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Shiva, Tiruvempavai, Tirupporsonnam, devotion, ritual bathing, religious paradox</keywords>

This is a poem structured as a dialogue between two girls playing a game; one asks a question that seems to mock Shiva’s strange appearance or habits, and the other provides a profound theological response.</meta>

<header>104 HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

### THE SACRED CHAZHAL
<note>The *Chazhal* is a traditional game played by young women, accompanied by rhythmic clapping. The verses follow a pattern of "question and answer" regarding the mysteries of God's nature.</note>

102. "He smears himself with white ash and wears a hissing snake; the words he speaks with his sacred mouth seem to be the ancient scriptures <note>original: "Vedas"—the oldest layers of Sanskrit literature and Hindu scripture.</note>. Tell me, my friend, why is this?"
"Though he smears himself with ash, speaks those words, and wears a snake, he has become the Lord and the very inner nature of every living soul. *Chazhalo!*"

103. "He is my father, my Master, and the Lord of all; yet why does he take a stitched-up loincloth <note>original: "kovanam"—a simple strip of cloth worn by ascetics, symbolizing renunciation.</note> as his only garment?"
"Understand that the four scriptures, which contain all lasting wisdom, serve as the very threads for the loincloth he has put on. *Chazhalo!*"

104. "His temple is the cremation ground; his garment is the skin of a fierce tiger; he has no mother and no father; he stands entirely alone. Look at him!"
"Even though he has no mother or father and stands alone, if he were to be moved to anger, the entire world would turn to mere dust. *Chazhalo!*"

105. "He is without end, yet he has immersed me—one who approached him like a lowly dog—into a flood of supreme joy. Look at his grace!"
"Those sacred feet that immersed you in the flood of joy are the one supreme treasure even for the gods who dwell in the heavens. *Chazhalo!*"

<summary>This page contains verses from the "Sacred Chazhal," a poetic dialogue where the outward, often "frightening" or ascetic attributes of Shiva are questioned and then defended as signs of his supreme divinity and grace.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Tiruvachakam, Tiruchazhal, Shiva, white ash, Vedas, cremation ground, sacred feet, Tamil Saivism</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---

One girl asks a skeptical or mocking question about Lord Shiva's unconventional appearance or behavior, and the other responds with a deep theological explanation, ending with the rhythmic refrain "Chazhalo!" (a sound used in the game).</meta>

<language>English</language>

<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

## ŚIVA'S MYSTERIES (An Antiphony <note>An "antiphony" is a piece of music or poetry performed by two groups or individuals in call-and-response format.</note>)

102. "His body is smeared with white ashes; a snake is his strange decoration; he speaks the secret scriptures. Tell me, my friend, what kind of god is this?"
"Why talk of the ash-smear, the holy speech, or the strange decoration? Know only this: this god is the true inner nature of every living thing. *Chazhalo!*"

103. "He is my father and my master, the supreme Lord of all people, yet he is dressed only in a stitched, hanging loincloth. Tell me, friend, is he not mad?"
"Know that the four Vedas <note>original: "Vedas"—the oldest and most sacred Hindu scriptures.</note>, filled with meaning, and the eternal Shastras <note>original: "Śāstras"—sacred laws or treatises.</note> are merely the threads from which his loincloth is woven. *Chazhalo!*"

104. "The cremation ground is his beautiful temple; a tiger’s skin is his clothing; he has no father or mother; he stands all alone. Look at this, my friend!"
"Though he has no parents or relatives, if his anger were to be kindled, look! The whole wide world would immediately turn to dust and ashes. *Chazhalo!*"

105. "Though I am as lowly as a dog <note>original: "cur"—a common term used by the poets to express deep humility before God.</note>, when I turned to him who has no end, he submerged me in an overwhelming sea of joy. Look at this, my friend."
"Know that those holy feet that sank you into that sea of overwhelming joy are the most precious treasure even to the gods in heaven. *Chazhalo!*"

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Śiva, Antiphony, Vedas, Śāstras, Chāḷalō</vocab>

<summary>This page presents four verses of a rhythmic dialogue explaining the paradoxical nature of Shiva: his seemingly lowly or strange appearance (wearing ashes and rags) actually represents his role as the creator and the essence of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva, Māṇikka Vāsahar, Sacred Chazhal, Hindu theology, asceticism, spiritual joy, Vedas, Shastras</keywords>

### THE SACRED FLIGHT (Tiruvunthiyar <note>A section of the Tiruvachakam where the refrain "Undipara" suggests a game where players jump or toss something into the air, symbolizing the soul's flight toward God.</note>)

106. The bow was bent, the battle began, and the three demon cities were thrown into confusion. Let the message fly! See how they all burned together! Let the message fly! <note>original: "muppuram"—the three "Triple Cities" of the demons (Tripura) which Shiva destroyed with a single arrow to restore balance to the universe.</note>

107. We saw no second arrow. In the hand of the Lord of Ekambam, there was only one arrow for the three cities. Let the message fly! Even that one was more than enough! Let the message fly! <note>original: "Ekambar"—a title for Shiva as the Lord of the Ekambareswarar Temple in Kanchipuram.</note>

108. As soon as the chariot was finished and the Lord stepped into it, the axle snapped. Let the message fly! Yet even then, the three cities were destroyed. Let the message fly! <note>This refers to a legend where the gods grew proud of the elaborate chariot they built for Shiva; to humble them, Shiva broke the axle with just his weight before destroying his enemies by a mere laugh or a single arrow.</note>

### THE SACRED VISION (Thirunoakkam <note>A section named after a game where players look into one another's eyes while dancing or moving in a circle.</note>)

109. For so long, I was a wretched and lowly servant; I wasted my life like water poured onto a wasteland, failing to bow before the Supreme Lord. But then the Peerless Gem, the eternal First Cause, came to me and plucked out the very roots of my cycle of rebirth. Shall we not play the game of the Sacred Vision? <note>original: "born-again-bolt/lock"—the poet describes Shiva removing the spiritual "bolt" that keeps the soul locked in the cycle of reincarnation (samsara).</note>

<summary>These verses celebrate Shiva's power and grace, moving from the mythological destruction of the Triple Cities (Tripura) to the poet's personal salvation and liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Manikkavasagar, Tiruvachakam, Tripura, Kanchipuram, rebirth, Tiruvunthiyar, Thirunoakkam, Shiva's grace</keywords>

### THE DESTRUCTION OF THE THREE CITIES <note>original: "The Three Castles' Destruction." This refers to the mythological destruction of the "Triple City" (Tripura) of the demons by Shiva.</note>

106. The bow was bent and the battle began; the three cities were completely overwhelmed. (Fly, undi!) The three cities burned together in a single flame. (Fly, undi!) <note>original: "undī"—part of the refrain "undipara," a term from a game involving jumping or tossing objects into the air, used here to celebrate the soul's liberation.</note>

107. We saw only one arrow in Shiva's hand; just one single arrow for all three cities. (Fly, undi!) And even that one was hardly needed by him. (Fly, undi!)

108. The chariot split apart under the weight of his step, and the axle was completely shattered. (Fly, undi!) Yet the three cities still lay ruined and dead. (Fly, undi!) <note>According to legend, the gods were so proud of the elaborate chariot they built for Shiva that he humbles them by breaking it with his weight, showing he needs no help to destroy evil.</note>

### THE SHOULDER-PLAY <note>original: "Tōṇōkkam." A game where girls or women touch each other’s shoulders while dancing or moving in a circle, singing praises of God.</note>

109. I was a wretched slave who wasted my days for nothing, offering no worship to him, the Supreme Lord. Yet look! He, the incorruptible jewel of eternity, has come to pull back the prison-bolt of rebirth and set me free.
->Let us play the Shoulder-Play<-

---

In the poetry of all lands, lovers have called upon birds to be messengers to their distant beloved. This is so common in Indian poetry that it has become a recognized convention. Here, the saint sends his message of love and devotion—once through a humming bee and once through an Indian cuckoo—to Shiva, who lives in Tillai (modern-day Chidambaram).

<summary>This page concludes the verses on Shiva’s destruction of the demonic "Triple City" and begins the section on "The Shoulder-Play," where the poet celebrates being freed from the cycle of rebirth. It also introduces the literary convention of using birds as spiritual messengers.</summary>

<keywords>Manikkavasagar, Tiruvunthiyar, Thirunoakkam, Shiva, Tripura, Chidambaram, reincarnation, sacred games, bird messengers</keywords>

### THE SACRED HUMMING-BEE <note>original: "Thirukothumbi." A poetic form where the author addresses a king-bee, instructing it to fly to the feet of the Lord. In Hindu devotion, the soul is often compared to a bee seeking the nectar of God's grace.</note>

110. He did not say, "He is a hard-hearted thief and stubborn-minded." Instead, He melted my stony heart and, in His mercy, He made me His own. Go and hum at the golden, ringing feet of the Lord of the beautiful assembly hall in Tillai, O King-bee! <note>original: "Tillai"—the ancient name for Chidambaram, home to the famous temple where Shiva performs his cosmic dance.</note>

111. To the Master who made even a dog like me sing of His feet; to the Great One who forgives the faults in the heart of a demon like me; to the Lord who acts like a mother and accepts the humble service of one who has no merit at all—go and hum to Him, O King-bee!

112. Where would I and my mind be today if the Lord and His Lady <note>original: "Thaiyal"—referring to Shiva's consort, Parvati or Shakti, who represents His creative energy.</note>—He with the low-hanging matted hair—had not claimed me? Go and hum at the honeyed, flower-like feet of the Master, who is the heavens, the four directions, and the great sea, O King-bee!

113. I was sinking daily into the depths of false wealth, thinking it was the only reality. But He took me as His own. To Him whom I call "Father," "My Soul," and "Lord of the Assembly Hall"—go and hum at His red, flower-like feet, O King-bee! <note>original: "Ambalava"—The Lord of the Hall (Nataraja).</note>

### THE BEE'S MESSAGE

110. I was a hard-hearted thief and stubborn, but no such
      name did He call me;
      He melted my stony heart, and by His mercy He
      captured me.
      Swans are plentiful in Tillai's lovely hall of gold,
      His dwelling. <note>Tillai is the ancient name for Chidambaram, the site of the golden temple where Shiva performs His cosmic dance.</note>
      Fly, king of bees, and hum at His golden anklets,
      telling my message.

111. Though I am like a dog <note>original: "Cur"</note>, my Lord has set me to
      singing of His great glory;
      To me, the madman, His patient grace is forever
      bringing forgiveness;
      Without scorning me, He condescends to accept the
      service I can do for Him.
      He is both Mother and God. Go, king of bees, and hum
      my message to Him.

112. My heart and mind would have wandered far from
      Him, but He compelled me;
      The Lord with matted hair <note>original: "tangled locks"</note> and His beautiful spouse <note>Referring to the goddess Parvati, Shiva's consort.</note>,
      they saved and held me.
      He is the sky, the mighty sea, and the one dwelling
      in the east, west, north, and south.
      His feet drip with honey. There, king of bees,
      speak my praise.

113. I lay immersed in the false treasures of this world,
      deceiving myself;
      I held them to be true treasure, but He received me
      as His own.
      He is my very life, abiding in the hall of Tillai.
      Go, king of bees, and entrust my words to His
      red lotus feet.

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar (Māṇikkavāśagar), The Bee's Message, Tillai (Chidambaram), king of bees, lotus feet (a symbol of divine purity), grace, self-deceivèd</vocab>

<summary>Māṇikkavāśagar expresses profound humility and gratitude for Shiva's grace, contrasting his own perceived unworthiness with the Lord's mercy. He uses the poetic device of a bee messenger to send his praises to the golden hall of Chidambaram.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikkavāśagar, Tiruvachakam, Shiva, Chidambaram, Bhakti, divine grace, humility, Parvati</keywords>

### THE CUCKOO'S CALL

114. O little cuckoo, dwelling in groves of honey-sweet fruit, listen to this:
      The generous One, who left heaven to come to this earth to win over humanity,
      He who entered my heart, putting the flesh to shame, and became my very understanding—
      Call Him to come here, the Bridegroom of the one whose eyes outshine the deer's. <note>The "one whose eyes outshine the deer's" refers to the human soul, personified as a bride longing for the divine Bridegroom, Shiva.</note>

---

### THE MORNING HYMN

115. Praise to You, the source and substance of my life! The day has dawned.
      We stand here with flower offerings for Your lotus-like feet, praising You.
      Waiting for the grace to bloom from Your sacred face, we worship Your feet with joyful smiles.
      O Lord Shiva, dwelling in Tirupperunturai <note>original: "Tirupperunturai" — the sacred site where the author first encountered his divine Guru.</note> surrounded by cool fields where muddy-petaled lotuses bloom,
      You who fly the high-raised bull-banner, my Master who has made me Your own—
      My Lord, arise from Your sleep! <note>The "Morning Hymn" (Tiruppalli Ezhuccchi) is a genre of poem used to "awaken" the deity in the temple for morning worship, symbolizing the awakening of the soul to God's presence.</note>

<vocab>Māṇikkavāśagar, Kuyil Pathu (The Cuckoo's Call), Tiruppalli Ezhuccchi (The Morning Hymn), Tirupperunturai, Shiva, Bull-banner.</vocab>

<summary>This page features two hymns by Māṇikkavāśagar: one calling upon a cuckoo to act as a messenger to Shiva, and the first verse of the "Morning Hymn" used to symbolically wake the deity at the temple in Tirupperunturai.</summary>

<keywords>Māṇikkavāśagar, Tiruvachakam, Cuckoo's Call, Morning Hymn, Tirupperunturai, Shiva worship, bhakti poetry</keywords>

### THE CUCKOO'S ERRAND
<note>original: "Kuyil Pattu" — A poem where the soul, personified as a lover, asks a cuckoo bird to act as a messenger to bring God (Shiva) to them.</note>

> 114. Listen, little cuckoo in the honey-sweet orchard groves.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He set Heaven aside; to save us humanity, to earth
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He came.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Giving without limits, caring nothing for this flesh of
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;mine,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He entered my mind, and there He became my very 
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;thought.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He, the Peerless One, the husband of the goddess whose 
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;pure eyes
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Outshine the gazelle in softness—please call Him 
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;here. <note>The "spouse" refers to the goddess Parvati, representing the divine energy or the perfected soul.</note>

---

<note>The following paragraph reflects the perspective of the original 1921 editors, who viewed Hindu temple rituals through a Western missionary lens.</note> 
One of the traditional practices in temple worship <note>original: "idolatry"</note> is the belief that every morning, with solemn ceremony, the deity must be awakened from sleep, bathed, and dressed. Here is a song used to rouse the Lord from slumber. However, notice how successfully the author has filled his poem with the fresh feeling of morning and the sights and sounds of the sudden break of the Indian dawn.

### THE IDOL'S AWAKENING
<note>original: "Tiruppalli Ezhuccchi" — Literally "The Awakening from the Sacred Couch," a genre of hymns sung in South Indian temples to wake the deity for the day's first rituals.</note>

> 115. Praise to You, rare treasure,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Source of all prosperity,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Dawn has come; at Your feet,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We lay these beautiful flowers.
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Praising You, we await
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The sweet and beautiful smiles 
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On Your face, as we fall
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Face down in worship at Your feet.
>
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Shiva, Lord, dweller in
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Perunturai, where white-petaled 
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Lotus flowers open wide
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the cool, moist meadows,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You whose banner bears the bull, <note>The bull (Nandi) is the sacred mount and symbol of Shiva.</note>
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You the Lord of all my life,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now, O Lord of us all,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Arise from Your couch in Your grace.

<vocab>Māṇikkavāśagar, Śiva (Shiva), Perunduṟai (Perunturai), Tiruvasagam, Cuckoo's Errand, Idol's Awakening (Tiruppalli Ezhuccchi), Tamil poetry, Saivism, hymn, prayer</vocab>

<summary>This page features two hymns by Māṇikkavāśagar: a plea for a cuckoo to call God to the poet, and a morning hymn (Tiruppalli Ezhuccchi) designed to ritually awaken the deity Shiva in the temple at Perunturai.</summary>

116. The sun has reached the East; the darkness has fled.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The sun of grace rises from your pure, lotus-like face.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As it rises, your beautiful eyes—like fragrant blossoms—begin to open.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Swarms of bees are humming all around.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O Lord Shiva, who dwells in Tirupperunthurai, <note>original: "Tirupperunthurai" — A sacred town in Tamil Nadu where the author is said to have met his guru.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You who grant the treasure of grace, O Mountain of Bliss,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O surging Ocean, please arise from your sleep. <note>original: "palli elundarulyaye" — A ritual address asking the deity to wake and bless the world.</note>

117. The cuckoos have called from the flower groves; the roosters have crowed.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The small birds have chirped; the conch shells have sounded. <note>The blowing of the conch shell is a traditional part of morning temple rituals.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The stars’ light has faded as the dawn draws near.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With great longing, we wait; O God, show us your feet adorned with beautiful anklets.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O Lord Shiva, who dwells in Tirupperunthurai,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You who are difficult for all others to know, yet so easy for us to reach,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Our Lord, please arise from your sleep.

<vocab>
**Tirupperunthurai**: A major Shiva temple and the site of Māṇikkavāśagar's conversion.  
**Siva Peruman**: "Lord Shiva," the supreme deity in the Saivite tradition.  
**Tiruppalliyeluchi**: The "Sacred Awakening of the Lord," a genre of morning hymns.  
**Saivite Saints**: The 63 Nayanars and other poets like Māṇikkavāśagar who championed the worship of Shiva.  
**Tamil hymns**: The devotional poetry (Bhakti) composed in the Tamil language, known for its emotional intensity.
</vocab>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O mountain of joy and ocean of bliss,

118. On one side are the players of the sweet-sounding lute; <note>original: "vīṇai" — a traditional Indian stringed instrument often associated with divine music.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;on another, those who chant the <term>Rig Veda</term> <note>The Rig Veda is the oldest of the four sacred Vedas (scriptures) of Hinduism.</note> and sing songs of praise.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;crowded clusters of flowers in their hands;

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and those who tremble in religious ecstasy.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;in prayer above their heads.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;my Master, who has taken even me as your own 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and shown me such sweet grace,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;please arise from your sleep!

119. You dwell in the heart of the Mistress, <note>The "Mistress" refers to the goddess Parvati, Shiva's consort and the personification of divine energy (Shakti).</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and the Mistress dwells in your heart.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since you both dwell within the heart
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;of me, your humble servant,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;then, O Master, grant me the grace 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to always be in the company of your devotees.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O Eternal First Cause who dwells 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;in the <term>Golden Hall</term>, <note>original: "Ponnambalam" — The sacred hall in the great temple of Chidambaram where Shiva is said to perform his cosmic dance of creation and destruction.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;stand before me now,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;so that my heart's deepest longing may be fulfilled!

118. On this side, some men play

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sweet to me have been your ways.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Rise from your couch in your grace.

The rest of our examples of the "Holy Utterances" <note>original: "Tiruvāśagam" — the collection of hymns by Māṇikka Vāśagar.</note> may be left to explain themselves without comment, except for a single line of title. Where two or more stanzas are given from a poem, the title provided here is a translation from the Tamil.

### ONLY WITH YOU AND YOUR SAINTS!

119. Our Lady <note>This refers to the Goddess Parvati (Shakti), Shiva's consort.</note> is always in your heart,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As you are in hers; and if you both
Dwell in mine, grant me a place
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Among your servants, O eternal First.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Unending Lord, who resides in <term>Tillai’s</term> <note>Tillai is an ancient name for Chidambaram, the site of the famous temple of Shiva as Nataraja.</note> hall,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let this deep yearning of my soul be satisfied.

<meta>This page continues the "Holy Utterances" (Tiruvāśagam) of Māṇikka Vāśagar. These verses express the poet's profound sense of gratitude for divine grace, his struggle with worldly temptations, and his utter dependence on Shiva for spiritual liberation.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

120. You gave yourself to me, and you took me to yourself.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O **Shankara** <note>original: "Śaṅkara" — a name for Shiva meaning "the Giver of Happiness."</note>, who has the better of this bargain?
I have gained eternal, boundless bliss,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But what have you gained from me?
O my Lord, who has made my heart your temple;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O Shiva, dwelling in holy **Perunturai** <note>Modern: Tirupperunturai, the site of the poet's spiritual awakening.</note>;
My Father, my Master, you have taken residence in my body;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For this, I have nothing I can give you in return.

121. Caught in the net of the anger of those
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whose eyes are like cruel fish <note>The "fish-eye" is a classic poetic trope in Tamil literature, often used to describe beauty but here used in the context of worldly temptation and the "net" of desire.</note>, I was wasting away;
O Light of Wisdom, I can find no other help.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O You who share your form with her whose feet are soft as cotton <note>A reference to Shiva's half-female form, **Ardhanarishvara**, where he shares his body with his consort, Parvati.</note>,
With your coral-hued lips,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Say to me, "Do not fear!"
This is the grace I have longed for,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O my Master!

122. O Supreme Lord, who fills both the earth and the sky!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I have no other attachment but you.
O King of the City of Shiva <note>original: "Sivapuram" — the heavenly home of Shiva's devotees.</note>, shining with glory!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O Shiva, dwelling in holy **Perunturai**;
To whom shall I complain? To whom shall I tell my grief
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If you, who have claimed me as your own, do not show your grace?
I cannot live in this world surrounded by the vast sea;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Grant me your mercy, and call me to come to you!

<summary>In these three verses from the Tiruvāśagam, Māṇikka Vāśagar marvels at the "unequal exchange" where Shiva gives himself in return for the poet's unworthy soul, and pleads for rescue from the distractions of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Tiruvāśagam, Tirupperunturai, Shankara, Sivapuram, divine grace, spiritual surrender, Tamil Saivism</keywords>

### WHAT CAN I GIVE YOU?

120. You gave yourself to me, and you gained me in return;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which of us drove the better bargain?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I found bliss in infinity;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But what did you derive from me?
O Shiva, God of **Perunturai** <note>original: "Perunduṟai" — the site of the poet's conversion, modern Tirupperunturai.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You took my mind to be your shrine:
My very body is your home:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What can I give you, Lord, that is truly mine?

---

### THE PAIN OF PASSION

121. I am caught in the snare of passion from the
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;liquid eyes of women;
Stabbed at the heart, like a stray dog <note>original: "cur" — an aggressive or lowly dog; used here as a common poetic expression of religious humility.</note>. O light of wisdom,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I see no other help.
Only Lord, whose Lady's <note>A reference to the goddess Parvati, who forms the left half of Shiva's body.</note> feet are softer than
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;soft feathers,
How I long to hear your coral-red lips speak words of comfort
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to me.

---

### LONGINGS FOR DEATH

122. Our Supreme Lord, dwelling in both earth and heaven,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;See how I have no other help but you.
You, King of Shiva's world <note>original: "Śiva's world" — Shivaloka, the celestial realm of Shiva.</note>, bright beyond telling,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Dweller in Perunturai, look upon me.
Who will hear my cry, who will listen to my complaining,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If you deny your grace, you who saved me?
I find no joy remaining in this earth surrounded by the sea.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Now let your grace speak, and bid me come to you.

<vocab>Manikka Vasagar, Shiva, Perunturai, Bhakti, Tamil poetry, Shaivism</vocab>

<summary>In these verses, the poet Manikka Vasagar explores the "divine bargain" of grace, his struggles with worldly desire, and his intense longing to leave the mortal world for Shiva's presence.</summary>
<keywords>Manikka Vasagar, Shiva, Perunturai, divine grace, devotion, spiritual liberation, Bhakti poetry</keywords>
