<header>118 HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

### NO OTHER REFUGE

123. O Partner of Her whose feet are soft as cotton <note>A reference to the goddess Parvati, who forms the left half of Shiva's body.</note>, besides You I have no other support. You governed me rightly, King of **Shivapuram** <note>original: "Śivapuram" — the city of Shiva, representing a spiritual or celestial realm.</note>, Shiva who dwells in **Tirupperunturai**. I was afraid; I am a deceiver who, out of delusion, forgot the grace You once gave me. I cannot live here any longer; grant Your grace and tell me, "Come to Me."

---

124. O Partner of Her of flawless, ancient fame, besides You I have no other support. You who wear the crescent moon, King of Shivapuram, Shiva who dwells in Tirupperunturai. Shall I worship another? Shall I praise another? Tell me, do I have any other help? O Rider of the young bull, I cannot live here; grant Your grace and tell me, "Come to Me."

---

### THE REVELATION AT KAZHUKKUNDRAM

125. Lord of **Perunturai** where there is no discord! To those who speak Your praise, You are a joy beyond compare. You wipe away our sorrow, our Master. You are the seed that never withers; after my past deeds reached their balance so that no more karma might grow <note>This refers to the concept of *Karma-samya*, the point where a soul's good and bad deeds are balanced, making it ready to receive divine grace.</note>, You came and showed Your countless sacred forms to me at **Kazhukkundram** <note>original: "Kazhukkuṉṟam" — Tirukkalukundram, also known as 'The Hill of the Sacred Vultures,' where the saint is said to have had a profound vision of Shiva.</note>.

<summary>In these verses, the poet Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses his absolute spiritual dependence on Shiva and recounts a mystical experience at the hill of Kazhukkundram after his worldly karma was resolved.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Shiva, Tirupperunturai, Shivapuram, Kazhukkundram, Parvati, Karma, devotion, Tamil Saivite poetry</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the translations from the Tiruvāśagam, specifically the sections expressing the saint Māṇikka Vāśagar’s total dependence on Shiva and his transformative experience at Kazhukkundram.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀSAHAR</header>

123. In You dwells She whose feet are softer than down <note>A reference to the goddess Parvati, who is said to reside in the left half of Shiva’s body in his Ardhanarishvara (half-man, half-woman) form.</note>; see how I have no other help but You. King of Shiva's world <note>original: "Śivapuram"</note>, my gracious master, Dweller in **Perunturai** <note>original: "Perunduṟai" — The site of the saint's initial conversion and a major temple to Shiva.</note>, look upon me. Fear takes hold of me; for, in dark, godless confusion, I forgot the grace that once saved me. I am as lowly as a dog and full of deceit. Life here is joyless. Now let Your grace speak, and bid me come to You.

124. In You dwells She whose ancient praise is flawless; see how I have no other help but You. King of Shiva's world, wearing the bright crescent moon, Dweller in **Perunturai**, look upon me. Whom besides You could I worship with my praises? Can any other provide a refuge for me? O Rider of the bull, my life is joyless. Now let Your grace speak, and bid me come to You.

---

### THE BALANCING OF DEEDS <note>This title refers to the Hindu theological concept of *Karma-samya*, the moment when a soul's good and bad deeds are perfectly balanced, making it ready to receive divine grace.</note>

125. O lord of **Perunturai**, a place of peace! To those who call Your name, You are a true joy beyond comparison. You made my sorrow cease when my good and evil deeds were balanced fairly. Then, so that the unwithering seeds of rebirth <note>original: "seeds of birth" — The karma that causes a soul to be born again into the cycle of suffering.</note> would not grow, You showed Your beautiful self in **Kalukkundram** <note>original: "Kaḷukuṇḍu" — Modern Tirukalukundram, also known as the "Hill of the Sacred Vultures," where the saint experienced a divine vision.</note>.

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāsahar, Śiva, Perunduṟai, Kaḷukuṇḍu, deeds, grace</vocab>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses his absolute dependence on Shiva, describing himself as a humble seeker begging for liberation from the world, and recounts how Shiva appeared to him at Kalukkundram after his karma reached a state of balance.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Shiva, Perunturai, Kalukkundram, Karma-samya, Parvati, Ardhanarishvara, Bhakti poetry, liberation</keywords>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>HYMNS OF TAMIL ŚAIVITE SAINTS</header>

126. I no longer know myself, nor do I know the passing of day or night. He who is beyond the reach of mind and speech has turned me into a madman <note>original: "matthoanmatthan" — A common theme in mystic poetry where the devotee's intense love for God is perceived as madness by the world.</note>. He who possesses the fierce, mighty bull and dwells in **Tirupperunthurai**—the sacred Teacher <note>original: "panavan" — often referring to a Brahmin or a spiritual guide; here, Shiva appearing in human form as the saint's guru.</note>—I know not what He has done to me, O Supreme Light!

127. I desire no fame; I desire no wealth; I desire neither the earth nor the heavens. Daily I will refuse even to touch those who do not desire Shiva. I have gone and reached the feet of the Lord who dwells in **Tirupperunthurai**. I have bound myself to Him; I will not stray from Him, nor will I ever let Him go.

128. Shall I call You honey from the mountain branch? Shall I call You the nectar of the roaring sea? I cannot bear to be without You, our Hara <note>original: "Haran" — A title for Shiva meaning "the Destroyer" of sin or "the Captivator" of hearts.</note>, our rare medicine, my King! O Pure One, Your sacred form is covered in white ash, and You dwell in **Tirupperunthurai** surrounded by lush, muddy fields!

129. While the people of this wide world perform penance, I have lived in vain, burdened by this body of flesh like a useless tree in the forest. O Dweller of **Tirupperunthurai**, where the honey-dripping *konrai* flowers bloom <note>The Golden Shower tree, whose yellow blossoms are sacred to Shiva.</note>! Even though I am a sinner, may I not still call upon You to grant me Your grace?

<vocab>Thirupperunthurai, Paramjyoti, Śaivite, Hymns, Tamil</vocab>

<summary>This page contains four verses from Māṇikka Vāśagar expressing his ecstatic devotion, his rejection of worldly desires in favor of Shiva's feet, and his humble plea for grace despite his perceived unworthiness.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Tiruvāśagam, Tirupperunthurai, Shiva, divine madness, renunciation, sacred ash, konrai flowers, bhakti</keywords>

<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀŚAHAR</header>

### LIFE'S CONSUMING

126. I can no longer understand myself, nor do I know the difference between day and night. He who transcends both words and thoughts has completely taken away my senses. He who uses Vishṇu as His bull <note>In Hindu mythology, Vishṇu once took the form of a bull to serve as Shiva's mount.</note> and dwells in **Perunduṟai**—O Supreme Light, appearing in the guise of a Brāhman <note>A member of the priestly class; Shiva is said to have appeared to the author as a human guru/teacher.</note>, He has cast a strange spell over me.

127. I do not ask for fame, wealth, the earth, or heaven. I desire no more births or deaths. I will have nothing to do with those who do not love Śiva. It is now my privilege to see the eternal **Perunduṟai** and to wear the King’s foot as my crown <note>A gesture of total humility and devotion, placing the feet of the divine upon one's head.</note>. I will never leave His shrine, nor will I ever let Him leave His own devotee.

128. Are You like honey on a branch too high for me to reach? Or are You the nectar churned from the ocean <note>Refers to *Amrita*, the elixir of immortality produced by the gods and demons churning the primordial ocean of milk.</note>? O Hara <note>original: "Hara" — A name for Shiva meaning "the Seizer" or "the Destroyer" of sin and suffering.</note>, sublime King in **Perunduṟai**, surrounded by lush, watered fields, I see You! Your form is smeared with sacred ash, yet You are spotless. How can I bear this ecstasy?

129. Many who live on this great earth perform penance; yet I, burdened by this body of flesh, have grown like a barren tree in the jungle. O Dweller in ancient **Perunduṟai** where the *konrai* <note>original: "kondai" — The Golden Shower tree, whose yellow blossoms are sacred to Shiva.</note> tree blooms, may I, a sinner, cry out, "Will You not give Yourself to me?"

<vocab>Māṇikka Vāśahar, Vishṇu, Perunduṟai, Brāhman, Śiva, Hara, kondai tree, penance</vocab>

<summary>In these verses from the Tiruvāśagam, Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses a state of divine madness and total surrender to Shiva, declaring that he desires nothing but the presence of the Lord at Perunduṟai.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Shiva, Perunduṟai, mysticism, devotion, bhakti, sacred ash, spiritual ecstasy</keywords>

### WHAT WE FEAR

130. I do not fear the bright-scaled snake in its hole; nor do I fear the deceptive words of liars. But when I see those who have not approached the feet of our Lord—He of the matted hair and the eye in His forehead <note>A reference to Shiva's third eye, representing wisdom and the destruction of desire.</note>—and those who do not think, "There is no other God," then I am truly afraid. <note>original: "Namañjumāṟe" — the refrain meaning "the way we fear" or "we are afraid."</note>

131. I do not fear the spear stained with blood; nor do I fear the sidelong glances of women with bangled arms. But when I see those who lack love, who cannot praise my flawless Jewel as He dances in the Hall <note>The "Hall" (Ambalam) refers to the Golden Hall at Chidambaram where Shiva performs his cosmic dance.</note>—making my very bones melt with devotion—and who cannot joyfully drink of His grace, then I am truly afraid.

### THE TEN VERSES ON HOLDING FAST

132. O King of the heavenly beings! O Yoga that fills all space without exception! You are the healing medicine that ripened unexpectedly for me, ruling over my entire family line and granting us a life of true abundance. O certain conclusion of all Truth! O glorious Feet! O Wealth! O Lord Śiva! For my own sake, I have grasped You firmly. Where could You possibly depart to now? <note>original: "Sikkena-p-pidiththen" — This is from the famous *Piditha Pathu* (The Decad of the Tenacious Grip), where the saint boldly claims to have "caught" God so He cannot leave.</note>

<vocab>Tamil hymns, Saivism, Śaivite Saints, Thevaram, Thiruvacakam, Appar, Manikkavacakar, Namañjumāṟe, Piditha Pathu</vocab>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses that the only true thing to fear is a life without devotion to Shiva, concluding with a bold declaration of having firmly "grasped" the Lord through love.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Shiva, Chidambaram, Devotion (Bhakti), Fear, Spiritual Certainty, Tiruvāśagam</keywords>

<header>MĀṆIKKA VĀŚAGAR</header>

### PIOUS FEAR
<note>This section is from the *Accho Padigam*, where the saint lists things that do not scare him (like snakes or weapons) to emphasize that the only thing he truly fears is a lack of devotion in others.</note>

130. I do not fear smooth, lurking snakes;
I do not fear the fake truths of liars;
But when I see fools approaching
even the feet of Him, our King—
our three-eyed Lord with matted hair <note>A traditional description of Śiva, whose third eye represents wisdom and whose matted hair holds the Ganges river.</note>—
while being unaware of His great divinity;
fools who think other gods can exist,
such a sight inspires terror in me.

131. I do not fear a spear’s bloody blade;
nor the sidelong glance of a girl wearing bangles;
But when I see people devoid of grace,
drinking no sweetness from the praise
of my unpolished Gem <note>original: "unchiselled Gem" — a metaphor for the natural, pure beauty of God.</note>, whose dance
is seen in the hall of Tillai <note>Modern-day Chidambaram, where Śiva performs his cosmic dance, the Tandava.</note>, whose gaze
melts a person's whole being in ecstasy;
such a sight inspires terror in me.

### I HOLD FAST TO YOU
<note>original title: "I Cling to Thee"</note>

132. King of the heavenly beings! All-encompassing Excellence!
You have shown Your wonders even to a lowly person like me;
You are the medicine of true joy, ending the false sensory pleasures of earth;
You took my entire family line as Your own.
You are the meaning of the holy scriptures! Your glory is wondrous!
True wealth, our Śiva, I hold fast to You, Lord.
So that I never lose my grip, I hold fast to You;
Where could You possibly go, leaving me in sorrow? <note>This reflects the "Tenacious Grip," a state where the devotee feels they have "captured" God through love.</note>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses that his only true fear is the sight of those who lack devotion to Śiva, and he declares his inseparable, firm hold on the Divine, whom he credits with redeeming his entire family.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Śiva, Tillai, Chidambaram, Tiruvāśagam, Pious Fear, Bhakti, Tamil Saints</keywords>

133. King of the heavenly beings, who delights in the bull-vehicle! <note>original: "vidai" — refers to Nandi, the sacred bull that serves as Lord Śiva’s mount.</note>
You are the true reality of a sinner like me.
I lay in this hut of worms, rotting with stench, 
growing old until I was nothing but dust. 
But You protected me from reaching that low end and claimed me as Your own.
My God, great ocean of mercy!
I have gripped You firmly and I hold You fast; 
where can You go from me now?

134. Entering this body of foul flesh as if it were a great golden temple,
You softened my heart until my very bones melted.
O Lord, You who are the stainless gem!
O pure Light who cut through all my bonds—
the sorrow, the cycles of birth and death, and the confusion of this world.
O Joy, I have gripped You firmly and I hold You fast;
where can You go from me now?

135. I do not want relatives; I do not want a hometown; I do not want fame.
I do not want the company of the learned; I have had enough of worldly study.
O Dancer who resides in the shrine at Kuttālam! <note>original: "Kuttralam" — a sacred site in Tamil Nadu famous for its waterfalls and the Kutralanathar Temple dedicated to Śiva.</note>
I only pray for a heart that melts and yearns for Your jingling, ankleted feet,
with the same intensity that a mother cow yearns for her calf.

<summary>In these verses from the Tiruvācakam, Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses intense devotion, claiming he has "captured" God through love. He renounces all worldly ties—family, fame, and even intellectual learning—desiring only a heart that instinctively longs for Śiva.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Tiruvācakam, Śiva, Kuttālam, devotion, renunciation, grace, Piditha Pathu</keywords>

> 133. King of the heavenly beings, who always rides the bull as Your steed, <note>The bull is Nandi, the sacred mount of Lord Śiva, symbolizing dharma and strength.</note>
> I am full of evil, yet You are my only treasure.
> To prevent me from rotting in my corrupt body and truly dying,
> You have preserved me, and now I belong to You.
> You are our God; You are a boundless sea of grace.
> Rescued from my physical nature, now I cling to You, Lord.
> I will never let You go; I hold You fast.
> Where can You go, leaving me in my sorrow?

> 134. You entered my wretched, physical body
> As if it were a grand golden temple;
> Softening and melting my whole being, You saved me,
> O gracious Lord, You divine jewel!
> Sorrow, the cycle of birth and death, and all the delusions that deceived me—
> You removed them all, breaking every bond.
> True bliss, our compassionate Light, I hold You fast;
> Where can You go, leaving me in my sorrow?

### NOTHING BUT YOUR LOVE

> 135. I do not ask for relatives, nor a name, nor a home,
> Nor the company of learned scholars.
> Human learning has no value to me;
> Lord of Kuttālam, I come to You. <note>original: "Kuttālam" — a sacred site in South India famous for its waterfalls and the Kutralanathar Temple dedicated to Śiva.</note>
> Will You grant me just one favor?
> Give me a heart that melts in sweet longing for You,
> Just as a cow yearns for her newborn calf,
> As I long for Your holy feet.

<vocab>Mānikka Vasahar, Kuttālam, celestial ones, Saiva Siddhanta, Thiruvasagam, Tamil poetry, devotional hymns</vocab>

<summary>In these verses, Māṇikka Vāśagar describes the Lord's entry into his body as if into a temple, transforming his physical nature into spiritual bliss. The poet concludes by renouncing all worldly status and intellectual pursuits, asking only for a heart that loves God with the instinctual intensity of a mother cow for her calf.</summary>

> 136. Lacking virtue, lacking penance, lacking concentration, and lacking wisdom, I whirled about and fell like a leather puppet in a play. <note>original: "தோலின் பாவைக்கூத்" (tholin pavai-kūttu) — A traditional shadow puppet made of leather, moved by strings or sticks, symbolizing a person with no self-will or spiritual direction.</note> But He showed me the Great Light and the path to the world from which one never returns. He revealed His beautiful form and made me His own. When will I, a wretched sinner, finally be united with Him?

> 137. I was laboring among fools who knew nothing of the path to liberation. He taught me the way of devotion <note>original: "பத்திநெறி" (bhakti-neri) — the path of love and surrender to God.</note> so that my ancient karmas might be destroyed. He cleansed the impurities of my mind, transformed me into His divine likeness, and ruled over me. My Father gave me such grace—who else could ever receive the like? Oh, what a wonder! <note>original: "அச்சோவே" (Achcho-ve) — An exclamation of wonder, joy, and awe at God’s grace.</note>

> 138. I used to mistake false paths for the true way. To keep me from wandering into those lesser ways and to lead me to His divine grace, the Dancer—who has no outward sign or form—revealed His sacred dance to me. <note>original: "கூத்தன்" (Kūttan) — The Dancer; a reference to Śiva as Nataraja, whose cosmic dance represents the five activities of creation, preservation, destruction, illusion, and grace.</note> He granted me the knowledge to understand it. Who else could receive such grace? Oh, what a wonder!

<vocab>karmas, bhakti, liberation, Nataraja, Tiruvācakam, Māṇikka Vāśagar</vocab>

<summary>Māṇikka Vāśagar expresses profound humility and wonder, describing how Lord Śiva rescued him from a meaningless life and false spiritual paths to grant him the supreme grace of the divine dance.</summary>
<keywords>Māṇikka Vāśagar, Tiruvācakam, Lord Śiva, divine grace, bhakti, spiritual transformation, leather puppet metaphor, cosmic dance</keywords>

### LONGING FOR UNION

136. I had no virtue, spiritual discipline, knowledge, or self-control. <note>original: "penance"</note>
->Like a puppet to be turned<- <note>original: "A doll to turn" — This refers to a leather shadow puppet (tholin pavai-kūttu) that has no movement of its own, symbolizing a person without spiritual agency or direction.</note>
At the will of others, I danced, whirled, and fell. But for me,
->He filled every limb<-
With love’s mad longing; and so that I might climb
->to that place from which there is no return,<- <note>Refers to the state of liberation or Moksha, where the soul is freed from the cycle of reincarnation.</note>
He showed His beauty and made me His own. Oh,
->when shall I go to Him?<-

### THE WONDER OF GRACE

137. I was a friend of fools; no one like that can know
->the way of freedom. Yet to me<-
He showed the path of love, so that 
->the fruit of my past deeds might be ended.<- <note>original: "Fruit of past deeds" — A reference to Karma, the accumulated consequences of past actions that bind a soul to the world.</note>
Cleansing my foul mind, He made me like
->a god.<-
Ah, who could win what the Father has
->bestowed?<-

138. Thinking it was right, I walked the path of sin;
->but, so that I might leave such paths<-
And find His grace, the dancing God—
->who lives far beyond our thoughts—<-
Revealed His dancing to me—oh, wonder beyond greatness! <note>The "dancing God" is Śiva as Nataraja, whose cosmic dance symbolizes the five divine acts: creation, preservation, destruction, illusion, and the bestowal of grace.</note>
->He showed His dance.<-
Ah, who could win what the Father has
->bestowed?<-

<summary>In these verses, the poet Māṇikka Vāśagar marvels at how Lord Śiva's grace transformed him from a helpless "puppet" of sin into a divine being, leading him toward eternal liberation through the vision of the cosmic dance.</summary>

<meta>This page begins Appendix I, which provides a geographical and historical guide to the various sacred shrines and sites mentioned throughout the hymns of the Tamil Saivite saints.</meta>

# APPENDIX I

## SHRINES MENTIONED IN THESE POEMS

*   **Āduturai** (or **Āvaduturai**): Located in the Tanjore <note>modern: Thanjavur</note> District; it is now a station on the South Indian Railway.
*   **Aiyāṛu**: Located twelve miles from Tanjore.
*   **Ālavāy** (or **Uttarakosaimaṅgai**): Located in Madurai.
*   **Annāmalai** (or **Tiruvannāmalai**): Located in the South Arcot District.
*   **Ārūr**: Located in Tiruvarur, <note>The original text uses "Tiruvallur," but the location in the Tanjore District identifies it as the famous shrine of Tiruvarur.</note> Tanjore District.
*   **Athihai Vīraṭṭānam**: Located in the South Arcot District.
*   **Bramāpuram**: Also known as Shiyali <note>modern: Sirkazhi</note> in the Tanjore District.
*   **Cheṅkāṭṭankuḍi**
*   **Chidambaram** (also known as **Tillai** or **Perumpaṭṭapuliyūr**): Located in the South Arcot District; it is the most venerated site in Shaivism <note>original: Saivism</note>.
*   **Chola**: One of the three great kingdoms into which the ancient Tamil country was divided.
*   **Comorin**: The extreme southern tip of India <note>modern: Kanyakumari</note>; it remains a major site of pilgrimage.
*   **Dharmāpuram**: Near Tranquebar <note>modern: Tharangambadi</note> in the Tanjore District.
*   **Kāviŗi** (or **Cauvery**): The most sacred river in South India.
*   **Kachchi Ehambaram** (or **Kāñchipuram**): The modern-day city of Kanchipuram <note>formerly: Conjeeveram</note> in the North Arcot District.
*   **Kediḷa**
*   **Kēṭāram**
*   **Koḍumudi**: Located near Erode in the Coimbatore District.
*   **Kōḷili**: Near modern Tiruvarur, Tanjore District.
*   **Kongu**
*   **Kuṇḍaiyūr**: Near Tiruvarur, Tanjore District.
*   **Kūṭṭālam**: Located in the Tanjore District.
*   **Malapāḍi**: Located in the South Arcot District, near Trichinopoly <note>modern: Tiruchirappalli</note>.
*   **Maraikkāḍu**: The modern-day Vedaranyam <note>original: Vetharaņiam</note> in the Tanjore District. Interestingly, the modern name is a Sanskrit translation of the Tamil name, meaning "Forest of the Vedas"—that is, a secluded place for studying the sacred scriptures.
*   **Maruhal**: Located on the Cauvery River in the Tanjore District.
*   **Neyttānam**: Located on the Cauvery River in the Tanjore District.
*   **Oṭṭiyūr** (better known as **Tiruvoṭṭiyūr**): Located a few miles north of Madras <note>modern: Chennai</note>; it is now essentially a suburb of the city.
*   **Palaņam**: Located in the Tanjore District.
*   **Perumpaṭṭapuliyūr**: See **Chidambaram**.
*   **Perunduŗai**: Also known as **Āvudaiyārkoil** in the Tanjore District.
*   **Puhalūr**
*   **Puṅgūr**: Located seven miles from Shiyali, Tanjore District.
*   **Pūnturuṭṭi**
*   **Puvaņam**: Located twelve miles from Madurai.
*   **Tillai**: See **Chidambaram**.
*   **Tirupuṭṭūr**: Located in the Ramnad <note>modern: Ramanathapuram</note> District, near Pudukkottai.
*   **Tiruvannāmalai**: See **Annāmalai**.
*   **Tiru Neyttānam**: See **Neyttānam**.
*   **Tūṅgānaimāḍam**
*   **Uttarakosaimaṅgai**: Located in the Ramnad District.
*   **Valivalam**: Located on the Cauvery River in the Tanjore District.
*   **Venneyṇallūr**: Located in the South Arcot District.

---

*Note:*—"Tiru" is a title meaning "Holy." In cases where it has become a permanent part of the location's name, that name is listed here under the letter T.

<summary>This appendix provides an alphabetical gazetteer of the sacred Tamil shrines mentioned in the hymns, identifying their geographical locations and modern names as of the early 20th century.</summary>
<keywords>Tamil Shrines, Shaivism, South India geography, Tanjore, Madurai, Chidambaram, Tiruvannāmalai, sacred rivers, Tiru honorific</keywords>

# APPENDIX II

## SYSTEM OF TRANSLITERATION AND PRONUNCIATION OF TAMIL LETTERS

---

| Tamil Letter | Sanskrit Equivalent | English Transliteration | Pronunciation (approximate) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| அ | a <note>original Sanskrit: அ/अ</note> | a | like the **u** in p**u**nch |
| ஆ | ā <note>original Sanskrit: ஆ/आ</note> | ā | like the **ā** in r**a**ther |
| இ | i <note>original Sanskrit: இ/इ</note> | i | like the **i** in s**i**t |
| ஈ | ī <note>original Sanskrit: ஈ/ई</note> | ī | like the **ī** in cl**i**que |
| உ | u <note>original Sanskrit: உ/उ</note> | u | like the **u** in f**u**ll |
| ஊ | ū <note>original Sanskrit: ஊ/ऊ</note> | ū | like the **ū** in r**u**le |
| எ | — | e | like the **e** in f**e**d |
| ஏ | ē <note>original Sanskrit: ए</note> | ē | like the **a** in f**a**ble |
| ஐ | ai <note>original Sanskrit: ऐ</note> | ai | like the **ai** in **ai**sle, but much shorter |
| ஒ | — | o | like the **o** in m**o**bility |
| ஓ | ō <note>original Sanskrit: ओ</note> | ō | like the **ō** in n**o**ble |
| ஔ | au <note>original Sanskrit: औ</note> | au | like the **ow** in c**ow** |

---

### CONSONANTS

*Note: The Tamil alphabet is not entirely phonetic like Sanskrit and other Dravidian alphabets. Several letters represent different sounds depending on how they are used in a word.*

---

| Letter | 1. When silent (indicated by a dot) <br/> 2. At the start of a word <br/> 3. After a hard consonant | After a soft consonant <note>term: 'Soft' consonants in this context usually refer to nasal sounds like 'n' or 'm'.</note> | In other places (usually between vowels) | Remarks |
| :---: | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| க் | k <note>original Sanskrit: क्</note> | g <note>original Sanskrit: ग्</note> | h <note>original Sanskrit: ह्</note> | **Guttural:** Produced at the back of the throat. |
| ச் | ch <note>original Sanskrit: च्</note> | j <note>original Sanskrit: ज्</note> | s <note>original Sanskrit: स्</note> | This is pronounced as a palatal sibilant <note>term: A hissing sound made by placing the tongue against the hard palate.</note> |
| ட் | ṭ <note>original Sanskrit: ट्</note> | ḍ <note>original Sanskrit: ड्</note> | ḍ | **Cerebral:** Produced with the tongue curled far back against the roof of the mouth (retroflex). |
| த் | t <note>original Sanskrit: त्</note> | d <note>original Sanskrit: द्</note> | th | **Dental:** More distinct than English dental sounds, made with the tongue against the teeth. |
| ப் | p <note>original Sanskrit: प्</note> | b <note>original Sanskrit: ब्</note> | p | **Labial:** Produced using the lips. |
| ற் | *t* | *d* | *r* | Hard palatal **ṟ**, unique to Tamil when pronounced after a soft consonant. |

---

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues Appendix II, detailing the transliteration and pronunciation of Tamil nasal and liquid consonants, as well as the system used for Sanskrit-derived terms.</meta>

| Tamil Letter | Sanskrit Equivalent | Transliteration | Pronunciation (approximate) | Remarks |
|:------|:---------|:-----------------|:----------------------------|:--------|
| ங் | ङ <note>original Sanskrit: ṅa</note> | ṅ (before g) | like the **n** in si**n**ging | Guttural **n** <note>term: A sound produced at the back of the throat.</note> |
| ஞ் | ञ <note>original Sanskrit: ña</note> | ñ | like the **n** in gi**n**ger | Palatal **n** <note>term: A sound produced with the tongue against the hard palate (roof of the mouth).</note> |
| ண் | ण <note>original Sanskrit: ṇa</note> | ṇ | **n** produced with the tongue as far back as possible | Cerebral **n** <note>term: Also called retroflex; the tongue curls back to touch the roof of the mouth.</note> |
| ன் | न <note>original Sanskrit: na</note> | n | as in English | |
| ம் | म <note>original Sanskrit: ma</note> | m | as in English | |
| ய் | य <note>original Sanskrit: ya</note> | y | as in English | |
| ர் | र <note>original Sanskrit: ra</note> | r | as in English (when soft) | |
| ல் | ल <note>original Sanskrit: la</note> | l | as in English | |
| வ | व <note>original Sanskrit: va</note> | v | as in English, but not quite as firm | |
| ழ் | – | ḷ | an **r** sound produced with the tongue as far back in the throat as possible | Unique to the Tamil language |
| ள் | ळ <note>original Sanskrit: ḷa</note> | ḷ | an **l** sound produced against the palate | Palatal **l** |

---

Sanskrit words are transliterated according to their Sanskrit pronunciation, using the system found in other books in this series. The only exceptions are words that have been modified by long-term usage in the Tamil language—for example, the name of the author <term>Māṇikka Vāsahar</term> <note>original: Māṇikka Vāsagar; a 9th-century poet-saint who composed the Tiruvāçakam</note>. The Sanskrit alphabet is represented as follows:

k kh g gh ṅ
ch chh j jh ñ
ṭ ṭh ḍ ḍh ṇ
t th d dh n
p ph b bh m
y r l v
ś ṣ s h
ṛi ṁ ḥ

# INDEX

->(The numbers refer to pages, not to stanzas.)<-

**ĀDIŚESHA**, 7. <note>The king of all serpents in Hindu mythology, often depicted as the couch of Vishnu.</note>
Aiyāṛu, 61. <note>A sacred site in Tamil Nadu, home to a famous temple of Shiva.</note>
Alvār, 4. <note>The twelve poet-saints of the Vaishnavite tradition (devotees of Vishnu).</note>
Aryan, 3.
Ash, sacred, 23. <note>Vibhuti, the holy ash used in Saivite worship to represent the temporary nature of the physical world.</note>
Astrology, 21.
Asuras, 7. <note>A class of divine beings in Hindu mythology, often depicted as the rivals of the gods.</note>
Athikai Vīrattānam, 39, 43. <note>A celebrated temple where Shiva is said to have destroyed the three flying cities of the demons.</note>
Āvaduturai, 49, 65.
Avudaiyārkoil (Perundurai), 86, 111, 117 f., 121. <note>The site of the enlightenment of the saint Māṇikka Vāsahar.</note>

**BRAHMĀ**, 4, 6, 13, 21, 39, 41, 55, 89. <note>The creator god within the Hindu trinity.</note>
Brahmans, 17, 33, 59, 71, 88. <note>The traditional priestly class in Hindu society.</note>
Buddhism, 11, 27, 86.

**CAUVERY**, 15, 55. <note>The Kaveri, the major sacred river of South India.</note>
Chidambaram (Tillai, Perumpattapuliyūr), 5, 17, 65, 86, 107, 109, 115, 131. <note>The "Golden Hall" where Shiva performs his cosmic dance.</note>
Comorin, 57. <note>Cape Comorin, also known as Kanyakumari, the southernmost tip of India.</note>
Conjeeveram, 19. <note>Modern Kanchipuram, one of the seven holiest cities in India.</note>
Consecration, 53.
Cuckoo as messenger, 107. <note>A common poetic device where the soul sends a bird to carry a message to God.</note>
Cycles of existence, 67. <note>The concept of reincarnation and the repetitive nature of the universe.</note>

**DANCE** of Śiva, 5, 6. <note>The Tandava, symbolizing the cosmic cycles of creation, preservation, and destruction.</note>
Dēvāram, 2. <note>The foundational collection of hymns by the three primary Saivite saints.</note>

**EMOTION**, emphasis upon, 89, 91, 125.

**FAVOURITISM** in God, 73.

**GAMES**, songs sung in, 101 f. <note>Folk games like tossing balls or swinging, which the saints used as metaphors for spiritual devotion.</note>
Ganges, 5, 23, 51, 79. <note>The holy river personified as a goddess residing in Shiva's matted hair.</note>
Grace, divine, 65, 89, 127.

<column-break/>

**HARA** (Śiva), 33. <note>original: Hara; a title for Shiva meaning 'the Remover' or 'the Destroyer' of evil.</note>

**IDOL**, worship of, 111.
Indra, 51, 89, 113. <note>The king of the Vedic gods and the ruler of the heavens.</note>

**JAINS**, 11, 27, 33, 59, 61. <note>Followers of Jainism; during this historical period, there was significant religious competition between Jains and Saivites.</note>
Joy in God, 47-51, 75, 77.

**KAILĀSA**, 5, 39. <note>The sacred mountain in the Himalayas, considered the earthly home of Shiva.</note>
Kāḷi, 5. <note>A fierce goddess associated with time, change, and destruction.</note>
Kaḷukkundu, 119.
Katpaha tree, 47. <note>original: Kalpavriksha; the celestial, wish-fulfilling tree of Hindu mythology.</note>
Kedila, 61. <note>The Gadilam River in Tamil Nadu.</note>
Kētāram, 83. <note>Kedarnath, a prominent pilgrimage site in the high Himalayas.</note>
Kodumuḍi, 77.
Kōḷili, 81.
Kondai (cassia flower), 5, 73, 121. <note>The golden shower tree flower, which is traditionally worn by Shiva.</note>
Kulachchiṛai, 31. <note>A prime minister and one of the 63 Nayanmars (Saivite saints).</note>
Kūn Pāṇḍiyan, 29. <note>A king of the Pandya dynasty who converted from Jainism to Saivism.</note>
Kundaiyūr, 81.
Kuttālam, 125.

**MADURA** (Ālavāy, Uttarakosamangai), 10, 19, 31, 33, 99. <note>The capital city of the Pandya kingdom and a center of Tamil culture.</note>
Mahādev (Śiva), 51. <note>original: Mahadev; 'the Great God'.</note>
Maḷapāḍi, 73.
Mangaiyarkkarasi, 29. <note>The queen who, along with Kulachchirai, helped restore Saivism to the Pandya kingdom.</note>
Mārkaṇḍeya, 15, 79. <note>A young devotee whom Shiva saved from Yama, the god of death.</note>
Maruhal, 27.
Meru, 7. <note>The golden mountain at the center of the universe in Hindu cosmology.</note>
Mīnākshi, 31. <note>The 'fish-eyed' goddess and consort of Shiva at Madurai.</note>
Muyalahan, 5. <note>The dwarf-demon representing ignorance and ego, crushed under Shiva's foot.</note>

**NAMASIVĀYA**, 25, 67. <note>The sacred five-syllable mantra meaning 'Adoration to Shiva'.</note>
Nandan, 73. <note>Nandanar, the only Dalit (outcaste) saint among the 63 Nayanmars.</note>
Nandi (Śiva's bull), 5, 61. <note>The sacred bull who serves as Shiva’s mount and the guardian of his abode.</note>

# INDEX

**NATARĀJAN (Śiva)**, Frontispiece, 5. <note>The "Lord of the Dance," representing Shiva's cosmic dance of creation, preservation, and destruction.</note>
**Neyttānam**, 15. <note>A sacred site in Tamil Nadu, home to a historic Shiva temple.</note>

**PALNY**, 15. <note>modern: Palani. A famous hill temple site dedicated to Lord Murugan, though mentioned here in the context of Saivite hymns.</note>
**Pāri**, 81. <note>One of the seven great "last patrons" of the Sangam era, known for his extraordinary generosity.</note>
**Pārvati (Umā)**, 5, 13, 59, 61, 63, 65, 75, 79, 93. <note>The goddess who is the consort of Shiva, representing the creative power or Shakti.</note>
**Periya Purāṇam**, 29. <note>A 12th-century hagiography detailing the lives of the sixty-three Nayanars (Saivite saints).</note>
**Pessimism**, 51, 67, 83. <note>Refers to the poetic themes regarding the weariness of worldly life and the desire for spiritual liberation.</note>
**Pilgrimage**, no need for, 57.
**Planets**, 21. <note>Refers to the influence of astrology and how the saints viewed the celestial bodies in relation to God's will.</note>
**Puhalūr**, 81.
**Pungūr**, 79.
**Pūnturutti**, 47.
**Purāṇas**, 4. <note>original: Purāṇas. A vast genre of Indian literature about history, folklore, and mythology.</note>
**Pūvaṇam**, 41.

**RĀMESWARAM**, 55. <note>One of the holiest pilgrimage sites in India, where Shiva is said to have been worshipped by Rama.</note>
**Rāvaṇa**, 7, 39. <note>The demon king who attempted to lift Mount Kailash and was crushed by Shiva's toe before becoming a devoted singer of his praises.</note>
**Rig Veda**, 3, 17, 59, 115. <note>The oldest of the four Vedas, the foundational scriptures of Hinduism.</note>
**Rudra**, 3, 41. <note>A name for Shiva in his fierce, ancient Vedic form.</note>

**SANKARA (Śiva)**, 51. <note>A name for Shiva meaning "The Giver of Happiness" or "The Auspicious One."</note>
**Shiyāli**, 10, 11. <note>modern: Sirkazhi. The birthplace of the saint Sambandar.</note>
**Sin**, sense of, 43-47, 97, 99, 101.
**Śiva**:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Meaning of name, 3. <note>"Auspicious."</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Conception of, 4 f.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Blue-throated, 8. <note>Refers to the myth where Shiva drank the halahala poison to save the world, staining his throat blue.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Delivering from death, 15, 79. <note>Refers to Shiva rescuing his devotee Markandeya from Yama, the god of death.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Including good and evil, 19, 103.

**Unreality of life**, 51, 83. <note>The concept of *Maya*, or the transitory and deceptive nature of the material world.</note>

**VĀDĀPI**, 29. <note>The capital of the Chalukya dynasty, famously captured by the Pallavas.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Vāk**, 59. <note>Sanskrit for "Speech" or "Voice," often personified as a goddess.</note>
**Valivalam**, 21.
**Vāsuki**, 8. <note>The king of serpents who was used as a rope to churn the ocean of milk.</note>
**Vedāraṇiyam**, 63. <note>A coastal town where the Vedas are said to have worshipped Shiva.</note>
**Vedas**, 55.
**Vedāngas**, 55. <note>Six auxiliary disciplines for understanding and performing Vedic rituals.</note>
**Venneynallūr**, 75. <note>modern: Tiruvennainallur. The site where Shiva appeared to claim the saint Sundarar as his slave.</note>
**Vīṇa**, 49, 115. <note>An ancient Indian stringed instrument often associated with Shiva and Sarasvati.</note>
**Vishṇu**, 4, 41, 83. <note>The Preserver god in the Hindu trinity, often appearing in Saivite hymns as a devotee of Shiva.</note>
